- AD only when validated and the client asked;
- bogus answers as SERVFAIL with EDE 6;
- negative trust anchors;
- `hickory-proto`'s verification primitives behind a `Validator`.

In hickory 0.26 the chain-of-trust logic (`DnssecDnsHandle`, with NSEC/NSEC3 denial proofs, wildcard checks, NSEC3 iteration limits and a validation cache) lives in `hickory-net`. The primitives in `hickory-proto` alone would mean writing that ourselves.

**Decision:**
- **Use `hickory-net`'s `DnssecDnsHandle`** (`dnssec-ring`, `tokio`; it adds no TLS or HTTP stacks). It wraps a `DnsHandle` we implement over one of our upstream groups.
  - The question, and every DNSKEY and DS lookup the proof needs, go to the same upstreams with DO=1 and CD=1 (we check, so the upstream mustn't drop what it thinks is bogus).
  - There's one handle per group, so its validation cache lasts across queries.
  - It's wrapped as `telltale_upstream::dnssec::Validator`.
- **Verdict:** the weakest proof among the answer records, or among the authority records for NXDOMAIN and NODATA. Errors from the validator other than upstream failures count as bogus.
- **Serving:**
  - Secure gets AD for clients that asked; insecure and indeterminate get no AD.
  - Bogus becomes SERVFAIL with EDE 6, never a stale answer, and is never cached; in permissive mode it's served and counted.
  - RRSIG, NSEC and NSEC3 records are stripped for clients without DO.
  - Validation runs on cache misses only, and validated answers are cached with their AD bit. The cache key already separates DO and CD.
- **Negative trust anchors:** `[dnssec] negative_trust_anchors` plus every route with `dnssec_nta`.
- **Mode names and default:** `off`, `validate`, `permissive` (spec's `validate_permissive`). The default stays `off` until it has run on the owner's nodes for a while; then a follow-up makes `validate` the default.
- **Timing:** a cold chain (root, TLD and zone keys) takes several sequential lookups. Validation gets three times the query budget, so a slow first lookup still lands in the cache even if that client gave up.
- **Found on the way (all queries):** a truncated UDP answer's TCP retry shared the adaptive per-attempt timeout calibrated on small answers (about 3× average round trip), so large answers (DNSKEY sets with signatures, long TXT) timed out. The TCP retry now gets the configured timeout of its own.

**Verification:** unit tests for verdicts, NTA coverage and stripping. `deploy/dnssec-e2e.sh` (CI, real DNS tree, forwarding to 1.1.1.1 and 9.9.9.10) checks:
- ietf.org and cloudflare.com are secure with AD;
- google.com is insecure without AD;
- dnssec-failed.org is SERVFAIL with EDE 6, and answered with CD;
- signed NXDOMAIN and NODATA validate;
- verdicts are counted.

**Deferred:**
- RFC 5011 trust-anchor updates (the built-in root keys cover KSK-2017 and KSK-2024);
- aggressive NSEC caching (RFC 8198, with recursion);
- per-reason EDE codes (7, 9, 10, 12) instead of 6;
- the `dnssec` field in query events and the Settings UI.

## ADR-061 — Pi-hole import v1: a reviewed starting configuration, faithful group semantics (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.3 (API-007; `spec/08` §8) asks for an importer of Pi-hole v5 and v6 Teleporter archives covering adlists, domain lists, groups, clients, local DNS/CNAME records, upstreams, and conditional forwarding, with a report of every unmapped setting. The spec doesn't say whether the result is applied or reviewed, how Pi-hole's per-entry group assignments map onto our per-group list sets, or what happens to Pi-hole behaviors we don't have.

**Decision:**
- **Output, not apply:** `telltale import pihole PATH` writes TOML for review (like `import zone`, ADR-041), validated to load before it's written. Applying through the API (and the UI's import page) waits for T6.7's restore path.
- **Inputs:** v6 zip (`pihole.toml` and a `gravity.db` of the group tables), v5 tar.gz (JSON per table plus `setupVars.conf`, `pihole-FTL.conf`, `custom.list`, `dnsmasq.d`), a directory, or a bare `gravity.db`. Readers are ours: zip and tar.gz in about 200 lines on `miniz_oxide`, with CRC checks and size limits. SQLite goes through `rusqlite`, already in the tree. Archived databases are read from a private temporary copy.
- **Groups stay exact:** each group gets exactly the lists Pi-hole gave it, so `default` doesn't fall back to "every list". Domain entries become inline lists, one per (type, set of groups). Disabled groups get no lists. Clients in no group go to a `pihole-no-group` group with no lists, as Pi-hole blocks nothing for them.
- **Matching:**
  - exact entries use `match = "exact"`;
  - a regex our parser would read as a plain name is wrapped as `/regex/`;
  - entries our engine can't run are reported, not dropped silently.
- **Upstreams:** they go in the `default` upstream group with strategy `fastest`, matching Pi-hole's preference for the fastest server.
- **Conditional forwarding:** each rev server becomes its own upstream group and route. The route covers the domain and the network's reverse zones: non-octet prefixes are expanded, so a /23 is two /24 zones, never widened. It gets a negative trust anchor.
- **Devices:** DHCP reservations become named devices (MAC and IP join a matching client, or a new one named by the host name). The DHCP server is not imported.
- **Report:**
  - v6: every setting Pi-hole marks `### CHANGED` that isn't mapped;
  - v5: every unmapped key in `setupVars.conf` and `pihole-FTL.conf`, except the old install's own plumbing (interface, addresses, web server);
  - names only, never values, since they include password hashes.
- **Not imported:** clients matched by host name or interface (we match by address); the `IP` blocking modes; query history; passwords and tokens.

**Verification:**
- Unit tests on hand-written inputs in both formats.
- `deploy/pihole-import-e2e.sh` (CI) configures the official `pihole/pihole` images (v6 2026.09.0 = FTL v6.7.1, v5 2024.07.0) through their own tools, exports Teleporter archives, imports them, and serves the result: local names, a CNAME's TTL, exact and regex denies (`;querytype`), the blocking mode, and group scoping.
- Pi-hole-generated files aren't committed (their comments are Pi-hole's text, NFR-006).

**Consequences:**
- Merging into an existing configuration is manual (both define `default`).
- The importer's e2e showed that the first blocking snapshot waited for every list's first fetch, including retries, so an unreachable adlist delayed all blocking at first start. Fixed: the fetcher signals the compiler at most 10 s (`settle`) after the first update in a round, so lists that arrive together still compile once.

## ADR-062 — Technitium import v1: read the API, not the backup (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.4 (API-007, P1). `spec/08` §8 says "backup zip → blocked/allowed zones, block-list URLs, forwarders + protocols, Advanced Blocking app config → groups". Technitium's backup holds `dns.config`, zone files, and the allowed/blocked lists in its own versioned binary serialization. That format is undocumented and changes between releases. Its HTTP API returns the same data as documented JSON.

**Decision:**
- **Source:** `telltale import technitium URL` reads a running server through the API with an API token, which comes from `TECHNITIUM_TOKEN` or `--token-file`, never argv.
- **Calls, all read-only:**
  - `settings/get`;
  - `zones/list`, then `zones/records/get` per zone;
  - `allowed/export`, `blocked/export`;
  - `apps/list`, and `apps/config/get` for Advanced Blocking;
  - `dhcp/scopes/list` and `dhcp/scopes/get` (optional).
- **Output:** reviewed TOML like ADR-061, validated before it's written.
- **Mapping:**
  - **Forwarders** go in the `default` group: concurrent forwarding becomes `parallel` with the same fan-out, but only with two or more usable forwarders. `name (ip:port)` pins the address, with the name as TLS name.
  - **Forwarder zones:** forwarders by priority, a route, and an NTA unless every forwarder of the zone validated.
  - **Primary zones:** records of the types we serve, with their TTLs.
  - **Blocked and allowed zones** become subtree inline lists.
  - **Advanced Blocking groups** become groups. The catch-all network's group becomes `default`, and other networks become `networks` (ADR-050). URL lists are shared across groups by URL. Regexes are wrapped as `/re/`. Server-wide lists are added to every group, since Technitium applies both. Disabled groups get no lists. The bypass list becomes a group with no lists.
  - **Blocking answer:** NXDOMAIN, or custom addresses, become the group's `block_mode`.
  - **Rate limit:** the per-/32 limit (queries per minute) becomes `[ratelimit]`.
  - **Other settings:** `dnssecValidation` and `saveCache` carry over. DHCP reservations become named devices.
- **Not imported (reported):** secondary and stub zones; QUIC forwarders; forwarders with no address; listener- and host-based group selection; per-list answers; the DHCP server; encrypted listeners; recursion ACLs, TSIG, zone transfers, the proxy, and other apps. Listeners, logging, users, and tokens are not imported either.

**Verification:**
- Unit tests on hand-written API responses.
- `deploy/technitium-import-e2e.sh` (CI) runs against `technitium/dns-server:15.6.0`. It configures everything above through Technitium's API, installs Advanced Blocking from Technitium's store, and creates an API token. It then imports, serves the result, and checks the answers.

**Consequences:**
- Importing needs the old server running, which matches the migration path (import, compare, switch).
- A backup-zip reader can follow if the format gets documented.
- The e2e found a real-data bug: concurrent forwarding with a single usable forwarder produced an invalid `parallel` group. Fixed and tested.

## ADR-063 — Backups v1: a checked archive of config and local data, without the cluster key (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.7 (API-007, P0) asks for Teleporter parity: one archive with config and local data, optionally with query history. `spec/08` §8 sketches `telltale ctl backup create [--include-qlog]` writing a `.ttbk` (tar.zst + manifest + signature) restored onto a new primary. `ctl` (the API-wrapping CLI, API-008) doesn't exist yet. The spec doesn't say what the signature is signed with or whether cluster identity is included.

**Decision:**
- **Commands:** `telltale backup create | show | restore`, local commands that work on the data directory.
  - `create` is safe while the server runs: SQLite databases are copied consistently with `VACUUM INTO`, and query-log segments only grow, so the size seen at open is copied.
- **Contents:**
  - config files under `config/`;
  - `state.db` (users, tokens, UI-made entries, audit), with sessions and idempotency keys removed;
  - `rollups.db` and `anomaly.json`;
  - the query log (`qlog/`, `qlog-nodes/`) only with `--include-qlog`;
  - lists, snapshots, the cache, and `setup-token` are left out, since they're rebuilt or transient.
- **Format:** a tar stream compressed with zstd (frame checksum on), with `manifest.json` last. It holds the format version, source node and paths, and each entry's size and BLAKE3 hash. The file is written owner-only and renamed into place when complete.
- **No signature in v1:** a signature by the source node's key proves nothing to the fresh node it's restored onto, which doesn't know that key. Integrity comes from the manifest hashes and zstd's checksum. Confidentiality is the file's permissions, as with Pi-hole's Teleporter. Encryption with a passphrase is the natural next step.
- **No cluster identity by default:** `cluster/`, with the CA key, isn't included. A portable file with the CA key could impersonate the cluster. A restored member starts standalone, and the manifest records that it was a member so restore can say what to do.
- **Restore:**
  - streams into a staging directory under the data directory;
  - checks every entry against the manifest, and anything unexpected, a path outside `config/` or `data/`, or `..`, fails before any file is moved;
  - refuses to replace existing files without `--force`;
  - chowns restored files to the data directory's owner.

**Verification:**
- Unit tests: contents, permissions, round trip, session stripping, `--force`, corruption, path containment, and the query log.
- `deploy/backup-e2e.sh` (CI) backs up a running node with an admin and an API-made name, restores it onto a second node, and checks the following on that node:
  - the name answers;
  - the admin signs in, and the old session doesn't carry over;
  - the audit log is present;
  - damaged archives are refused.

**Consequences:**
- **Download:** `GET /api/v1/backup` (admin, audited `backup.create`, without the query log) and a Settings → System button. Restore through the API is deferred: restoring under a running server is risky.
- `--include-cluster-key` (for disaster recovery of a lone primary) and passphrase encryption are follow-ups.

## ADR-064 — Agent principals v1: scoped tokens, deny by default, a reason for every change (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.5 (AGT-001..005, AGT-009). `spec/13` §2 asks for:
- agent tokens with an owner, scopes, an optional group restriction, rate limits, and expiry;
- attribution `agent:<token> (owner: <user>)` with the client and a required reason;
- dry runs with impact estimates on every mutation;
- guardrails and a cluster-wide kill switch;
- an OpenAPI document written for agents and linted.

Tokens so far had a role-like `scope` (read/write/admin), and the audit log already recorded `token:<name> (owner: <user>)` (ADR-038).

**Decision:**
- **Agent tokens:** `POST /tokens` with `kind: "agent"`.
  - The scopes are AGT-004's list: `analytics:read`, `querylog:read`, `config:read`, `config:write:{clients,records,forwards}` (`*` for all), `ops:pause`, `ops:cache`, `cluster:admin`.
  - The default is `analytics:read` + `config:read`: read-only, without the query log.
  - Optional `group` and `ratePerMinute`.
  - Each scope needs a role from the creator (`config:write:*` and `ops:*` need operator, `cluster:admin` needs admin). A token's effective role is the lower of its owner's and its scopes'.
  - Stored in three new `tokens` columns (migration 5). User tokens are unchanged.
- **Deny by default:** one table maps method and path to a scope. Anything not in it is refused for agents: users, tokens, passwords, backups, the audit log, and routes added later until they're mapped. The role checks still apply on top.
- **Group restriction:** honored where a group view exists.
  - The query log and top lists: the middleware replaces the `group` parameter.
  - Devices: listed for the group only; writes only into, and only on devices already in, the group.
  - Everything else is refused for restricted tokens rather than shown unfiltered.
- **Every change needs a reason:** agents' non-GET requests without `X-Telltale-Reason` get 400.
- **Attribution:** the actor is `agent:<token> (owner: <user>) via <X-Telltale-Client>`, with kind `agent`.
- **Rate limits:** a per-token token bucket in memory, refilled per minute, default 120 (`[agents] rate_per_minute`). Over the limit: 429 with `Retry-After`. Per node, so a cluster allows N× in total; documented.
- **Kill switch:** `[agents] enabled`, a shared section, so it replicates to every node (and, since ADR-059's addendum, doesn't trouble N−1 replicas while unset). It's applied on start and every reload.
- **Impact estimates:** device, name, and forward changes return `recentQueries` and `impact`, a sentence with the numbers. They're counted from the in-memory top lists for the current and previous hour (a lower bound, cheap, no log scan). Promotion gets `?dryRun=true`, returning a `PromotePlan` (epoch, emergency, impact) from the same checks without acting.
- **Lint:** `.spectral.yaml` (spectral:oas plus operation summary, description, tags, operationId, success response, and parameter descriptions) runs in CI. Every response now has a description.

**Verification:**
- Unit tests:
  - the scope map denies by default;
  - scope parsing and the roles scopes imply;
  - the kill switch, rate limit, required reason, and group refusal;
  - the forced group parameter.
- API tests: agent tokens over HTTP (scopes, refusals, reason, attribution, kill switch); group-restricted tokens; the promote dry run.
- `deploy/agent-e2e.sh` (CI) on a running node:
  - a dry run of every mutation reports an impact and changes nothing;
  - a real change is attributed with its reason;
  - a reload with `enabled = false` stops the agent and not the admin.

**Consequences:**
- MCP (T6.6) builds on these principals.
- `ops:pause` and `ops:cache` exist ahead of their endpoints.
- Per-group analytics views beyond top lists would let restricted agents see more.
- OAuth for agents (AGT-008) stays P1.

**Amended 2026-10-08 (review 06-02, 06-09; amendment Proposed):** an agent's pre-save list check returns how many lines couldn't be used and why, not their text (the node would otherwise read any URL it can reach for the agent). Viewers list only their own plans; operators and admins see all.

## ADR-065 — MCP server v1: read-only tools over the REST routes, one implementation for two transports (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.6 (AGT-006, AGT-008 bearer part, AGT-009; ADR-010). `spec/13` §3.1 lists the read-only tools, and §4 suggests a `telltale-mcp` crate with schemas generated from the Rust types (`schemars`) and a catalog snapshot test. The AC asks for:
- an MCP conformance test;
- scope and privacy authorization tests;
- a scripted agent completing the "why is the TV slow" story.

**Decision:**
- **Where:** a module of `telltale-api` (`mcp.rs`), mounted at `/mcp` on every node's API listener behind the same authentication. A separate crate would add nothing at this size. `POST /mcp` takes JSON-RPC 2.0 (single or batch) and answers JSON (Streamable HTTP without SSE; `GET /mcp` is 405). Protocol `2025-06-18`, also answering `2025-03-26` and `2024-11-05`. Methods: `initialize`, `ping`, `tools/list`, `tools/call`. Notifications get 202.
- **Tools call REST in-process:** each tool builds one or more `GET /api/v1/...` requests and sends them through the REST router with the caller's own `Authorization`/cookie. Scopes, group restriction, rate limits (one per inner call), privacy levels, and the kill switch apply exactly as for REST, with no second authorization model. The REST problem+json becomes the tool's error text.
- **Catalog (read-only):** `get_overview`, `top_items`, `search_queries`, `explain_decision`, `get_client_profile` (the device, then its queries by IP), `latency_breakdown`, `upstream_health`, `list_effectiveness`, `find_anomalies`, `cluster_status`, `get_config` (one section).
  - Each description starts "Read-only.", and each tool carries `readOnlyHint`.
  - `test_resolution` is deferred: it needs resolution without cache or log, which the pipeline doesn't offer yet.
- **Schemas:** hand-written JSON Schemas, frozen in the committed `docs/api/mcp-tools.json` and diffed by a test (`UPDATE_MCP=1` to regenerate). Generating them from the REST parameter types would tie tool arguments to query-string shapes; the snapshot still prevents drift.
- **Guardrails:** results are capped at 32 KiB (with a note) and 200 rows. Each `initialize` starts a session (`Mcp-Session-Id`), and the client name/version from it is passed on as `X-Telltale-Client`, so agent actions are attributed to the MCP client (AGT-005).
- **stdio:** `telltale mcp --stdio` relays newline-delimited JSON-RPC between stdin/stdout and a node's `/mcp` with a token, so there's one implementation of the tools. The node address comes from `--url` or the config files; nothing but protocol goes to stdout.
- **Agents and `/mcp`:** `POST /mcp` needs no scope of its own and no reason header (the tools are read-only; the inner calls are checked). Group-restricted tokens may use it, since their inner calls are narrowed.

**Verification:**
- Unit tests: the catalog snapshot; side-effect statements and caps; arguments become queries.
- An API test: initialize with a session, list, call, scope error inside a tool, unknown method, no credentials.
- `deploy/mcp-e2e.sh` (CI) runs the official `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` client against a live node. Over Streamable HTTP, the listed tools must match the committed catalog. The test then plays the "why is the TV slow?" story (device profile with recent queries, upstream health, explain on a blocked name), repeats it over `telltale mcp --stdio`, and checks that a token without `querylog:read` gets an error from `search_queries`.

**Consequences:**
- Write tools with plan/apply and approvals (§3.2) are P1.
- OAuth for MCP clients (AGT-008) is P1.
- MCP resources and prompts are later.
- An SSE stream (`GET /mcp`) would be needed only for server-initiated messages.

**Amended 2026-10-08 (review 06-04, 06-07; amendment Proposed):** the REST calls behind a tool carry the `/mcp` request's client address (`OnBehalfOf`, an extension only in-process requests can carry), so changes made through MCP are audited from the agent's address, not `0.0.0.0`. They don't take from the agent's rate-limit bucket again: the rate counts tool calls, and each message of a JSON-RPC batch, which holds at most 16 messages. A resource cut at 32 KiB says so.

## ADR-066 — CA rotation: two trusted CAs, three member-gated phases (Accepted)
**Context:** T5.4c (CLU-001, CLU-005). `spec/12` and the roadmap ask for:
- CA rotation: a new key, a cross-signed transition, node certificate renewal over the channel, and the old CA retired;
- re-sharing the key with eligible nodes.

ADR-057 already renews node certificates (same key) over the channel. A CA rotation changes the trust anchor every TLS handshake and every manifest signature depends on.

**Decision:**
- **Two trusted CAs instead of cross-signing:** during a rotation `ca.crt` is a bundle of both CAs. TLS trusts every certificate in it, so certificates from either CA verify. Manifest signatures and issued certificates are checked against any CA in the bundle. The bundle's first CA is the one that signs, and `ca.key` is always its key. Cross-signing adds nothing in a cluster whose members all receive the bundle.
- **Distribution:**
  - The primary's signed manifest carries `ca_bundle`. A node adopts a new bundle only if it keeps at least one CA the node already trusts, so trust moves step by step and a stolen CA alone can't replace it.
  - Witnesses follow manifests for this alone.
  - Eligible nodes get the next CA's key with the existing key share, now re-sent whenever it changes. A key that arrives before its CA is trusted is held (`ca-pending.key`) until a verified bundle includes it.
- **Phases, driven by the primary, each gated on every non-ephemeral registry member** (heartbeats now carry `trust_fp`, the CAs a node trusts, and `issuer_fp`, the CA of its certificate):
  1. `trust`: the bundle is old + new; the old CA signs.
  2. `switch`: once all trust both, the new key becomes `ca.key` (the old is kept as `ca-old.key`) and the bundle becomes new + old. Nodes see that their certificate isn't from the signing CA and renew within a minute (self-issued with the new key, or over `cert.renew`). A node whose `ca.key` doesn't match the signing CA asks the primary rather than sign with the old key.
  3. `finish`: once every certificate is from the new CA, the bundle is the new CA alone, and the old key and the rotation state are deleted.
- **Operator interface:** `telltale cluster rotate-ca` starts a rotation on the primary while it runs, and the running primary carries it out (it re-reads its identity every cycle). `--status` and a `ca_rotation` Cluster check show the phase and pending members. Each new CA's name carries a label, so the two trusted CAs never share a subject.
- **Fixed on the way:**
  - Configuration fetches used the TLS settings from start-up, so they'd fail after a rotation. They'd also present an old certificate after a renewal. They now read the current identity.
  - Witnesses never renewed their certificates (ADR-057 gap); they do now.
  - Cluster files were made owner-only only after being written, and two streams could collide on one temporary name. Each write now uses its own temporary name, and secrets are created owner-only.

**Verification:**
- Unit tests:
  - bundles: verify, issuer, order-independent fingerprint;
  - start: refuses a second rotation;
  - adoption: refuses unrelated trust, promotes a held key, switches, then retires.
- `deploy/cluster/rotate-e2e.sh` (CI) with a primary, an eligible replica, and a witness under DNS load:
  - all three phases complete, and every node ends trusting only the new CA, with a certificate from it;
  - the replica holds the new key, and no old, next, or pending key is left;
  - every query was answered during the rotation;
  - a configuration change replicates afterwards (signed with the new key), and a restarted replica reconnects and syncs.

**Consequences:**
- A member offline for the whole rotation must rejoin.
- Join tokens pin the CA fingerprint, so tokens from before a rotation stop working.
- A forced rotation (with members down) is a possible follow-up.

**Corrected 2026-10-08 (review 05-10):** "a stolen CA alone can't replace it" overstates the rule. The rule stops a CA the node doesn't trust from replacing trust; it doesn't contain a stolen *current* CA key, whose holder can rotate trust to a CA of their own in three manifests, exactly as the legitimate primary does. That is inherent (the key signs everything): a suspected compromise of the cluster key means re-creating the cluster, as `docs/running.md` now says.

## ADR-067 — Quick rules: their own layer, the most specific scope wins, expiry on the server (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.12 (owner request 2026-10-05) wants everyday per-person rules:
- unblock a game's server for one phone for two hours;
- block a site for the kids' devices until tomorrow.

The engine can already express device-scoped rules (`$client` in the manual-rules overlay, ADR-003), but the overlay uses the lists' four tiers (important allow > important block > allow > block). Those can't say "this device's rule beats its group's rule beats the lists": a group-wide allow would override a parent's block for one child. Nothing expires on its own.

**Decision:**
- **A quick rule:**
  - allow or block a domain and its subdomains;
  - for listed devices (by name, IP, or MAC, as `[[client]]` keys), listed groups, or everyone; *(Amended by ADR-183, Proposed: `devices` takes a `[[client]]` name (any case), an IP or a CIDR, as the code always has. A MAC is given through a named device (`[[client]] match = ["aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff"]`), and a rule for that device then follows it wherever the neighbor table or a router sees that MAC.)*
  - an optional expiry (an absolute time; the UI offers durations);
  - a note, who made it (user or agent), and when.
- **Storage:** in `state.db` as a managed entry of kind `rule` (ADR-040). In a cluster, rules travel with the shared configuration the primary publishes (ADR-047), and a replica's UI forwards the write to the primary (ADR-054). Rules are capped at 1,000 per cluster.
- **Matching:** quick rules are their own layer, checked before the lists:
  1. Among rules matching the query name (any suffix of it) whose scope includes the client, a device rule beats a group rule, which beats an everyone rule.
  2. Within one scope, the longer (more specific) domain wins, then allow beats block.
  3. A match decides allow or block outright. Otherwise the lists decide as before.

  CNAME inspection (FLT-007) uses the same decision, so a block also catches a CNAME to the blocked name.
- **Hot path:** the rules sit in a read-mostly table behind `ArcSwap`, keyed by the hash of each rule's reversed domain. A lookup probes the query's suffixes, at most one probe per label, with no allocation. With no rules it costs one branch.
- **Expiry:** each node ignores a rule from the moment its expiry passes, by its own clock (T6.11 shows clock offsets). A sweep every 5 s on the primary deletes expired rules and audit-logs each expiry. A replica never needs the sweep to stop applying a rule.
- **Explanations:** an answer decided by a quick rule is attributed to it ("allowed by a quick rule for Mom's phone, expires 21:30"). The query log records it, and "Why?" and explain show it above the list matches.
- **Agents:** the scope `config:write:rules`, with the usual reason header, dry run, and impact estimate (recent queries the rule would change).

**Consequences:**
- A new rule takes effect on the next query, with no list recompile.
- Cost: about 52 ns per query with 1,000 rules, about 4 ns with none (xxh3 suffix keys, measured 2026-10-05).
- Rules are deliberately simpler than list syntax: no regex, no query types. Anything fancier belongs in a list.
- Device scoping is only as good as recognising the device; the docs say so.
- Expiry depends on node clocks being roughly right. A node whose clock is off keeps or drops a rule early or late by that offset, and the Cluster page flags offsets of 2 s or more.
- **Mixed versions (CLU-010):** the config schema rejects unknown keys, and an empty `rule` list is left out of the shared configuration. So an N−1 replica follows the cluster as long as nobody has made a quick rule. Once one exists, the replica refuses that version and keeps serving its last good one until it's upgraded. Quick rules therefore wait for a cluster-wide upgrade, as any new shared section does.

## ADR-068 — One process per data directory: an exclusive lock held for the process's life (Accepted)
**Context:** T6.14 (owner request 2026-10-05). Scaling a single-volume Deployment (or starting a second `telltale run` with the same configuration) makes two processes share one data directory: the node identity and cluster certificates, `state.db`, the query-log segments, and the cache dump. Nothing stopped it, and the damage is silent: two nodes with one identity, interleaved query-log writes, and SQLite contention.

**Decision:**
- `telltale run` takes an exclusive advisory lock (`flock`, through `std::fs::File::try_lock`) on `<data_dir>/telltale.lock` before anything else touches the directory, and holds it until the process exits.
- If another process holds it, the new one retries for 10 s (a previous process may still be shutting down), then exits non-zero with an error. The error names the directory and the holder (pid, host or pod name, and start time, which the holder writes into the file), and points to `mode: scaled`.
- If the lock file can't be opened at all (a read-only or unusual file system), the node logs a warning and starts anyway: the lock protects against a mistake and must never stop DNS (CLU-004).
- `telltale backup restore` takes the same lock without waiting, so it refuses to restore under a running server.
- Read-only commands (`qlog search`, `explain`, `backup create`) don't take it.

**Consequences:**
- The operating system releases the lock when the process exits, even after a crash or `SIGKILL`, so there is no stale-lock cleanup.
- A rolling update of a single-volume Deployment would wait for the old pod forever. The chart uses a StatefulSet and the homelab Deployment uses `Recreate`, and the docs tell people with their own manifests to do the same.
- On network file systems `flock` may be emulated or local-only. That is acceptable for a guard against mistakes.

## ADR-069 — Editing upstreams, lists, and groups from the UI: overrides in state.db, the files untouched (Accepted)
**Context:** T7.5 (owner report 2026-10-06). On a node installed with `install.sh`, the upstreams and lists come in the starter `/etc/telltale/telltale.toml`. ADR-040 makes anything the files define read-only through the API ("files win"), so none of them could be changed in the UI. Rewriting the file isn't an option either: Docker and Kubernetes mount it read-only, and on Git-managed nodes the next deploy would replace it.

**Decision:**
- Upstreams, upstream groups, lists, and client groups made or changed through the API are managed entries in `state.db` (like ADR-040's), by name. An entry can:
  - add a new name;
  - **override** a file entry of the same name (the whole entry is replaced);
  - **hide** a file entry (a tombstone: the name is left out).
- The files are never rewritten. The UI marks each entry as *file*, *UI*, or *UI override of the file*, and "Revert to the file" deletes the override or tombstone.
- Every change is validated on the merged configuration before it's stored (a group can't lose its last upstream; a list can't be removed while a group uses it; the default upstream group must keep a member), with dry run, `If-Match`, idempotency, and audit as for the other managed kinds.
- In a cluster, the merged configuration is what the primary publishes (ADR-047), so changes reach every node.
- On a node whose configuration comes from Git (`config_source = "gitops"`), each change response carries the TOML to add to the repository or the Helm values to make it permanent ("Keep it in Git"), because an override lives only in that node's `state.db`.
- Devices, local names, forwarded domains, and quick rules keep ADR-040's rule (files win), since the starter config doesn't define them.

**Consequences:**
- A standalone node can be run entirely from the UI after `install.sh`.
- Two sources of truth for the same name are possible; the UI and the `GET` endpoints show which one is in effect (`source`: `file`, `api`, or `override`).
- A file change to an overridden entry has no effect until the override is reverted; the UI says so on the entry.
**Amended by ADR-180:** a list's local file (`path`) made through the API is set or changed only by an administrator, and plugin upstreams aren't made through the API.

## ADR-070 — Agents' plans: a dry run kept in memory on the node that made it, applied with If-Match (Accepted)
**Context:** T7.1 (AGT-007). A `plan_*` MCP tool must show what a change would do and let the agent apply it later, only if nothing changed meanwhile and, with `[agents] require_approval`, only after an operator approves. The spec doesn't say where plans live, how long they last beyond "10 minutes", or how a cluster shares them.

**Decision:**
- A plan is the REST write (method, path, body) plus its dry-run answer, the config version it saw, the reason, and who asked (the caller's token or session). Plan tools call the REST dry run in-process with the agent's own credentials, so scopes, group restrictions, rate limits, and the kill switch apply unchanged.
- `apply_plan` replays the same write with `If-Match: <version>` and the plan ID as the `Idempotency-Key`. A 412 marks the plan `stale`. Only the caller that made a plan can apply or discard it.
- Plans live in the memory of the node that made them, at most 500, open for 10 minutes and listed for a day. They aren't stored or replicated: a restart drops them, and the agent plans again.
- With `[agents] require_approval = true` a new plan is `pending` until a person with the operator role approves or rejects it (`POST /api/v1/plans/{id}/approve|reject`, the "Agent changes" page). Agent tokens can never approve or reject. Both decisions are audited.
- Approval is checked when the plan is made: turning the setting on doesn't hold plans already made, and turning it off doesn't release pending ones.
- Immediate operations (`flush_cache`, `pause_blocking`, `resume_blocking`) have no plan, as `spec/13` §3.2 says; they're audited like any agent write.
- `plan_set_schedule` waits for schedules (FLT-010). Alert destinations for pending plans wait for OBS-010.

**Consequences:**
- Nothing new is persisted, and a plan can't outlive the configuration it was checked against.
- In a cluster, an operator approves on the node the agent used. Agents should use the node whose UI people use (normally the primary). If agents and people use different nodes, plans would need to move to the replicated `state.db`.

## ADR-173 — A replica forgets a peer it keeps no link to once that peer is silent (Proposed)
**Context:** A node's member list (`telltale_cluster_peers`, `telltale_cluster_peer_up`, the chart's `TelltaleDNSClusterPeerDown`) is every peer it has ever had a stream with since it started; a stream that ends only marks the peer disconnected. On the primary that is right: every node is meant to dial it. On a replica it isn't. A replica's own stream goes to the primary, and to another eligible node only as a fallback while the primary doesn't answer (ADR-051, GitHub issue #4), until it moves back (issue #43). The streams under automatic failover (ADR-056) are the only other standing links. Seen on a home cluster (2026-11-18, 0.8.0-edge.393): two native replicas linked to each other for about a minute while the controller restarted for an upgrade, moved back to it, and then each listed the other as down until its next restart, so the alert fired on both replicas for days (across several builds, each restart opening a new fallback window) while the controller saw both up.
**Decision:**
- A node forgets a member that is not connected, has no stream open to it, and hasn't been heard from within the up window (three heartbeats, 15 s), unless it keeps that peer: the primary keeps every peer; a replica keeps the primary and, while automatic failover is active, every voter in the registry.
- "The primary" is a member whose last Hello said it was primary, or the node the last version this replica applied names (`applied.json`). A Hello is sent once, when a stream opens, so a node promoted on a stream that was already open still says "replica" there; the version it publishes next names it, and the replica applies that one. (Taking the highest epoch instead was rejected: every voter absorbs the newest epoch, so several members tie on it.)
- The sweep runs on every node with the voter mesh's 3-second loop (`mesh`), in any failover mode. A peer forgotten this way comes back with its next Hello, like any new stream, and is logged at `info`.
- The sweep fails closed. Who the voters are comes from the registry file, and a read that fails leaves a registry with this node alone, which reads as "automatic failover isn't active". With automatic failover set and no other node in the registry, the sweep does nothing for that tick. (Skipping it whenever fewer than three voters are found was rejected: a replica that isn't a voter, or a cluster set to automatic with two voters, would then keep every passing stream for good.)
- A stream its dialer ends from outside (the `switch` a replica uses to leave a peer for the primary) now marks the peer disconnected and logs `disconnected`, like a stream that closes. It used to cancel that clean-up, so the replica that moved back kept the peer as connected (and, once silent, down) for good, and this sweep would never have dropped it. When two replicas had a stream each way, as in the incident, the stream the other side ended already cleared the flag, so this matters for a stream in one direction only.
- Nothing else changes: the registry, the primary's member list, and the events log are untouched.
- Requirements: CLU-001 (the member list), CLU-005 (voters), CLU-008 (an alert fires when a node disappears).
**Consequences:**
- After a passing replica-to-replica stream, each replica reports the other as down for at most about 18 seconds, below the alert's `for: 1m`. A replica that is really down is still reported by the primary, which is where the alert fires for it. That depends on the primary being among the Prometheus scrape targets.
- A replica's own member list (its metrics and the peers in `/api/v1/system/info`) no longer shows another replica it had a passing stream with. Nor do the lists of nodes that didn't answer in a federated read, a simulation or a support package built on a replica (`missingNodes`, "not connected"): they are built from the member list, so a forgotten replica is not named in them, where before it was listed as not connected. The primary's lists are unchanged.
- The sweep changes the member list and the Prometheus alert, not the in-app view. The Cluster page, `/api/v1/health` and the in-app `NodeDown` alert also list the registry's nodes that have no entry in the member list, as down with no link (the `last seen` counted from when the node joined, and only once this node has been up for 90 seconds). On a replica, a forgotten replica becomes exactly that row. It is the row a replica that never had a stream to a sibling already shows, so the sweep doesn't add a false alarm there; it also doesn't remove the one that was there. Whether those views should apply the same "expected links" rule is left to the owner.
- Forgetting a replica lifts the exclusion of its URL from `primary_urls()` (the list of where the primary may be, which leaves out the URLs of nodes known to be replicas) until its next Hello. A node whose joining URL belongs to an old primary that is a replica now, and was forgotten, may try that URL once more: one needless move, healed by the next Hello.
- A primary that was rebuilt under a new node ID stays in a replica's list as down until that replica restarts: its last Hello said it was the primary, and the sweep keeps the primary. That was so before the sweep too (a replica then forgot no peer at all), and the user guide says so (`docs/running.md`, "Removing a node").

## ADR-174 — Federated reads never wait on a runtime worker: a sync-to-async bridge has an OS-level deadline (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #7: nodes whose DNS, API, metrics and cluster ports stop answering while systemd says the service is active. The reporter, nbacheller, traced a mechanism in the code and proposed a fix, and this decision builds on that work. Async API handlers called blocking `Backend` methods straight on a runtime worker, and on a replica a federated read (the configuration version for an `ETag`, the config entries, the devices with their identities, and the reads behind the stats) waited for the cluster channel with a scoped thread that ran `Handle::block_on` and was joined: no bound. That wait needs the runtime as well: the stream's writer and reader tasks run on a worker, and the RPC's own timer is advanced only by one. With every worker (as many as CPUs: two on the reporter's container) parked in such a wait, nothing can send the RPC, read its answer or fire its timeout, so nothing ends the wait. The API, the metrics port, the cluster stream, the accept loops, TCP, DoT, DoH and DoQ, and every cache miss (all on that runtime) stop, and the process doesn't act on SIGTERM; UDP answers from the cache go on. With `TOKIO_WORKER_THREADS=1` a single request to `GET /api/v1/config/entries` on a replica shows it. The pattern is as old as the federated reads (v0.1.0). It is not proven that this caused the reporter's incident (DNS stopped some seconds before the node left the cluster), so the issue stays open; the watchdog's dump (ADR-163) is how a hang we have not found is read.
**Decision:**
- The handlers whose backend calls can federate run them in `blocking()` (the blocking pool), as about forty others already did: the device list and the `ETag` reads of `/clients`, `/records`, `/forwards` and `/rules`, `/config/entries`, and, in the write path every configuration change takes, the group check for a restricted agent token and the version read for the `ETag`. Headers, status codes and JSON are unchanged. A test keeps it true for the next handler: `clu_004_every_federating_backend_call_sits_behind_blocking` reads the production code of `telltale-api` and `telltale`, derives which `Backend` methods can federate from the bridge's callers (not from a list), and fails for a call of one that is not inside `blocking(..)` or `spawn_blocking(..)`, apart from calls that are another type's method of the same name or already run inside one, each named in the test by file, function and method, with its reason and its exact count (an entry whose count is wrong fails, so a call added later to a listed function is found).
- The bridge changes. `Federated::call_one`, `gather` and the cluster support package start their RPCs as tasks on the runtime and wait for them with `recv_timeout` on a `std` channel, for the RPC's own timeout plus 250 ms, instead of a scoped thread running `Handle::block_on`. What has not come in by then is "didn't answer in time": listed in `missingNodes`, or (for the configuration version and entries) answered from this node. A runtime that can't run the tasks costs a read its deadline, never the node its life, from whatever context it is called: a blocking-pool thread, a worker, a current-thread runtime. A task whose caller has gone is not sent. No `block_in_place`.
- **Bounds.** Three, for the three things a read can use up. At most 32 federated reads wait at once (a count, no new dependency): each holds a blocking-pool thread for up to its deadline, so a slow or dead primary can't be used to use up the pool. The calls of all reads together are a budget of 32,768, counted from when a call starts until its read is done with its answer: a read takes a share of it for each of its calls, all of them or none, and the share travels with the call's answer and goes back when the read returns from its wait (or is dropped), or when the call's task is dropped. So the budget bounds the tasks that are running and the answers that wait for their read, together. One read has at most 4,096 calls. A read that is over a limit, or that the budget has no room for, is told "wasn't asked" without being sent: nothing is queued, and it takes no place and no share (a place and the shares are taken in one atomic step, so a refused read never holds either, not even for a moment). A place is given back when the read's last task ends, so tasks queued on a stuck runtime keep theirs, and each keeps its share. **And bytes.** A count does not bound memory: an answer is as big as its peer makes it, up to the cluster channel's 1 MiB frame, so 32,768 held answers could be 32 GiB. So each answer is weighed as it comes in, on the task that got it and before it is kept: its length is added to what its read holds and to what all reads hold, and if either would pass its cap (20 MiB a read, 40 MiB in all: `READ_BYTES`, `HELD_BYTES`), the answer is dropped there and its call is "wasn't kept", which `gather` treats as a node that didn't answer (`missingNodes`, the same label and path as a timeout): no answer is cut short, the rest of the read is kept, and the read is counted (reason `bytes`). The bytes are part of the call's share and go back with it (when the read returns from its wait or is dropped, or the task is dropped), and an answer that comes in after its read stopped waiting is not kept (once the read has given up it is not weighed at all; one that comes in between the end of the wait and that moment is weighed and given back at once). The read's own total is taken first and given back if everyone's has no room, so for an instant it can turn away another answer of the same read, never one of another read. Measured (a scratch program on the build VM that builds each read's largest legitimate answers as the code does and counts the heap; time buckets like a home network's, 1.5 KB each as JSON, or a busy one's, 4.5 KB): one call's answer is a window of 240 buckets, 360 KB (home) to 1.07 MB (busy, which is over the 1,047,552 bytes an answer may be: such a node's minute windows already fail with "answer too large"); a top list of 100 is 8 to 30 KB, a page of 1,000 query-log rows 355 KB, the settings entries tens of KB, a support part at most the frame. On a cluster of 5 nodes (4 peers asked), one read's answers are: for the dashboard's ranges 0.36 MiB (1 hour of minutes) to 16.9 MiB (48 hours of minutes; 50 MiB with busy buckets), for the API's longest ranges (7 days of minutes, 400 days of hours) 59 to 62 MiB (about 180 MiB with busy buckets, if they fitted the frame), and for a read of one call a peer at most 4 MiB. Parsed, answers take 3.3 to 4.5 times their bytes, and the whole read (parse, merge, response) peaks at 4.1 to 5.7 times them: 95 MiB for the dashboard's 48-hour read of 5 home nodes, 333 MiB for the API's 7-day one. On two real processes (a replica with one runtime worker, and a primary built to pad each of its time-bucket answers to about 900 KB), a 7-day read took the replica, which had no data of its own, from 42 MB resident to a high-water mark of 251 MiB with a first cap of 32 MiB (33 MB of answers kept), at the Pi's limit, and to 185 MiB with 20 MiB (20.7 MB kept): 6.6 to 7.3 times the answers above the node's own use. Eight times the largest legitimate read would be 500 MiB a read, more than the Pi's whole unit (`MemoryMax=256M`), so the caps are chosen from memory: with 20 MiB, a read of the peers' answers took that replica to 185 MiB, 72 % of the Pi's unit (the Helm controller has 512 MiB), and the cap is 1.2 times one of the dashboard's 48-hour reads on 5 home nodes (1.6 times on 4) and 5 times the largest read of one call a peer (4 MiB, a full frame from each of 4 peers; a page of 1,000 query-log rows from 4 peers is 1.4 MiB); 40 MiB for all held answers is two reads at their cap. The API's longest ranges come back partial on 5 nodes and on 3 (29.5 to 31 MiB of answers); before, such a read could not have finished on a Pi (333 MiB measured, for 5 nodes). What a caller does after the wait (parsing the answers, merging them, the response) is not covered: when `wait` returns, the bytes go back while their parsed form is being built, so how many reads merge at once is bounded only by the blocking pool's 512 threads (there is no limit on concurrent HTTP requests), as before this change; each merging read is bounded by its 20 MiB of answers (about 145 MiB above the node's own use), so two reads at their cap merging at the same moment would not fit a Pi. **The caps bound the peers' answers only.** This node's own part of a read is not counted (`Federated::timeseries` adds `self.local.timeseries(..)` and the minutes shipped to it after the wait), so one read on a node with its own week of minutes and big buckets can pass a 256 MiB Pi too. That case is not measured (the 185 MiB above is a node with no data of its own); its arithmetic: the own part costs its windows × the bytes a window × the parse factor (3.3 to 4.5) × the merge factor (about 1.25), so a 7-day read of minutes on a node with home-sized buckets (43 windows of about 368 KB) adds about 15 MiB × 4.5 × 1.25 ≈ 85 MiB of heap to the peers' share (20 MiB × 4.5 × 1.25 ≈ 112 MiB), with the node's own 40 MiB or so on top: at or over a Pi's 256 MiB with the allocator's slack. The dashboard's 48-hour reads are 13 windows a node, under a third of that. Counting this node's own part against the read, refusing minute steps over 48 hours on a cluster, or accepting it as documented here is the owner's to choose. Covering the merge needs `wait` to return the answers with a guard the caller drops after merging (the final review's design note): `gather` could carry that guard through its own loop without touching its callers, but the merge is in the callers (the `Backend` methods that parse and merge, about 30), so the guard would be gone before the merge it is meant to cover, and holding it through the merge means changing each of them; not done. A merge that folds each window into the result as it comes (the parsed parts are 4.2 times the merged result for the 7-day read, so that read would peak near its answers plus its result, about 122 MiB, an estimate from the measured parts) or a cap on concurrent federated reads is a later design. 32,768 is 32 reads of 1,024 calls, the most that the first form of this bound (a flat 1,024 calls a read, with 32 reads) allowed to be running or waiting at once, so the number of calls and answers held is the one that was already accepted; the ceiling of 4,096 only lets one read use what many small ones could, and at most 8 reads of that size fit at once. Measured on the build VM, with both ends of the stream in one process and a peer that never answers: 32,768 calls outstanding (8 reads of 4,096) took 139 MiB of resident memory, about 4.4 KiB a call, of which one call's future is 1,632 bytes; no answer was in it, only calls out, and the asking node's own share is less than that, since the peer's handler tasks and buffers are in the number. That is what the flat cap allowed too, though nobody had measured it, and whether 32,768 is still the right budget is the owner's to say (it is one constant, `CALL_BUDGET`). Why 4,096: the longest range any step documents is 87 windows a node today (below), so a read for it from N peers is 87 N calls; the flat cap of 1,024 refused it whole from 12 peers (13 nodes), 7 days of minutes (43 windows) from 24 peers and 400 days of hours (41) from 25. In `mode: scaled` the controller asks each resolver pod as it asks any node, so each pod counts as one of those peers (the chart's default is 2 pods and nothing scales them on its own, so only a deliberately large deployment reached the flat cap). At 4,096 a read for a range goes whole for floor(4,096 / windows) peers: 47 for all of history by the day, 95 for 7 days of minutes and 99 for 400 days of hours. The day range gains one window every 240 days (88 from March 2027), and the number is floor(4,096 / windows), so the 47 falls to 46 then and after that by one every one or two windows (45 at 90 windows, mid-2028; 44 at 92); the others do not move.
- A request for a range of time is one call to each peer for each window of 240 buckets of its step. `timeseries_chunks` counts the windows before it builds one, and a range of more than 256 windows a peer is asked of no peer: they are listed in `missingNodes` and the answer is this node's own (and the minutes shipped to it), as for any read the bridge doesn't send. The longest range any step documents is 87 windows (`step=day`, since 1970; 7 days of minutes is 43, 400 days of hours 41, 15 minutes of seconds 4), so 256 is nearly three times that, and 4,096 calls a read lets floor(4,096 / windows) peers ask for a range whole: 47 for all of history by the day (one window more every 240 days, so 46 from March 2027), 95 for 7 days of minutes and 99 for 400 days of hours. This fan-out is not from this change: the windows were added on 2026-11-16 (in no tagged release when this was written), so that a busy peer's day of minutes fits the 1 MiB frame (CLU-002), and the final review of this change found that a request for a very long range at a fine step (a viewer's `step=second` from the epoch, or to a year far ahead) made one call a peer for each of millions of windows, from a list as long as the range, before any cap saw it. A node that is not in a cluster is not affected: its own data is read with a range query that costs what is stored, not the span. (A clustered node with no reachable peer built the whole list of windows too, before asking anyone; it no longer builds any.)
- A refused read is counted, in the counter `telltale_federated_refused_total{reason}` (`waits`: too many reads waiting; `spent`: the budget of calls spent; `ceiling`: too much asked at once; `bytes`: sent, but an answer was dropped for its bytes; a closed set, never a node or anything a user sent; every reason has a sample from the start), and said in the log, at `warn`, the first time and then at most once a minute with how many were refused since the last line, for each reason. A read that loses answers to the caps on bytes is counted once, on the thread that waited for it, and only for an answer that read saw. Before it was a line at `debug` and healthy peers in `missingNodes`.
- The rule, in `spec/02` §8: code on a runtime worker never waits for work only the runtime can do, and a synchronous bridge to async code has a deadline of the operating system's (a `std` timeout), not the runtime's.
**Alternatives rejected:**
- `tokio::task::block_in_place` in the bridge (the reporter's proposal for it). It panics when the thread is not a worker of a multi-thread runtime (a `Handle::block_on` on another thread, a current-thread runtime, a `LocalSet`), and the release profile aborts on a panic, so a missed case would take DNS down with it. It also hands the worker's core to a new blocking-pool thread for each call, so the runtime's own liveness would depend on the pool (512 threads by default), and a pool used up wedges the runtime again.
- A flat cap on the calls of one read (the first form of the bound: 1,024). It refused ranges the API documents, whole, once peers x windows passed it (12 peers at `step=day` over all of history), and a ceiling high enough for a large cluster would, with 32 reads, have raised the calls and answers a viewer can hold four-fold. A budget for all reads, counted until a read is done with its answers, with a ceiling per read, keeps that count and answers the large cluster. (A first version of the budget gave a call's share back when its task ended; answers that waited for their read then did not count, and 32 waiting reads could hold four times the flat cap's. The final review found it.)
- Clipping a read that doesn't fit (a prefix of its windows, or fewer nodes) instead of refusing it whole. The answer would look complete and be a part; a refused read lists its nodes in `missingNodes`, which the page shows as partial results. (The caps on bytes drop whole answers, never part of one, and list their nodes the same way.)
- Caps on bytes eight times the largest legitimate read (about 500 MiB a read for the API's longest ranges on 5 nodes): more than the Pi's whole unit, so they would bound nothing there. Weighing an answer only when `wait` takes it from the channel: the answers would already be held in the channel, unweighed, until the waiting thread got to them.
- A floor under the worker count (`worker_threads(max(4, cpus))`) or a cap on the blocking pool: lowers the odds, fixes nothing, and costs threads on a small node.
- A runtime of its own for DNS (deferred answers, TCP, DoT, DoH, DoQ): the real gap in CLU-004 (an API or cluster mistake must not starve DNS), but it costs threads, which the footprint priority weighs, and it is a design of its own. Left to the owner.
**Not done (the conservative choice):** the `Backend` trait stays synchronous. Making its federated reads `async` retires the bridge and is the right long-term shape, but touches about 45 methods, the API backend, the fakes and the MCP tools: a later ADR. The watchdog (ADR-163) stays the backstop for a hang this does not cover; it restarts a stuck node after `stall_secs`, at most `max_restarts_per_hour` times, and does not see a partial stall (some workers parked, the others running).
**Known leftover (closed):** the rule above was not literally true of one wait: `alerts.rs` evaluated the drill-report alert with `Handle::block_on(b.drills(20, 0))` on a blocking-pool thread (inside `spawn_blocking`), a wait with no deadline of the operating system's. It now goes through the bridge (`read_within`: the read is a task under the same cap, and the thread waits on a `std` channel for 3 s and the grace), so a runtime that can't run it costs the alert that round's reports (each report is sent once, so the next round sends it), never the thread; and the node's own list of reports is read on that thread, not on the runtime. The alert loop itself is a task on the runtime, so on a runtime that runs nothing it waits like every task; the watchdog (ADR-163) is the backstop there. Test: `clu_004_the_drill_report_alert_gives_up_on_a_wedged_runtime`.
**Consequences:**
- With a healthy runtime and a caller on a blocking-pool thread (every caller today) nothing changes: the same RPCs, the same answers in the same order, the same headers and JSON. On a stuck runtime a federated read costs its deadline (2 s; 1 s for the version) instead of for ever, and a handler that is missed in future costs a worker for that long, not the node.
- A read made on a worker thread of a multi-thread runtime costs its deadline even when the runtime is healthy: tokio puts a task a worker spawns in that worker's own LIFO slot, which no other worker steals, and that worker is parked in `recv_timeout`, so the read's last (or only) task never runs, and the read falls back to this node's value (a `gather` of several peers loses the last). No caller does that: every federated call is under `blocking()` or `spawn_blocking`. It is a warning for the next handler, not a behavior to rely on.
- A read can now say "didn't answer in time" for a peer that would have answered in the last 250 ms of its deadline, or when the runtime is so busy that it can't start the call within the grace: bounded, rare, and visible in `missingNodes`. A read that asks for more than the bounds above is "wasn't asked" for all its peers: no range any step documents is near them for a cluster of up to 46 peers (the day range is 87 windows a peer today, and gains one every 240 days); a range of time over 256 windows a peer (more than 17 hours of seconds, 42 days of minutes, 7 years of hours, or a `to` far in the future) now gets this node's own data with the peers in `missingNodes`, where before the peers were asked a window at a time (and, for a range of years, the requests could use up the node's memory). Whether such a request should be refused with a 400 instead is left to the owner. A read whose peers' answers pass 20 MiB comes back partial, its nodes in `missingNodes` and the windows that did fit merged; the remedy is the same: a narrower `scope`, a coarser `step` or a shorter range. That is the API's longest ranges on 3 home-sized nodes or more. The dashboard at 48 hours makes two minute reads on each refresh (the summary and the chart; the previous period's summary is by the hour), each up to 2,881 buckets from every other node, so a refresh stays whole while those buckets average under about 3.6 KB as JSON on 3 nodes, 2.4 KB on 4 or 1.8 KB on 5 (20 MiB over 2,881 buckets a peer; counted as 13 full windows it is 2.2 KB on 4), and comes back partial above that; a live 4-node cluster measured 0.5 to 0.9 KB a bucket on average (1.3 KB at most), 1.8 MiB for one read, about ten times under. With windows near the frame's 1 MiB, 48 hours is partial from 3 nodes and 24 hours from 4. Two viewers refreshing at the same moment share the 40 MiB, so one of them can see a partial refresh, which the next poll, 15 s later, heals.
- A saturated cap is shared by every federated read of the process: the alert task's, the support package's, the cache warm-up's, a replica's reads of the configuration version and the settings, and an administrator's cluster-wide pause, resume and flush among them. A viewer who keeps 32 slow reads waiting, or eight reads of 4,096 calls on peers that answer slowly (a call counts until its read is done with its answer, so a read held up by one silent peer holds all of its calls for up to 2.25 s), makes the others "wasn't asked" for as long as the primary or a peer is slow; two reads holding 20 MiB of answers each while one silent peer holds them up make the others' answers "wasn't kept" in the same way, for the same 2.25 s. The administrator's changes then report the peers as not answering ("didn't answer (an older version can't pause)", "not reachable: flush it when it's back"), which is not what happened. The warning above says so. A lane of its own for the alert task and for the administrator's changes is left to the owner.
- **Known limit: a change can land after the caller was told it didn't answer.** The reads that change a node (`BlockingPause`, `BlockingResume` and `CacheFlush`, sent through `call_one` and `blocking_fan`) are tasks like the others. A task that has started goes on to the peer after the caller has given up, and the caller gives up at the call's timeout plus 250 ms counted from when the read was made, not from when its task first ran. On a runtime that is backed up, a pause or a flush can therefore reach the peer and be applied while the caller reports "didn't answer in time" for that node. Before, the caller waited for the real answer. The operations are safe to repeat (a pause is set to end a fixed time from now, so a repeat moves the end by the seconds that passed; a resume ends every pause; a flush empties the cache), so the cost is a second request, not a wrong state. A task that has not started when the caller gives up is not sent.
- **Known limit: `missingNodes` is shared by the requests of a node.** A node has one `Federated` and its list of nodes that didn't answer is a field of it, overwritten by every federated read, so a response can carry the list of another request that ran at the same time (older than this decision). The list is read right after the read, to put in the response's `missingNodes`, by `health` (which folds it into its reasons), and by `heatmap_network` and `slo_parts` to decide whether to ask older nodes again, which costs at most one more read; no write or access decision reads it. Three paths still write it in a second step after the read's own write, the shape the refused range lost: `heatmap_network` and `slo_parts` (after the retry for older nodes) and `queries` (when this node's own log is unavailable); none changes a decision. A range that is refused (more windows than the cap) used to write it in two steps, the nodes that are not connected and then the peers it did not ask, and now writes it in one, so it adds no interleaving of its own. Making the list belong to the request changes the `Backend` trait (the list is read through `missing_nodes()` after the call): left to the owner.
- The people who read the cluster's pages on a replica with few CPUs are served from the blocking pool: one pool thread per read, up to 32 at once.
**Verification:** `clu_004_direct_handlers_never_park_a_runtime_worker` (a runtime of one worker, every direct route, a configuration write, and a group-restricted agent); on two real nodes with one worker, `clu_002_a_wedged_runtime_costs_a_federated_read_its_deadline`, `clu_002_a_wedged_runtime_costs_a_gathered_read_its_deadline` and `clu_004_a_read_on_the_only_worker_gives_up_and_frees_it`; the bridge's tests (the order of answers, a call that never finishes, a wedged runtime and the late call not sent, the cap, a current-thread runtime); and a two-node run on the build VM with `TOKIO_WORKER_THREADS=1` (again for the bytes, with a primary built to pad its time-bucket answers: the 7-day read answers with the primary in `missingNodes`, the `bytes` count goes up, the other reads and DNS go on). The bounds: `clu_002_timeseries_windows_stop_at_the_cap` and `clu_002_the_documented_ranges_are_chunked_in_full` (the windows), `clu_004_a_read_over_the_ceiling_is_not_sent`, `clu_004_a_read_at_the_ceiling_is_sent_in_full`, `clu_002_the_documented_ranges_are_sent_in_full_to_forty_peers`, `clu_004_a_big_read_is_sent_when_the_budget_is_free_and_refused_when_it_is_not`, `clu_004_two_reads_over_the_budget_the_second_is_refused_and_takes_nothing`, `clu_004_a_finished_call_keeps_its_share_until_the_read_is_done_with_its_answer`, `clu_004_answers_held_while_waiting_never_pass_the_budget`, `clu_004_shares_come_back_when_the_caller_drops_a_read_with_answers_waiting`, `clu_004_shares_come_back_when_a_read_times_out_with_some_answers_in`, `clu_004_answers_waiting_when_the_runtime_is_dropped_keep_their_shares_until_the_read_is_done`, `clu_004_shares_stay_with_queued_tasks_after_the_caller_gives_up`, `clu_004_shares_come_back_when_the_runtime_drops_the_tasks`, `clu_004_the_worst_case_never_holds_more_than_the_budget` and `clu_004_reads_started_from_many_threads_never_pass_the_budget` (the bridge; the tests of each path a share takes also check its bytes); the bytes: `clu_004_an_answer_over_the_read_cap_is_dropped_and_the_rest_are_kept`, `clu_004_two_reads_over_the_held_cap_the_second_loses_only_the_excess`, `clu_004_an_answer_turned_away_by_the_held_cap_takes_nothing_from_its_read`, `clu_004_reads_from_many_threads_never_pass_the_caps_on_bytes` (`clu_004_the_caps_on_bytes_are_the_documented_ones` only pins the constants and the arithmetic above), and on two real nodes `clu_004_a_peer_whose_answer_is_over_the_cap_on_bytes_is_listed_as_missing`; the metric: `clu_004_refused_reads_are_a_metric_with_a_closed_set_of_reasons`; and on two real nodes `clu_004_a_timeseries_over_the_window_cap_asks_no_peer`, `clu_002_the_documented_ranges_are_asked_of_a_peer_in_full` and `clu_004_a_gathered_read_over_the_ceiling_asks_no_peer`, and the step for long ranges in `deploy/cluster/e2e.sh`; one critical section for a refused range's `missingNodes`: `clu_004_a_refused_range_lists_the_peers_in_one_step`; the guard: `clu_004_every_federating_backend_call_sits_behind_blocking`, with the scanner's own tests (`clu_004_the_scan_flags_a_call_outside_blocking`, `clu_004_the_scan_ignores_what_is_not_a_call`, `clu_004_the_scan_allow_list_is_exact`, `clu_004_an_allow_entry_hides_only_the_call_it_was_written_for`, `clu_004_the_scan_reads_rust_tokens`, `clu_004_the_scan_finds_the_test_code_of_a_file` and `clu_004_the_federating_methods_are_derived_from_the_bridge`); the log: `clu_004_refused_reads_are_logged_once_a_minute_with_their_count`, `clu_004_refusals_are_counted_exactly_under_concurrency` and `clu_004_a_refused_read_is_counted_for_the_log`.

## ADR-175 — The break-glass policy is visible: a public yes or no, the networks for admins, a label and a Settings note; the default stays through 0.x (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #63 ("`disable_local_login` does nothing"). With the flag on and `allowed_admin_networks` left at its default (private networks and loopback, ADR-034), a client on a home, Docker, or Kubernetes network still sees the password form and can sign in, so the page looks the same as with the flag off. The cause is the Accepted default, not a defect; the owner decided on 2026-11-18 to keep the default and make the policy visible. The first pass (the ADR-034 amendment) made start-up loud and the docs plain; nothing in the UI or the API said what the form is, or for which networks. The reporter (rowdynl) and nbacheller, who traced both causes and proposed a start-up warning and a Settings note, asked for exactly that.
**Decision:**
- **A public yes or no.** `GET /api/v1/auth/status` gains `passwordLoginRestricted`: `[auth.oidc] disable_local_login` is on. It is global, the same for every client; `localLogin` keeps its meaning (the form is offered to this client). It carries no network. An older server doesn't send it, and the UI reads a missing field as `false`.
- **The networks for admins, on an existing admin-only answer.** `GET /api/v1/users` (admin role; an agent token is refused by AGT-004's table; a token below the admin scope is capped to its role) returns `{ items, passwordLogin }` instead of `{ items }`, with `passwordLogin = { restricted, networks[], coversWholeNetworks, includesLoopback, loopbackIsTrustedProxy }`. No new route: the users answer is where an admin looks at who can sign in how, it is already behind `require_admin`, and an additive field breaks no client. `/system/info` was the other candidate and is not admin-only (viewers and display sessions read it). Because it sits on a route agents cannot call, it is not an MCP tool; the public boolean is readable by agents (it is in `GET /auth/status`).
- **One rule, and what it counts as wide.** A list is *wide* when it has a network that is neither this machine (`127.0.0.0/8` or a part of it, `::1`) nor a single host (`/32`, `/128`): `coversWholeNetworks`. The final review (2026-11-18) found that the first rule, "anything beyond loopback", warned about `allowed_admin_networks = ["192.168.1.10/32"]`, which is what the guide recommends, and so taught people to ignore the warning that matters (the default list). A wide list, and `[]`, are a warning at start-up and a note on the card; a list of this machine and single hosts is information ("restricted to loopback" for nothing but this machine, "restricted to N hosts" otherwise) and has no note; a `/31` is two hosts but a range, so it is wide. `coversWholeNetworks`, `includesLoopback` and the start-up line's wording all come from `telltale_api::auth::{covers_whole_networks, includes_loopback}`, so the log and the page cannot disagree; a test compares them over thirteen lists (the default, loopback, a host, hosts v4 and v6, loopback with a host, a `/31`, a host with a `/24`, everything, a `/7` around loopback, `::1/64`, a v4-mapped loopback, and `[]`) and three proxy lists. `networks` is the configured list as `address/prefix`, in order, while the flag is on; with the flag off the block says `restricted: false` and nothing else is meaningful (the unused list is not reported).
- **The sign-in page.** With the flag on and the form offered: the separator reads "or, as an admin on a trusted network, with a password" (when there are provider buttons), and a muted line under the form says "Password sign-in is limited to admins on trusted networks. Use it when your sign-in provider is down." A client outside the list sees what it saw before ("Password sign-in is turned off on this server."). No "break-glass" in the UI.
- **Settings, Users (admin only).** A "Password sign-in" card, only while the flag is on: the networks, an "Includes whole networks" note ("not just the machines you name") when the list is wide, a plain "nobody can sign in with a password" when the list is empty, a `trusted_proxies` reminder for a list that names this machine (`includesLoopback`) while `127.0.0.1` is inside no `trusted_proxies` network (`loopbackIsTrustedProxy` false: the same `in_network` test `remote()` applies to a proxy's peer address, so an ingress network does not count; a hint, since this node cannot tell whether a proxy exists, and a proxy that connects over `::1` is not looked for), and where to change it (`allowed_admin_networks` under `[auth.oidc]`, restart) with a link to the guide.
- **The default stays through 0.x.** ADR-034's default is unchanged. Before v1.0 the owner may decide to tighten it; if so, one release warns first ("`allowed_admin_networks` is not set; the default becomes loopback-only in a later release") and the change is a superseding ADR with a release-note line.
**Alternatives rejected:** a loopback-only default now (it silently ends password sign-in for every existing user of the flag on upgrade, discovered only when the provider is down; it does not help behind a proxy on the same host, where every browser is loopback; and in Docker and Kubernetes the server rarely sees `127.0.0.1` from outside the container); the networks in the public status (it tells any visitor where an admin password is accepted); a new `GET /auth/policy` route (an extra operation to keep agent-ready, for data that fits an existing admin answer); the detail on `/system/info` (readable by viewers and wall displays).
**Consequences:**
- OpenAPI and the UI types change additively (`AuthStatus.passwordLoginRestricted`, `UserList`, `PasswordLogin`); `GET /users` clients that read `.items` are unaffected.
- Mixed versions: a new UI on an older server shows nothing new (missing fields read as "not restricted" and "no block"); an older UI on a new server ignores the fields.
- A Helm resolver pod, where nobody signs in, carries the same fields; nothing there reads them.
- Credit: nbacheller and rowdynl (issue #63).
**Verification:** `api_004_status_says_when_password_sign_in_is_restricted`, `api_004_the_policy_detail_is_admin_only`, `api_004_the_policy_reports_single_hosts_and_the_loopback_proxy`, `api_004_a_list_of_single_hosts_is_information`, `api_004_the_loopback_hint_follows_the_trusted_proxy_list`, `api_004_the_start_up_line_and_the_policy_agree`, the committed OpenAPI test, and the Playwright tests `api_004 the sign-in page says when password sign-in is restricted` and `api_004 settings shows where password sign-in is allowed`.
**Amended 2026-11-19 (hand-off 20; amendment Proposed):** the log hints at the mistake the Settings card can only guess at. When the TCP peer of a request is loopback, the request carries `X-Forwarded-For` or `Forwarded`, and the peer is not inside `[api] trusted_proxies`, `remote()` returns the peer exactly as before (which address is believed, `trusted_proxies` handling, the break-glass default, sessions and tokens are untouched) and logs one INFO line, at most once in ten minutes per process (one `AtomicU64` with the last time, like the federated reads' refusal line; no new dependency): requests from that address carry a forwarded-address header but `[api] trusted_proxies` doesn't list that proxy, so client addresses are taken as the proxy's; list it to restrict by client. The final review (F1) found that a proxy that sends only the standard `Forwarded` header would be given that advice wrongly: `remote()` reads a listed proxy's client only from `X-Forwarded-For` and never parses `Forwarded`, so listing such a proxy would stop the line and leave every client looking like the proxy. A request with `Forwarded` and no `X-Forwarded-For` therefore gets a different line from the same limiter: this node reads client addresses only from `X-Forwarded-For`, so they are taken as the proxy's whatever `trusted_proxies` says; make the proxy send `X-Forwarded-For`, then list it. (Teaching `remote()` to read `Forwarded` would change which address is believed, which this amendment does not.) The line names the peer (the connection's own address) and never the header or any address in it: the header is whatever the sender wrote, and copying it would let a client write the log. Only loopback peers get it: a LAN host that sends the header itself is not a proxy, and a line for it would be noise about a spoof attempt. Verification: the `api_004_*hint*` tests (one line for a burst and one more after ten minutes, with a clock the test moves; none for a listed proxy, a non-loopback peer, or a request with no forwarded-address header; never the header's value; `remote()`'s answer the same in every case).

## ADR-176 — The Get started page, README and running guide lead with the native install for a Raspberry Pi or Linux; the Docker Compose bundle stays supported and is the Docker path (Accepted)
**Context:** user feedback, and the owner's direction of 2026-11-18: the Get started page's Raspberry Pi tab led with the Docker Compose bundle, so someone who only wants TelltaleDNS on a Pi or a Linux machine had to install Docker first. The other path was already there and tested: `deploy/systemd/install.sh` downloads one static binary, checks its signature and checksum, installs a sandboxed systemd unit and a starter config, and has a guided setup (OPS-001, DOC-001, T7.6) and an uninstall (OPS-004, T9.21); `deploy/systemd/e2e.sh` runs it end to end in CI. OPS-004 (`spec/01`) named the two paths the other way round: "the same image via Docker/Podman Compose (primary path); a static binary + systemd unit + install script (secondary path)". The docs and the page were reordered; the requirement text was not, and nothing recorded the switch.
**Decision:**
- **What leads.** The Get started page (its first and default tab; `#pi` and `#native` open it), the README quick start, and `docs/running.md` "Raspberry Pi and Linux" lead with the native install. The Docker Compose bundle follows as the Docker path, for anyone who already runs Docker (on the page, under Docker; `#compose` and `#docker` open it).
- **What stays.** The Compose bundle (`deploy/compose/`), its image, `deploy/compose/e2e.sh`, and support for it. CI keeps testing both paths (the `native` job runs the Compose bundle and `install.sh` with the systemd unit).
