Nothing about the binary, the config or the defaults changes: the installer still installs the latest release unless `--edge` is given, and Compose still pulls `:latest` unless `TELLTALE_TAG` says otherwise.
- **The requirement.** OPS-004 now reads: "Raspberry Pi / Linux: a static binary + systemd unit + install script (primary path); the same image via Docker/Podman Compose, and on Kubernetes (OPS-002), are supported alternatives." That is the old text with the two paths' labels exchanged and Kubernetes named as an alternative; nothing it required is dropped (the Compose bundle stays in CI), and `docs/v1-requirements.md` is regenerated from it.
- **Relation to ADR-004.** ADR-004 (Accepted) puts the OCI image and the Helm chart in Tier 1, the static binary + systemd unit in Tier 2 (also `spec/08` §1), and says the Pi runs the image. This ADR changes which path is recommended on a Pi or Linux machine, not what is built, tested or released first: both paths are tested in CI. The owner approved it as an amendment, not a re-tiering, and the pointer is the whole amendment: ADR-004 and `spec/08` §1 each carry one "Amended by ADR-176" line, and their text and the tiers are unchanged.
**Alternatives rejected:** keeping Compose first and adding native as a second tab (what the feedback was about); calling Compose "advanced" or dropping it (many people run Docker or Podman already, and the bundle is the tested Docker path); changing the requirement without a record of why.
**Consequences:**
- More first installs run `install.sh`, which on Ubuntu changes the machine's resolver. The installer's wait for port 53 to be free before it starts the service, and the systemd e2e step's record of who holds port 53 when it fails, were added after a CI run in which the service could not bind port 53 and the log did not say who held it; more first installs make them matter more.
- Documentation and page order, the wording of OPS-004, and one "Amended by" line each in ADR-004 and `spec/08` §1: no code, schema, API or image changes, and nobody on Compose has to move.
- The owner approved this on 2026-11-18, with the decision "Go with native first" for the Get started page and the guides; the OPS-004 wording above follows from it.
**Verification:** `python3 site/build.py --check` (the page, the guides and every link in them), `python3 spec/trace.py --check` (the OPS-004 row of `docs/v1-requirements.md`), and the CI `native` job, which runs both paths: the Compose bundle (`deploy/compose/e2e.sh`) and the installer with the unit (`deploy/systemd/e2e.sh`), with `deploy/systemd/guided-e2e.sh` and `deploy/systemd/portwait-test.sh` for the installer's answers, its wait for port 53 and its single start.

## ADR-177 — Trust follows manifests in order: a node takes CAs only from a manifest no older than the one it last took them from (Proposed)
**Context:** ADR-066 moves the trusted CAs in steps carried by the primary's signed manifests, and every step is a new `(epoch, seq)` (the bundle is part of the manifest's metadata, so a step always publishes a new version). A node took the bundle of any manifest that verified against the CAs it trusts now and kept one of them, and it did so before it compared the manifest's version with what it had applied: a node that can't apply a version must still follow the CA steps, or the rotation never completes. But manifest frames are accepted from every authenticated peer, the newest frame from any stream replaces the last, and a node that was the primary keeps sending its last manifest on every stream after it steps down. So an older manifest that still verified could move trust backwards. After the retire, the switch's manifest (`[new, old]`, signed by the new CA) made a node trust the retired CA again until the primary's next change; in the trust phase, the manifest from before the rotation (`[old]`) made it drop the CA being added, and the key it held for that CA (`ca-next.key`) was deleted with it. A failover during a rotation and the old primary's return did it with no attacker at all. Found by reading the code in the 2026-11-18 review of the CA rotation race (no incident).
**Decision:**
- A node takes the CA bundle of a manifest only when (1) the manifest's epoch isn't older than the epoch the node knows, and (2) its version, epoch first and then seq, isn't older than the version of the manifest it last took its CAs from. The same version is taken again: a manifest sent again finishes a step a crash cut short, and a primary that published a version, crashed before it recorded it, and published that version again is followed.
- The version is kept beside `ca.crt`, in `ca.version` (`{"cluster_id", "epoch", "seq"}`). The trust change writes it itself, after `ca.crt`, under the lock the CA key files already share (the key share and the trust change). It is written for every manifest whose CAs are taken, also when the bundle doesn't change, so a node with no mark (upgraded from a build without one) or with one a crash left behind (after `ca.crt`, before the mark) has the current version once its next manifest arrives. No file means no mark, and the first manifest is taken as before: existing nodes upgrade with no migration, and an older build ignores the file. A file that can't be read or parsed counts as no mark too: the rule fails open on purpose, so a damaged file never stops trust from moving (availability over strictness), and the next manifest taken writes a good one.
- The mark belongs to its cluster. A mark with another cluster's ID is no mark, and `cluster init` and `cluster join` remove one left in the directory, so a node that left a cluster and joined another (with an older build in between, which doesn't know the file, or not) is never held back by the first cluster's versions. A backup (ADR-063) holds no `cluster/` directory, so a restore brings no mark.
- A manifest refused this way isn't applied either (it is older than one the node has taken already). That holds for a replica's follower and for a witness's trust follower (`follow_trust`). It is one line at `debug` each time, since a replay can repeat it. Trust is still taken before configuration is fetched or applied.
- No wire, manifest, key-share or election change.
**Not done (left to the owner):**
- A node that stops being the primary still sends its last manifest. Every receiver that hears its Hello learns the newer epoch first (a former primary is a voter, and the Hello comes before the manifest on the stream), so it now refuses that manifest for trust as it already did for configuration. Clearing the head on step-down is likely safe but wasn't proven against the whole set of cluster end-to-end scripts.
- Taking a manifest only from the node its signed `primary` names: no node relays another's manifest (a node sends only the head it published itself), so it would break no catch-up, but it wouldn't stop a former primary from sending its own last manifest either, only a member replaying one it received.
- The registry and failover mode a witness keeps from the manifests are not ordered by this: an older manifest still replaces them.
- Telling apart two manifests of one version (follow-up, designed, not built): `ca.version` would also hold a digest of the bundle taken, and at an equal version a node would take only that same bundle. Crash repair still works (the manifest sent again carries the same bundle); a primary that publishes a version again with other content after a crash is followed one version later, and a rotation makes a new version at every step.
**Consequences:**
- A manifest of an older version can no longer re-trust a retired CA, undo a rotation step, or delete the key of the CA being added.
- Two different manifests of the same version can't be told apart: the equal version is taken, so either one moves trust. That happens only if the primary publishes a version and then crashes, or fails to save `published.json` (a failed save is only a warning), before it records it, and publishes that seq again with other content, a rotation step having run in between. Someone who holds both signed manifests can then move every node between the two bundles until the next version. It needs a crash or a failed save during a rotation, and both signed manifests. The digest above would close it.
- A primary whose versions go backwards (same epoch) can't move trust until its seq passes the mark of the others, as it already can't move their configuration, and a rotation waits; DNS is unaffected. Triggers: its data directory put back from an older copy, or its `cluster/published.json` lost or unreadable (for example a full disk and a restart), after which its seq starts again low (from what it last applied as a replica, if anything). Recoveries: (a) promote another node, since a new epoch beats any seq; (b) where no other node can be promoted, stop each of the other nodes, delete its `cluster/ca.version` and start it again. That is safe: with no mark, the next manifest is taken. Configuration still needs the promotion, or the primary's seq to pass what each node applied.
- One small file per node, written once for each manifest taken. Manifests come with configuration changes.
- Mixed versions: an older build ignores the file and doesn't move it, so after a downgrade and an upgrade the mark is behind the node's trust (never ahead, unless the cluster's versions went backwards meanwhile): it holds nothing newer back, and moves on with the next manifest.
**Verification:** in `crates/telltale-cluster/src/net_trust_tests.rs`, `clu_001_trust_is_not_taken_from_an_older_manifest`, `clu_001_follow_ignores_the_cas_of_an_older_manifest` (and doesn't apply it), `clu_001_follow_trust_ignores_the_cas_of_an_older_manifest`, `clu_001_the_same_version_again_is_taken_and_mends_a_crash`, `clu_001_a_trust_mark_from_another_cluster_is_ignored`, `clu_005_a_new_epoch_moves_trust_though_its_seq_is_lower` and `clu_001_a_node_without_a_trust_mark_starts_one_with_its_next_manifest`; `clu_001_a_new_identity_starts_without_a_trust_mark` in `node.rs`. `deploy/cluster/rotate-e2e.sh` shows the rotation still completes (the happy path only).

## ADR-178 — A node that grants a pre-vote to a node that sorts before it gives that node the round, so two candidates rarely split the vote, and with three voters don't, short of a lost or badly delayed message, a restart, or a disagreement over the epoch (Proposed)
**Context:** `deploy/cluster/gitops-failover-e2e.sh` kills the primary of three eligible nodes (three voters), so two replicas can lead. Of the 79 CI jobs that reached that step, 65 had a new primary at the next epoch after 15 to 16 s, 12 needed a second round (30 to 35 s), and 2 needed a third (49 to 50 s), which failed the script's 45 s limit. The VM study had 2 of 48 runs at two rounds. `spec/12` §10 promises a new primary within 30 s. There was jitter (the caller draws `jitter` in 0 to 7.5 s on every step and passes it to `tick`), but:
- **It never applied to the first attempt.** While the primary lives, a replica's retry time is long past, so the replica sends its pre-vote on the first tick of its election loop (every 250 ms) after it is free to stand. Both replicas are freed at the same moment. The lease ends when the same renewal is 15 s old on both, and under the GitOps authority the hold-back ends when the dead primary's last heartbeats, on two streams opened 10 to 20 ms apart, are 15 s old.
- **The scripts start the replicas back to back, so their loops tick almost in step.** In the 48 kept runs of the VM study, the two replicas' tick phases were 0 to 34 ms apart, median 8 ms (read from the times both logged `automatic failover on`, which the loop logs on a tick). Independent phases would spread over 0 to 125 ms. The only two runs there that needed a second round had the two smallest gaps, 0.4 and 0.8 ms. In one of CI's three-round failures, both replicas logged that line in the same microsecond.
- **Pre-votes less than about one message delay apart cross.** Each node grants the other's (a pre-vote binds nothing), both ask for real votes, each votes for itself, and neither has a majority. Each one's own vote is a 15 s lease on its ballot, so the other's next request is refused until that lease ends (the fencing rule): a split costs a full lease.
- **The retries run on the same tick grids.** The jitter is rounded up to the next tick, so two retries in one tick can split again.

**Measured:** `crates/telltale-cluster/tests/rounds.rs` runs the real `Elector` on every node, driven as `failover::run` drives it. Each node's loop steps on its own 250 ms grid and on every reply, with the hold-back from heartbeats, the jitter drawn on every step, an fsync (0.5 to 5 ms) before a changed ballot leaves, and an explicit one-way delay (50 µs plus an exponential). The primary is killed 0.1 to 0.6 s after the replicas get the cluster key, as in the scripts. The replicas' phases are within 30 ms of each other (as measured); the mean delay is the model's one free parameter.
- Before this decision, at a mean of 6 ms, 18.3 % of 200,000 GitOps failovers (three voters) needed a second round (CI: 17.7 %), at 0.5 to 1 ms 3 to 6 % (the VM: 4 %).
- Every first pre-vote went out on the step that found its node free to stand.
- A split happened only when the two candidates' first pre-votes were less than 30 ms apart (three voters): 34 % of the runs under 1 ms apart split, 15 % of those 10 to 30 ms apart, and none of the 1,316 runs further apart.
- The model's third round (0.15 % at the calibrated point, up to 0.8 % with fully aligned ticks) is below CI's 2 of 79 (2.5 %, a 95 % interval of 0.3 % to 8.8 %), so the CI count is either an unlucky draw from the aligned case or the sign of something the model leaves out (epochs in heartbeats, the server's own processing time, runner-wide stalls).

**Decision:**
- A node that **grants a pre-vote** to a candidate whose node ID **sorts before its own**, for the epoch it would stand for itself or a newer one, gives that candidate the round:
  - a pre-candidate stands down (it becomes a follower) and may stand again `ROUND_MS` (2 s) later. It does not wait for the retry time it set when its attempt started (`LEASE_MS / 3` plus the jitter, 5 to 12.5 s after that start);
  - a follower doesn't stand for `ROUND_MS` from then on, or until a later retry time it already had (`next_try_ms` is pushed, never pulled forward).
- So giving a node the round costs the giver that round and no more.
- A node that grants a pre-vote to a node that sorts after it, that refuses one, or that grants a real vote isn't affected. Neither is a candidate (it has voted for itself) or a primary.
- Nothing else changes: the reply is the ballot's as before, and a pre-vote still changes no ballot. One vote per epoch, the refusal of candidates behind, the lease and its margin, majority, pre-vote, epochs, the wire, and the registry are unchanged. The first attempt is still made at once, and the jitter and the back-offs after a round that times out are as before.
- REQ: CLU-005, in `Elector::on_ask` / `give_way`.

**Why it is safe (any number of voters):** safety never depended on when a node stands.
- Two primaries in one epoch are prevented by `Ballot::decide`, the leases and `MARGIN_MS`, which this doesn't touch.
- The change moves only when a node next stands, and drops a pre-vote that hasn't become a vote.
- The giver's retry can now come earlier than its own retry time (in the unit test, 3.01 s instead of 13.499 s). It is still at least `ROUND_MS` after its attempt began, which the code before this decision already allowed after a round that timed out with no jitter.

**What it guarantees: no split when neither candidate can win without the other**, short of a lost or badly delayed message, a restart, or a disagreement over the epoch (listed below). That covers three voters (the two candidates and the dead primary, as in the e2e), and four voters with one dead (each candidate needs every other live node). A split needs both candidates to vote for themselves, so here each must have the other's pre-vote grant before the other's real request arrives. Take the one that sorts after (B):
- If B grants A's pre-vote as a pre-candidate, it stands down.
- If B grants it as a follower, it doesn't start for 2 s.
- In both cases, by the time B stands again, A's pre-vote round is over: A has voted for itself (and refuses B), or has given up.
- If B is already a candidate, it refuses A, and A can't vote for itself without B's grant.

So with three voters, or four with one dead, a second round needs one of:
- a message delayed past a round (2 s), or lost;
- a restart;
- or the two candidates asking for different epochs (a node doesn't give way to a request for an older epoch than the one it would stand for).

**What it only reduces: more voters.** When a candidate can reach a majority without the other, a split stays possible. With three eligible nodes and two witnesses (five voters, majority three), and the primary gone:
1. The higher candidate collects the witnesses' pre-votes and votes for itself before the lower one's request reaches it.
2. It then refuses that request, so it never gives way.
3. The lower one collects the same witnesses' pre-votes (they bind nothing) and votes for itself too.
4. The witnesses' real votes go one each way, two votes each, and a lease passes before the next round.

What decides it is how the majority is made up, not how many candidates there are: four voters with one dead and three candidates don't split (with the same exceptions as three voters), while five voters with two candidates can. The same holds for five eligible nodes with some in maintenance or holding back under GitOps.

**Measured after it**, on the same seeds (200,000 each; mean / p99 / worst seconds to a primary after the kill):

| Scenario (voters) | Before: 2 rounds / 3+ | Before: mean / p99 / worst | After: 2 rounds / 3+ | After: mean / p99 / worst |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GitOps, the scripts' timing (3) | 18.3 % / 0.148 % | 16.2 / 34.5 / 103.0 | 0 / 0 | 13.0 / 15.2 / 15.3 |
| Three eligible, the scripts' timing (3) | 18.5 % / 0.153 % | 15.3 / 33.8 / 69.2 | 0 / 0 | 12.1 / 15.2 / 15.3 |
| GitOps, independent phases (3) | 3.05 % / 0.026 % | 13.5 / 31.5 / 66.3 | 0 / 0 | 13.0 / 15.2 / 15.3 |
| Three eligible + two witnesses, the scripts' timing (5) | 12.4 % / 0.071 % | 15.0 / 33.7 / 67.1 | 2.3 % / 0.001 % | 13.2 / 30.5 / 45.5 |
| Three eligible + two witnesses, independent phases (5) | 1.91 % / 0.014 % | 13.1 / 30.2 / 53.2 | 0.38 % / 0 | 12.9 / 15.2 / 37.9 |
| Five eligible, the scripts' timing (5) | 24.2 % / 2.06 % | 17.2 / 45.2 / 117.2 | 4.0 % / 0.069 % | 13.5 / 30.1 / 56.4 |
| Five eligible, independent phases (5) | 3.8 % / 0.10 % | 13.4 / 29.9 / 116.4 | 0.74 % / 0.003 % | 12.9 / 15.2 / 45.7 |

Notes on the table:
- With three voters, 0 of 200,000 runs needed a second round (a 95 % upper bound of 1.5 × 10⁻⁵).
- A single candidate (two eligible nodes and a witness, three voters, `failover-e2e.sh`) always took one round, before and after.
- The partition simulator (`tests/sim.rs`, 3 to 5 voters) still passes. It prints how many schedules took 10 s or more to recover after healing, but doesn't assert it.
  - At CI's 10,000 schedules that count went from 3 (a pre-candidate keeping its own retry time) to 8 (the round-only wait). That isn't significant: one generator drives each schedule's delays, drops, jitter and chaos, so any change re-randomises every schedule (two-sided p ≈ 0.23).
  - At 100,000 schedules on the same seeds (`f2el_r4`): main had 249 (worst 32.6 s), the earlier form 47 (worst 16.45 s), and the round-only wait 52 (worst 16.45 s). Giving way cut that tail about five-fold, and the two forms of it don't differ.
- There is one real mechanism left, at any voter count: a pre-vote grant delayed by more than `ROUND_MS` plus the giver's tick lag can still let two candidates cross and split. The simulator exercises it (it delays 5 % of its messages by up to 3 s); `rounds.rs` can't (its delays average 6 ms). Each occurrence costs one lease. In production it needs an RPC stall of about 2 s (TCP retransmits, an overloaded Pi). It is not a safety issue.

**What it costs: a lower node that can't win.** A node that keeps granting pre-votes to lower nodes that can't win is held back after each one: for a round (2 s, plus the next tick of its loop) as a follower or as a pre-candidate that stands down. `rounds.rs` builds the case, with only one node, H, able to win once the primary is gone (200,000 seeds each; mean / p99 / worst seconds after the kill, before → after):

| Scenario | the scripts' timing | independent phases |
|---|---|---|
| `Cut4`: four voters, L and X sort before H, the L-X link cut | 12.5 / 15.3 / 24.5 → 14.5 / 19.3 / 27.1 | 12.5 / 15.3 / 24.6 → 14.0 / 17.5 / 26.1 |
| `Star5`: five voters, three lower leaves that reach only H | 12.9 / 15.2 / 24.4 → 14.9 / 20.1 / 34.6 | 12.9 / 15.2 / 24.4 → 14.6 / 19.3 / 34.6 |

- H is elected in every run, so the election is delayed, never stopped. The lower nodes' asks follow their own jitter, redrawn on every step, so each further delay is a fresh draw.
- With the scripts' near-lockstep ticks, all of them are freed together, so H's first attempts are often spoiled; each spoil costs H a round.
- The test's bound is `LEASE_MS` plus twenty spoiled attempts of a round and a tick each (60 s).
- `Star5`'s worst case, 34.6 s, is above `spec/12` §10's 30 s (see "Spec and tests").
- **In an earlier form of this decision**, a pre-candidate that gave way kept its own retry time (5 to 12.5 s after its attempt started). That took 17.4 / 32.6 / 62.0 and 14.6 / 26.6 / 57.0 (`Cut4`), and 17.4 / 33.8 / 64.3 and 15.0 / 26.8 / 50.3 (`Star5`) on the same seeds. Every split rate in the table above is the same either way.

**Spec and tests:** with three voters the worst case in the model is one round, 15.3 s after the kill (mean 13.03 s), so `spec/12` §10's 30 s holds and stays as written. In the partial-partition cases above (`Star5`: lower nodes that can't win), the model's worst case is 34.6 s against 24.4 s on main, which exceeds that 30 s; the promise was written for a plain kill, and the spec isn't changed. `gitops-failover-e2e.sh` (three voters) keeps its 45 s limit, and its comment now says why: one round that splits for a reason the model doesn't have still passes.

**Consequences:**
- In a tie, the node with the lower node ID becomes primary (`docs/running.md`, the Cluster guide). The ID is the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the node's key, so the tie always goes to the same node, and that can't be configured.
- If that lowest-ID node is systematically slow (for example a Pi whose SD-card fsync takes more than 2 s), it takes every tie and can time out its own real round, which then costs a lease. The hazard existed before; now it falls on the same node every time.
- A node that keeps granting pre-votes to lower nodes that can't win (they reach it but not a majority) is held back after each one, as measured above. Its attempts are delayed, never stopped (any number of voters). An eligible node could ask over and over on purpose to hold higher nodes back, but it holds the cluster key and could already stand and win (ADR-154's trust note), so this widens nothing.
- With more than three voters a split is still possible, now and then (see the table): left to the owner. A wait that grows after a split round would make the third round rarer still.

**Alternatives rejected:**
- A random wait before the first attempt (Raft's randomized timeout). With loops that tick in step, two waits land in the same 250 ms tick with a chance of about 250 ms over the range, so a range short enough not to slow every failover (adding about a third of it on average) doesn't separate them.
- A finer tick, or a wake-up at the retry time: more wake-ups, and still a slower first attempt.
- Fixed delays by rank: a node waits whenever a lower one is dead, and the `Elector` doesn't know which voters are eligible.
- Widening the e2e limit, which would only hide the split.

**Verification:**
- `clu_005_crossed_pre_votes_elect_the_lower_node_in_one_round`, `clu_005_a_follower_gives_a_lower_node_whose_pre_vote_it_grants_a_round` and `clu_005_giving_way_costs_the_giver_one_round` (unit tests).
  - The first one's lease check fails when the follower's lease check is taken out of `tick`.
  - The third one fails when a pre-candidate keeps its own retry time.
- `clu_005_a_failover_with_two_candidates_takes_one_round` (three voters), `clu_005_four_candidates_split_the_vote_far_less_often` (five voters) and `clu_005_two_candidates_with_two_witnesses_split_less_often` (five voters): they fail before this decision and pass after.
- `clu_005_a_failover_with_one_candidate_takes_one_round`.
- `clu_005_a_lower_node_that_cannot_win_only_delays_the_election` (`Cut4`, `Star5`).
- `clu_005_failover_timing_never_elects_two_primaries`: adversarial delays up to 3 s, up to 5 % lost, all seven topologies.
- `tests/sim.rs`.
- `gitops-failover-e2e.sh` on real processes, against the base binary: on the build VM, 2 CPUs per cluster, two clusters at once with the binaries alternating, 21 runs each. The new binary needed one round in 21 of 21 (14 to 16 s). The base binary needed one round in 20 and two in 1 (36 s). All 42 passed.
- `failover-e2e.sh` and `deploy/cluster/e2e.sh` pass on the new binary.
- Twenty runs can't show a rate of 10⁻⁴; the simulation is the proof.

## ADR-179 — A credential file on an alert destination made through the API is set by an administrator, in a folder meant for secrets (Proposed)
**Context:** an alert destination can name `token_file` (ntfy, Gotify) or `password_file` (email). The node reads the file when it sends and passes what it holds on: as a Bearer token or in Gotify's URL to the destination's address, or in the login to the mail server. Destinations made through the API and the UI (T9.6, an override kind of ADR-069) needed the operator role (agents: `config:write:alerts`), and nothing said which file one could name. So the choice of a file, and of where its content goes, was open to a role that isn't trusted with the node's secrets: backups, users and the cluster's keys are an administrator's. Found in a code review on 2026-11-19.
**Decision:**
- **Who.** Only an administrator adds or changes an alert destination made through the API that names a credential file, before or after the change: an admin's own sign-in (a session or HTTP Basic) or an admin-scoped API token. An agent token never counts as one, whoever owns it and whatever else it may do (an admin's token that also holds `cluster:admin` included): a credential file is the kind of secret a backup holds, and agents can't download backups either. A token for MCP from the sign-in provider (ADR-071) is an agent token too.
- Anyone else gets 403 `forbidden` naming the field, with the hint "Setting or changing a credential file needs an administrator", and a dry run is refused the same way. They keep every destination without a credential file, may delete any destination (that only takes something away), and may send a test to any (it goes where an administrator chose).
- The check runs where the write is planned, on the node that applies it, against the destination in effect (the files' or the API's), before anything else in the body is checked. A write forwarded from a replica carries whether the entry node found the caller to be an administrator (`by_admin`). The primary trusts the entry node for that as it already does for who the caller is (ADR-054: members authenticate each other with mTLS, and an ephemeral member can't forward writes), so the flag gives a member nothing it couldn't do before. A write from a node that doesn't send the flag is not an administrator's.
- Every route that writes an alert destination goes through this: `PUT`/`DELETE /api/v1/alerts/destinations/{name}`, their dry runs, and a replica's forwarded write. There is no other: no bulk or import route, and no MCP or plan tool writes one (an MCP tool calls the REST routes with the credentials its request came with).
- REQ: OBS-010, API-002, in `credential_guard` (`api_backend.rs`), `ManagedWrite::by_admin` and the forwarded `Write::Managed`.
- **Where.** The file is an absolute path, written without `.`, `..` or empty parts, under one of three folders: `/run/secrets/` (Docker and Compose secrets, the documented and UI-suggested place), `/etc/telltale-secrets/` (the Helm chart's `secretMounts`), or `<data_dir>/secrets/` (for a native install, or anything else). These are where a file is put to be a secret for a service; the data directory itself (the state database, the cluster's keys, the setup token) and the rest of the filesystem are not. The rule bounds where a file may be, not which secret it is: `/etc/telltale-secrets/` also holds the chart's other mounts (an OIDC client secret, for one), and on a Compose host `/run/secrets/` can hold other services' secrets. `/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/` is excluded: Kubernetes puts the pod's service-account token there. The path is compared by components (`/run/secrets-x` is not under `/run/secrets`), as written and then with its links followed as far as it exists against the folders with theirs followed: a Kubernetes Secret mount's own links (`key -> ..data/key`) stay inside and pass, a link that leads out of the folder is refused (also when its target isn't there yet), and a file that doesn't exist yet passes on its path alone, so an administrator can create it afterwards. In `secret_path.rs`; one rule for both places below. *(Amended 2026-11-20, proposed with ADR-181: the cloud workload-identity token folders `/run/secrets/eks.amazonaws.com/` and `/run/secrets/azure/` (and `/var/run/secrets/...`) are excluded too, for alert destinations and routers alike; and no alert destination names a file the routers feature keeps in `<data_dir>/secrets/`: a name there starting `router-`, `.written` or `.request-key` (in any ASCII case), as written or through a link, an administrator's included, so not even an administrator can send a key pasted in the UI to an alert's address. A key a platform mounts in `/run/secrets/` or `/etc/telltale-secrets/` is still a file an administrator may name for an alert destination: that part of ADR-179 is unchanged.)*
- **At write time,** every API write of an alert destination that names a credential file, an administrator's included, is refused otherwise with 422 `invalid_config` naming the field and the folders. The message is the same whatever is at the path (nothing read from a file, and no difference between a file that is there and one that isn't), and an operator gets the 403 first.
- **At use time,** every destination in effect that names a credential file is held to the rule unless it is equal to one that this node's own sources define: its config files and, on a node that takes the cluster's configuration from Git, the commit in use (`<data_dir>/cluster/git-shared.toml`). The merged configuration doesn't keep where an entry came from, so the node asks the other way round: what its administrators wrote stays as written, and everything else is checked. That covers a destination made through the API on this node (stored by an older version, or restored from a backup), an API override of a file's destination (not equal to the file's), a destination this node took from another (a replica's Send test, and a primary promoted after a failover, whose configuration is its files plus the cluster's last manifest), and a primary pinned to a version, whose destinations come from an earlier version of its own configuration. A destination held this way is not used: its file isn't read, alerts aren't sent to it, its test send answers why, and the node logs one warning naming it each time the configuration changes (not at every check), with the reason shown as its last delivery on the Alerts page. The check fails closed: if it can't run, no destination with a credential file is sent until it does (one warning); a part of the path that can't be looked at is refused; and if the config files couldn't be read at start-up (the running configuration then stands in for them, API entries and all), nothing counts as the node's own until a reload reads them (one warning). The files are stored before the configuration made from them on every reload, so a check that reads the new configuration also reads its files. Nothing is read from `state.db` for it.
- **The cost:** on a node that follows another, a destination from the primary's config files whose file is outside the folders, and which this node's own files don't define the same way, isn't sent from here: a replica's Send test refuses it, and after a failover the new primary doesn't send to it, until its file moves into a folder or that node's own files define it the same way. While the cluster is pinned, an administrator who edits a config-file destination whose file is outside the folders stops it being sent until the pin ends (the pinned version still has the old definition). With the Helm chart every pod mounts the same ConfigMap (`statefulset.yaml` and `resolvers.yaml` both mount it at `/etc/telltale`), so such a destination is equal there and unaffected.
**Not covered:** destinations in this node's config files, the Helm values or the Git commit in use (whoever writes those controls the node), which keep any path; an administrator (trusted with the node's secrets already); a user of the host who can write or link files into one of the folders (they control what the folder holds); a destination stored before the upgrade, by a role below admin through an older version, that names another secret *inside* one of the folders (it passes the rule: see Upgrading); on a node whose own resolver is TelltaleDNS, anyone who may add local names or forwards can change where an `http://` destination's host resolves, so a token sent over `http://` can reach that address (`https://`, `smtps://` and STARTTLS are protected by certificate checks; prefer `https://` for a destination with a credential file; whether to refuse `http://` there is left to the owner).
**Consequences:**
- An admin's agent token (or a token for MCP from the sign-in provider) can no longer add or change a destination with a credential file; the admin's own sign-in or an admin-scoped API token can.
- An administrator can no longer name a credential file outside the three folders through the API or the UI, including when overriding a destination of the config files whose file is elsewhere (change it in the files, or move the file into a folder).
- **Upgrading:** a destination made through the API whose file is outside the folders stops sending after the upgrade, on every node that runs this version; the node logs which one and the Alerts page shows why, until an administrator saves it again with a file inside a folder. Destinations in the config files are unaffected. After upgrading, review the destinations made through the API that have a credential file (the Alerts page, or `GET /api/v1/config/entries?kind=alert_destination`), and look in the audit log for `alert_destination.put` by actors that weren't administrators.
- Mixed versions: an older primary applies a forwarded write without these checks (the node that applies the write decides, so the rules hold once the primary runs this version); a primary that runs it refuses an administrator's credential-file change that an older replica forwards, until that replica is upgraded or the change is made on the primary.
**Verification:** `obs_010_only_an_admin_sets_a_credential_file` and `obs_010_only_an_admin_changes_a_destination_with_a_credential_file` (the real routes, sign-in and backend: operators, an operator's and an admin's agent tokens, dry runs, an address, account or file change, deletes); `obs_010_a_write_says_whether_an_administrator_made_it` (which callers are an administrator); `obs_010_a_forwarded_write_says_whether_an_admin_made_it` (the flag on the wire, and a write from an older node) and `obs_010_a_forwarded_write_from_a_non_admin_is_refused` (the primary's `forward::apply`, dry run or not, nothing stored); `obs_010_an_operator_cannot_override_a_file_destination_with_a_credential_file`; `obs_010_credential_files_must_be_in_a_place_for_secrets` (a table of refused and accepted paths through the route) and `obs_010_a_link_that_leaves_a_secrets_folder_is_refused` (a Secret mount's links, links out to a file, through a folder, dangling); `obs_010_a_stored_destination_outside_the_secrets_folders_is_not_used` (a stored entry isn't read or sent to, says why; the files' destination with the same file still sends), `obs_010_a_replica_does_not_send_an_unchecked_destination` (a replica's Send test: an older primary's API entry and a primary's file entry this node lacks are held; one this node's files define the same way sends), `obs_010_a_promoted_primary_does_not_send_an_unchecked_destination` (through the alert task), `obs_010_an_api_override_of_a_file_destination_is_held`, `obs_010_a_git_commits_destination_counts_as_the_nodes_own`, `obs_010_without_its_files_a_node_holds_every_credential_file` (the start-up fallback, through `server::files_at_start`), and `obs_010_refused_are_destinations_not_the_nodes_own_outside_the_secrets_folders` (the dispatcher's list, logged once per configuration, failing closed when the check can't run); `obs_010_refusals_and_the_audit_log_hold_no_file_content`; and the helper's `obs_010_a_credential_file_is_under_a_secrets_folder_by_its_components`, `obs_010_links_are_followed_before_the_folder_is_compared`, `obs_010_a_path_that_cannot_be_looked_at_is_refused` (a real folder of mode 000; it says it skipped when run as root) and `obs_010_a_part_that_answers_permission_denied_is_refused` (the same rule through a seam, whoever runs it).

## ADR-180 — Plugin upstreams are defined in the config file, and a local file named through the API is an administrator's choice (Proposed)
**Context:** an upstream whose URL is `exec://<program>` makes the node start that program (with `args`) as the service user and supervise it; `unix://<socket>` makes it connect to a local socket and exchange DNS messages there (UPS-011, ADR-075). Plugins were documented as a config-file feature, but the API's upstream entries (ADR-069), a forwarded domain's servers (ADR-040) and the pre-save check (T9.12) took them from any caller with the operator role (agents: `config:write:upstreams`, `config:write:forwards`), so the choice of a program to run was open to a role that isn't trusted with the node itself. In the same way a list's `path` (ADR-069) made the node read a file the operator chose on every refresh, and a simulation's candidate (ADR-115, open to viewers) could name list, zone, hosts and TLS files for the node to read; a parse error quotes part of the offending line, an I/O error tells whether the file is there, and those messages reached callers below admin. Found in a code review on 2026-11-19, while checking the fields of ADR-179.
**Decision:**
- **The rule.** A setting a caller supplies through the API that makes the server start a program or connect to a local socket is a configuration-file matter: whoever writes the config files, the Helm values or the Git source controls the node already.
- **Writes.** Every API write refuses an upstream whose `url` scheme is `exec` or `unix` (in any letter case), or that has `args`, and a forwarded domain with such a server: 422 `invalid_config` naming the field, with the hint "Plugin upstreams (`exec://`, `unix://`) are defined in the config file." This holds for every caller, an administrator's session and admin token included: a program start isn't something a browser session, an API token or an agent should be able to cause, and the feature is a config-file one. A dry run is refused the same way. The check runs where the write is planned (`plan_override`, `forward_of`), on the node that applies it, so a write a replica forwards is checked by the primary whatever the replica runs. Overriding a config-file plugin upstream through the API is refused as well: the files are where it's changed.
- **Checks.** `POST /api/v1/checks/upstream` (**Test it**) refuses such a draft the same way, before anything is built: no program is started, not even for the few seconds of a check, and no socket is reached.
- **Stored entries.** An upstream made through the API that names a program or a socket and is already in `state.db` (stored by an older version, restored from a backup, or written by hand) is left out when the API's entries are merged over the files (`managed::merge`), so nothing is started for it. A forwarded domain made through the API keeps its other servers and loses those; the servers it keeps keep their upstream names (`forward:<domain>#<n>` is numbered by a server's place among the domain's servers), and the names the left-out servers had count as left-out upstreams; with none left it is left out, and its names go where they would without it (the `default` group, or a route that matches them). Nothing is deleted. What named a left-out upstream is adjusted so the merged configuration stays valid and every other entry made through the API stays in effect (the merge validates whenever it did with the plugin entries in it; the only references to an upstream or an upstream group are a group's members, a client group's `upstreams` and a route's group, and the routes made through the API are the forwards' own):
  - an upstream group made through the API loses it as a member, unless the files define an upstream of that name, which is then the one in effect (a left-out override never hides the files' entry);
  - such a group left with no member is left out too (an override of the files' group gives the files' back); a `parallel` one left with fewer members than it races races them all, or with one member uses `failover` (the closest to what it did);
  - a client group made through the API that sent its queries to a group left out uses the files' choice when it overrides a files' group, otherwise the default; its other settings stay.
  - The node logs one warning for each of these, naming it, when it starts, not at every rebuild. Removing a left-out entry (`DELETE /api/v1/upstreams/{name}`, `/api/v1/forwards/{domain}`) works as for any entry and takes it out of the upstream groups that still name it.
  - A new write that names what is left out (an upstream group's member, a client group's `upstreams`) is refused with 422 `invalid_config` naming it and the plugin hint, rather than stored and adjusted (`api_backend::left_out_guard`, from `managed::left_out_names`).
- **Every route that can reach such a URL:** `PUT`/`DELETE /api/v1/upstreams/{name}` and `/api/v1/forwards/{domain}` with their dry runs, `POST /api/v1/checks/upstream`, a replica's forwarded write, and MCP and agent plans (their tools call those routes with the agent's own credentials). No other kind becomes an upstream URL: upstream groups, client groups and Kubernetes map rules name groups, a stub zone's servers are IP addresses, quick rules and records hold none, there is no bulk or import route, and `POST /api/v1/cluster/promote` republishes the last version applied. `POST /api/v1/simulate` and a dry run's simulation build the candidate's upstreams on a thread without an async runtime, where a plugin upstream starts no program and opens no connection, as configuration checks have always built them.
- REQ: UPS-011, API-002, in `managed::plugin_field`, `managed::without_plugins`, `api_backend::plugin_refused` and `checks::upstream`.
- **A list's local file.** A list made through the API that reads a local file (`path`) is pointed at a file only by an administrator: an admin's own sign-in (a session or HTTP Basic) or an admin-scoped API token, never an agent token (as in ADR-179). A write by anyone else whose `path` differs from the file the list in effect reads (or that gives one to a list that read none) is refused with 403 `forbidden` naming the field, hint "Setting or changing a list's local file needs an administrator", dry runs alike, before anything else in the body is checked. Everyone with the operator role keeps URL lists, inline rules, a file list's other settings while it reads the same file, and deleting any list. The check runs where the write is planned (`local_file_guard` in `plan_override`), so forwarded writes are checked by the primary with the caller's `by_admin`.
- **No folder rule at use time.** The documented place for such a file is anywhere an administrator keeps it (`/etc/telltale/allow.txt` in the docs, a Pi-hole import's `file://` lists), so no allowed folder fits, and the merged configuration keeps no record of who made an entry: a list stored before the upgrade keeps reading its file. What reading it went wrong with is what a caller below admin no longer sees (next).
- **Errors below admin.** `GET /api/v1/lists` shows a failing list read from a local file with a generic reason to a caller below admin, and the whole message to an administrator (the node's log has it, as before); URL and inline lists are unchanged. The health reason (`GET /api/v1/system/health`) and the `list_failing` alert, which everyone who sees health or gets alerts reads, name such a list without its error (`health::list_failing_summary`). The pre-save upstream check, when a TLS file the draft names (`tls_ca`, `tls_client_cert`, `tls_client_key`) can't be used, and an email destination's test send, when its `tls_ca` can't be used, give a caller below admin a reason that doesn't say what the file holds or why it couldn't be read (the same for a missing file and a malformed one), and an administrator the message; the node logs it. These fields keep the operator role and get no folder rule: their content isn't sent anywhere.
- **Simulations below admin.** A simulation run by someone who isn't an administrator can name only the local files (a list's `path`, a zone's `file`, `[local] hosts_files`, an upstream's TLS files) and plugin upstreams that the configuration in effect names in the same field (`simulate::new_local_name`). Anything else is refused before it's read: `POST /api/v1/simulate` answers 422 naming the field, and a dry run's simulation answers `available: false` with `reason: local_file` while the dry run itself answers as before. A candidate that names only the configuration's own files, as a Git commit usually does, runs as before; so does an administrator's. Peers keep the rule they had (`sim.run` refuses a list file the peer doesn't read) and get only candidates the entry node let through.
- **Fetching and compiling for a simulation follow this node's rules,** for every caller, per list and in total:
  - Per list: the candidate's `[filter]` link-local switch, byte cap, timeout, `compile_memory` and `max_regexes` are replaced by this node's (a candidate's take effect only after a restart, since `[filter]` needs one), and a list's own `max_bytes` is held to what this node allows for that list now (`simulate::with_this_nodes_downloads`, on the entry node and on each peer). A list file is read only up to its cap, as the node's own refresh reads it. An administrator's simulation of a commit that changes them shows the lists as this node would fetch them until it restarts.
  - In total: one simulation reads at most 4 changed lists by URL (or from this node's stored copy) or from a file, and their caps add up to no more than 4 times this node's `max_list_bytes` (`simulate::over_fetch_limit`). 4 is the default `fetch_concurrency`: a simulation holds no more list text at once than this node's own refresh does with the default settings (each changed list's text is kept until the side snapshot is compiled), and a change made in the UI touches one list, or a few, at a time. Beyond that, `POST /api/v1/simulate` answers 422 naming the limit and a dry run's simulation answers `available: false` with `reason: download_limit`, before anything is fetched; a peer applies the same check with its own configuration.
  - One at a time: the node's one simulation at a time now covers fetching the changed lists (the slot is taken before the first download and kept until the replay ends), so concurrent simulations no longer download in parallel; the others get 409 `simulation_busy` (`reason: busy` for `simulate=auto`), as before.
- The backend learns whether the caller is an administrator from the API layer (`by_admin` on writes, as in ADR-179, and now on `check_upstream`, `alert_test` and `simulate`).
- REQ: FLT-004, OBS-024, OBS-010, API-002.
**Not covered:** the config files, the Helm values and Git (unchanged: a plugin upstream defined there starts its program as before, and a list there reads any file); a user of the host who edits `state.db` (such an entry is left out at the merge, as above); a list stored through the API before the upgrade (it keeps its file; only what its errors show changes); what else `GET /api/v1/lists` says of a file list (size, line and rule counts) and an email destination's `tls_ca` error as its last delivery on the Alerts page (follow-ups); whether a TLS file an operator names in a check or on an email destination is a valid certificate file (one bit: it then builds and the probe's or the server's answer comes back, never what the file holds); entries a replica receives in the cluster's shared configuration. The manifest doesn't say which entries the primary made through the API, so a replica, or a replica promoted after a failover (which builds on the last manifest), can't leave out one that an older primary accepted through the API and published. Upgrade the primary first. On a primary promoted from a replica, such an entry is part of the configuration it was promoted with (`applied.json`), not of its own files or `state.db`, where writes are planned: `DELETE` answers 404 there. An upstream is replaced instead with a plain one of the same name (`PUT /api/v1/upstreams/{name}`), which overrides it in what the node runs and publishes; for a forwarded domain from that base, the same works on its upstreams (`forward:<domain>#<n>`, as `GET /api/v1/upstreams` lists them), since a forward made there doesn't override the base's route.
**Consequences:**
- An administrator can no longer add, change or test a plugin upstream (or a forwarded domain to one) in the UI or the API, or override a config-file plugin upstream there; they define it in the config file.
- **Upgrading:** an upstream made through the API with `exec://` or `unix://` isn't used after the upgrade: its program isn't started, the node logs a warning naming it, the Upstreams page no longer shows it in use (an override's name shows the config files' entry), and the entry stays in `state.db` until it is removed. A forwarded domain keeps its other servers (with none, its names go to the `default` group). The upstream groups and client groups that named such an upstream are adjusted as above and each is named in a warning; everything else made through the API stays in effect. The fix is to define the plugin in the config file.
- Mixed versions: a replica forwards writes to the primary, which decides; an older primary still accepts such a write and publishes it, and replicas can't tell (above). Upgrade the primary first. The same holds for a list's `path`, and an older entry node can still send a viewer's candidate to a peer that runs this version.
- An administrator's agent token, or an administrator working through MCP, can no longer point a list at a local file; an admin's own sign-in or admin API token can. An operator can no longer add a list that reads a local file or point one at another file, and is told a generic reason when a file list or a TLS file fails. A simulation below admin that names a new local file is refused (or, in a dry run, not run).
- A simulation (anyone's, an administrator's included) that changes more than 4 lists read by URL or from a file, or lists whose caps add up to more than 4 times the byte cap, is refused (or, in a dry run, not run); a simulation that fetches lists makes the next one wait for it (409) where both used to download at once.
**Verification:** `ups_011_plugin_upstreams_cant_be_set_through_the_api` (every kind of caller, an admin's session and token included, dry runs; other upstreams still save), `ups_011_a_forward_to_a_plugin_is_refused`, `ups_011_a_check_never_starts_a_plugin` (no start is attempted; the same capture sees the supervisor try a config file's program), `ups_011_stored_plugin_entries_are_left_out_of_the_merge` (an added entry, an override of the files' entry, a forward, the group that named one, one warning each, nothing logged again on a rebuild, a normal override and forward still apply, a config file's plugin is untouched), `ups_011_a_stored_plugin_upstream_can_still_be_removed` and `ups_011_plugin_upstreams_are_known_by_their_scheme`; `flt_004_only_an_admin_names_a_list_file` (operators and agent tokens, dry runs, an admin's session and token, URL and inline lists, the same file kept, another file, deletes), `flt_004_a_local_list_error_is_generic_below_admin`, `api_002_a_tls_file_error_in_a_check_is_generic_below_admin`, `obs_010_a_tls_file_error_in_a_test_send_is_generic_below_admin` (a file that is there and one that isn't answer alike), `obs_024_a_simulation_below_admin_names_no_new_local_file` (zone, hosts, list and TLS files and plugins; the configuration's own files; an administrator's candidate) and `obs_024_a_dry_run_simulation_below_admin_reads_no_new_local_file`; for the adjustments after the upgrade, `ups_011_a_group_emptied_by_a_left_out_plugin_keeps_the_other_api_entries`, `ups_011_a_parallel_group_keeps_working_without_a_left_out_member`, `ups_011_a_client_group_naming_a_left_out_group_keeps_working`, `ups_011_a_forward_keeps_its_servers_that_arent_plugins` and `ups_011_a_forwards_servers_keep_their_names_when_a_plugin_is_left_out` (an API device, list and quick rule stay in effect in each), and `ups_011_a_write_naming_a_left_out_entry_is_refused`; `obs_024_a_simulation_keeps_this_nodes_download_rules` (a link-local address, this node's byte cap, a list's own cap; a viewer and an admin), `obs_024_a_simulation_fetches_within_this_nodes_budget` (too many lists by URL or from a file, too many bytes, refused before a request reaches a loopback server; within the limit as before; the compile limits), `obs_024_a_dry_run_simulation_past_the_fetch_limit_isnt_run` and `obs_024_one_simulation_at_a_time_includes_its_downloads`, and `api_002_a_valid_tls_file_in_a_check_shows_only_that_it_builds` (the one bit left, and no byte of the file); `flt_004_a_failing_local_list_is_named_without_its_error` (the health reason; the alert summary is the same function) and `flt_004_a_list_file_named_like_a_url_is_still_a_local_file` (a file list is told by its `path`, not by how its source reads). No test starts a program: every plugin path names a file that doesn't exist.

## ADR-181 — Routers from the UI: API-managed `[[router]]` entries, keys pasted and saved as private files on the node that took the write (Proposed)
**Context:** T8.2, ADR-080 (community request 2026-11-17; PR #66, contributed by Carlos Talbot). Routers (`[[router]]`, UniFi and OPNsense) could be set only in `telltale.toml`, with each credential in a file created on the host, and a change needed a restart. Users who run TelltaleDNS from the UI (Docker on a NAS) want to add their console there and paste its API key. A router holds a credential and says where it is sent, so who may set one, and which file may be sent where, follow ADR-179. The first version was revised after a review on the PR (any file could be named as a credential, a saved key was reused for a new address, the key in use was replaced before the change applied, files the API hadn't written could be deleted). A review on 2026-11-19 found more: a router could name another router's key file and send it to its own address; a key saved but not applied was removed; key paths were listed to viewers; an administrator's agent token could set keys; old key files stayed on nodes that didn't take the write; and backups were said to hold the keys, which they don't. A second round of review on the branch (2026-11-20) asked for the folders for secrets to count on every node (the owner chose that over re-entering keys after a failover), a pasted key with an unchecked certificate to go only to an address, pin diffs without key paths for those below admin, the audit entry of a change whose client went away, and a keyed hash in the idempotency record. A third round (2026-11-20) asked for pin and unpin answers without key paths below admin, a retry that can't make a change twice, a bound on the changes running at once, the folder rule bound to the files' router where this node's files define one, and wording that says what the code does. A fourth round (2026-11-20) found that binding a regression (a replica with a stale copy of the primary's `[[router]]`, or a pinned controller, stopped polling a mounted key) and moved the check to where the API's entries are merged, so every node is covered.
**Decision (proposed):**
- **Managed like the other named kinds (ADR-069).** `PUT`/`DELETE /api/v1/routers/{name}` with the `[[router]]` fields; an API entry overrides or hides the files' entry of that name; dry run, `If-Match`, idempotency, audit, Keep it in Git, forwarding from replicas; listed by `GET /api/v1/config/entries?kind=router`. No MCP tool: an agent isn't handed a credential to paste.
- **Who.** Only an administrator writes a router, `PUT` or `DELETE`, dry runs too: an admin's own sign-in (a session or HTTP Basic) or an admin API token, never an agent token, as ADR-179's `by_admin`. There is no agent scope for routers. Anyone else gets 403. The check runs where the write is planned, on the node that applies it, before the body's keys are looked at, so a dry run, a replica's forwarded write and an MCP client calling the REST route are covered; a forwarded write from a node that doesn't send `by_admin` is refused.
- **Paste only.** A body may carry `api_key`, `api_secret` or `password`, never a path: `api_key_file`, `api_secret_file` and `password_file` in a body are refused (422, hint "Paste the key; the node saves it") unless the value is the file the router already has (in the config files plus the API's entries, as the write is planned) and its endpoint (`type`, `url`, `site`, `tls_ca`, `tls_server_name`, `tls_insecure_skip_verify`) is unchanged. With a pasted value, the file the body names for that credential is never looked at: the new file replaces it (after a failover the planned router may not have the file the page last saw). The UI sends no `*_file` field at all; a credential the body leaves out keeps the router's own file. A change of endpoint needs each key pasted again. A key in a file elsewhere (a Docker or Kubernetes secret) is named in the config file.
- **Over https.** A pasted key goes only to an `https://` address, or to a loopback one written as an address (127.0.0.0/8, `::1`); `http://` to anything else, `localhost` included, is refused with 422: on `http://` the key can be read on the way, and on a node whose own resolver is TelltaleDNS a local name can change where a host name resolves (ADR-179, Not covered). For the same reason a pasted key with `tls_insecure_skip_verify` goes only to a router whose `url` is an address, not a host name (422, hint "use the address, or tls_ca and tls_server_name"): with the certificate unchecked, nothing else ties the key to the router. A router's `url` holds no `{` or `}`; `site` goes into the request's path only, as one percent-encoded segment. Routers in the config files keep the `http://` and unchecked-certificate rules they had.
- **Where keys live.** Each pasted value is written to a new file, `<data_dir>/secrets/router-<name>.<credential>.<16 hex digits>` (an unsafe or long name made safe and shortened, with a hash), created exclusively with mode 0600 in a folder made 0700, and synced with its folder, after the change is checked (never on a dry run). `secrets/.written` (0600; written to a temporary file, synced, renamed) records each file with its router, credential and endpoint, before the file is made; the temporary file has a fresh random name and is created exclusively. A key is pasted only on a node whose `node.data_dir` is written as a plain absolute path (no `.`, `..` or empty parts), the form the use-time rule and the sweep recognise; otherwise 422. A file is kept once the entry naming it is stored, in the same blocking step as the store (so a runtime that shuts down after the store can't remove it), even if the reload then fails; a change that fails before that removes what it wrote. Every configuration change made through the write path (the `PUT` and `DELETE` routes of the configuration: devices, names, forwards, quick rules and every managed kind) that isn't a dry run runs to its end in a task of its own together with its audit entry and its Idempotency-Key record, so a client that goes away doesn't stop it half way or leave it unaudited. That is the same work in the same order as before; what changes is only that it is no longer cut short where the client left (each change is one bounded unit under `config_writes`). A retry with the same Idempotency-Key gets the first answer once the first request has finished; while the first is still running, the retry gets 409 with `Retry-After` and isn't made a second time (the key is claimed, and the stored answer looked up, in one step). At most 32 such changes run at once; one more gets 503 with `Retry-After`; dry runs aren't counted, and a change gives its place back when its task ends. A change whose task fails (a panic) answers a fixed 500, and the server log says why. Other changes (promotion, users, tokens, blocking, acknowledgements) run and are audited in their request, as before. `state.db`, the configuration, the API's answers, the audit log, the replicated configuration and Keep it in Git hold the path only; the idempotency record holds each pasted value as a BLAKE3 hash keyed with this node's request key, 32 random bytes in `<data_dir>/secrets/.request-key` (0600, made on the first start), which is outside `state.db` and outside backups, so a copy of `state.db` can't be used to test guesses of a key.
- **Use time.** A router in effect that names a key file is polled only if one of these holds for each of its key files: this node's own config files (and, on a node that takes the cluster's configuration from Git, the commit in use) define that router with that file at the same endpoint; or the file lies in a folder a platform fills with secrets, `/run/secrets/` (Docker, Compose) or `/etc/telltale-secrets/` (the Helm chart's `secretMounts`), by ADR-179's folder rule without the data directory (compared by components, as written and with links followed, never `/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/`, `eks.amazonaws.com/` or `azure/`: `secret_path::in_folders`), on any node; or the file is recorded in this node's `.written` for that router, credential and endpoint and lies in this node's `<data_dir>/secrets/` (the same rule, limited to that folder: `secret_path::in_data_secrets`). The folder counts whatever this node's own files say, so a replica whose own file still holds an older copy of the primary's `[[router]]` (ignored, CLU-006) and a pinned controller whose files changed since the pin keep polling. Otherwise the router isn't polled and keeps no names, and the node logs why once per router and reason (a warning, on a replica too, whose Routers page shows the primary's status; information on a Helm resolver pod, which has no keys by design), with what to do: define the router in this node's config file, or put the key under `/etc/telltale-secrets/` or `/run/secrets/`, or, for a router made in the UI, enter the key again on the node that takes configuration changes. No API body can name a new file in those folders for a router (paste only: a body may only keep the router's own file at the same endpoint), so a router that uses one there came from whoever writes a config file or Git repository a node takes routers from, or from an administrator who kept the files' folder key in an override. **Such an override stays in effect only while the files vouch for it.** Where the API's entries are merged (`managed::merge`, on the node that applies them: a standalone node or the primary, never a node that follows another), an entry made through the API for a router is left out when it names, for some credential, a key file outside this node's `<data_dir>/secrets/` that the files' router of the same name doesn't name for that credential at the same endpoint; a router the files don't define counts as not matching. On a primary promoted after a failover, the files are the configuration it was promoted with (the last version it applied, `applied.json`, under its own node settings). So **an API router entry may name only a file under `<data_dir>/secrets/` or the very file and address the config files give that router.** A file under `<data_dir>/secrets/` passes the merge whatever it is (the test doesn't ask whether the key was pasted): an entry naming one there that this node didn't save for it (an alert's token file, say, or a key file of another router, in an entry restored or edited in `state.db`) is not left out. It stays in the configuration in effect, in place of the files' router of that name, and is published; the use-time rule holds it on every node whose `.written` doesn't record that file for that router, credential and endpoint (the third case above): not polled, logged once as a warning with why, its status `not_used` (`missing` when there is no file). The test is made on the path as written, in normal form and by components, with nothing looked up on the filesystem, so it fails closed: a mounted key that is a link out of the folder on the merging node, or a folder it can't search, can't keep a stale override in (review round 4, final review M2). That covers an override kept from before the files moved or removed the router, whatever folder its key is in, and an entry restored or edited in `state.db`. There are no stored router entries from a release: the `router` kind is new with this change. The node logs one warning per entry left out (as ADR-180 does for plugin entries), saying why and how to fix it (edit the router in the UI to save it for the files' address, or remove the entry); the files' router, if any, is the one in effect, and the configuration the primary publishes never carries the left-out entry, so no replica, and no primary promoted after a failover, sees it. Routers are named by nothing else in the configuration, so the merge stays valid. A key pasted in the UI (in `<data_dir>/secrets/`) is unaffected: its `.written` record vouches for it. The rule holds what this node can't vouch for: an entry stored by an earlier build, restored from a backup, edited in `state.db`, or replicated from another node, whose key file is outside the two folders or in this node's `secrets/` without its record. An entry made through the API that names a file in the two folders is left out by the merge unless the files give that router that file at that address (above); a router in the shared configuration that names one is polled on every node. If the check can't run, no router with a key file is polled.
- **Status.** `GET /api/v1/config/entries` gives each router a `credentials` status per key, as the node that answered sees it (on a replica, the primary, or the replica itself while the primary can't be reached): `saved`, `missing` (no file there, and it is one the node saves when a key is pasted: enter it again), `file_missing` (no file there, and it is one a platform mounts or a config file names: put the file there) or `not_used` (there, but not one that node sends for it). It is read without the secret (whether a file is there, and the rule above), and the UI shows "key saved", "key missing: enter it again", "key file missing: put the file there, or paste the key" or "key not used: enter it again". What a replica itself does is in its log (above). The Routers editor shows a warning when the page itself was opened over plain HTTP from another machine ("You are on a plain-HTTP page: a key you paste crosses the network unencrypted. Open this page over https first."); the server can't tell, behind a proxy, so it is a notice only.
- **What others see.** A router's key-file paths and `username` are shown to an administrator only; viewers, operators, agent tokens and MCP resource reads get the definition without them, and the status. The redaction is made where the answer leaves the node, so a replica's copy of the primary's entries is redacted too. The same fields read "(hidden)" in a pinned cluster's diff for those callers: in `GET /api/v1/cluster/rollout` (a viewer's read), in the `409 cluster_pinned` a write gets, and in the answer of a pin or an unpin (an agent's with `cluster:admin`, and so in an MCP plan's preview, which every operator reads through `GET /api/v1/plans`). Those diffs showed them before this change for routers in the config files too.
- **Removal, on each node.** A node removes a file recorded in its `.written` once no router names it, and only a file it makes: a direct child of its `<data_dir>/secrets/` named `router-<name>.<credential>.<16 hex digits>` (the name made safe), a regular file (not a link), lying in that folder with links followed. A record for any other name is kept, and its file left alone, with a warning (once); if a stored router entry can't be read (a row that isn't a JSON object), it removes no key file at all (it can't tell what is named) and logs a warning at each try, which doesn't name the row (Consequences, known limit). It removes a file not in the configuration in effect, not in its config files, not in its own stored entries (the last two keep a key while a reload has fallen back to the config files, the cluster is pinned, or a demoted primary may be promoted again). It also removes the temporary files a crash left behind (`.written.<16 hex digits>.tmp`, which hold paths only, and `.request-key.<16 hex digits>.tmp`, which is either a key never put in place or, after a crash between linking the key in place and removing the temporary name, a second name for the key in use, harmless in the 0700 folder; regular files only, once ten minutes old, whether or not the stored entries can be read). It sweeps at start, once the state database is open (the router task starts before the API opens it; until then the sweep is tried again every 5 seconds without polling anything again), after every change of the routers in effect and after a router write. Apart from those temporary files (`.written.<16 hex digits>.tmp` and `.request-key.<16 hex digits>.tmp`, regular files at least ten minutes old), it never removes a file that isn't on that list, nor anything but a name it makes in `secrets/` itself; each removal is logged at info.
- **Backups and snapshots.** `telltale backup` never includes `secrets/`: it copies a fixed list (the config files, `state.db`, `rollups.db`, `anomaly.json`, the query log on request). After a restore on another machine a router made in the UI shows "key missing" until its key is entered again. A copy of the data directory, a volume snapshot or a Kubernetes PVC snapshot does carry the keys in plaintext: keep the folder and files at their modes (0700 and 0600), keep such copies private, and encrypt the volume or the snapshot storage.
- **Failover.** Keys pasted in the UI stay on the node that took the write, the primary. The router is shared configuration, so every node has its entry, but only that node has the files. After a failover the new primary has the router and not its key: it shows "key missing" and doesn't poll it until an administrator enters the key again there. While the node that has the key runs, the names it reads reach every node (ADR-125). A router whose key is in `/run/secrets/` or `/etc/telltale-secrets/` on every node is polled by whichever node is primary, with no re-entry. A replicated, encrypted secret store with write-only references would remove the re-entry step for pasted keys; it is a follow-up.
- **No restart.** The router task compares the routers in effect every 5 seconds between its rounds of reads: a router added or changed is read within 5 seconds after the reads in progress end (a read of a slow router is bounded by its request timeouts), and a removed router's names go after the next round. A change to a router in the config files applies the same way after a reload (`SIGHUP`). A router's device names are dropped when it is removed or points at another device (type, address, site).
- REQ: T8.2, API-002, in `router_secrets.rs` (`prepare`, `UseCheck`, `sweep`), `api_backend::router_guard`, `routers::run`, and `config_entries`'s redaction (`telltale-api`).
**Not covered:** a user of the host who can write the data directory (they can read or replace the files, `.written` and the request key; and with a data directory placed under `/run/secrets/` or `/etc/telltale-secrets/`, every file in it counts as a mounted one, so a router entry edited into `state.db` there could make the node send, say, its `secrets/.request-key` to the entry's address: keep the data directory outside those folders); an administrator (trusted with the node's secrets already, and still able to set a router's address and paste a key for it); routers in the config files, the Helm values or Git, which keep any path (whoever writes those controls the node), apart from the use-time rule's view of them on a node whose own files don't define them, and which may still send a key with `tls_insecure_skip_verify` to a host name; keys on more than one node (none are copied); what a node receives in the cluster's shared configuration: the manifest doesn't say which routers the primary made through the API, so a replica, or a primary promoted after a failover, holds every router with a key file that its own files (or the Git commit it uses) don't define and that isn't in `/run/secrets/` or `/etc/telltale-secrets/`, one from the primary's config files included, unless the key was saved on that node for it. Who can make a node send a key from those two folders, and where: whoever writes the primary's config files or its Git repository (ADR-179). A page of the UI opened over plain HTTP from another machine: the key crosses the network as typed (the editor says so; serving the UI over https is the operator's).
**Consequences:**
- Routers can be set up from Clients → Discovered → Routers, UniFi and OPNsense, without touching the host.
- An administrator can no longer write routers with an agent token, name a key file through the API (only send back the router's own at the same endpoint), or paste a key for an `http://` address other than a loopback one.
- **The cost of the use-time rule:** a node that follows another (a native replica, a primary promoted after a failover) polls a router that only the primary's config files define when its key files are in `/run/secrets/` or `/etc/telltale-secrets/` there, as before this change; with a key file anywhere else (`/etc/telltale/opn-secret`, say) it no longer polls it, logs why, and says what to do. Defining the router the same way in that node's own config file keeps it polled there, but on a replica the configuration file's `[[router]]` section is then also reported by CLU-006's warning ("this node's configuration file sets shared settings that the cluster's primary replaces; they're ignored"): the shared routers still come from the primary, and the file's copy only vouches for the key file; moving the key under `/etc/telltale-secrets/` is the cleaner way. With the Helm chart every pod mounts the same ConfigMap and the controller mounts `secretMounts`, so nothing changes; resolver pods have no `secretMounts` and log the failed read as info, as before (ADR-120).
- **Native clusters:** router key files outside `/etc/telltale-secrets/` or `/run/secrets/` are no longer read on replicas or on a primary promoted after a failover, unless that node's own config file (or the Git commit it uses) defines the same router with the same key file and address, or the key was pasted in the UI on that node (release-note line). A key in those folders is read on every node, whatever the node's own file says.
- **Upgrading:** upgrade every node before writing routers through the API. An older primary doesn't know the `router` kind and refuses a replica's forwarded router write ("bad write"), and an older replica passes the primary's router entries to its viewers without the redaction. A router saved with the PR's build before this change (its `.written` lines hold paths only) shows "key not used" until its key is entered again. Two smaller changes for routers in the config files: `site` is now percent-encoded as one path segment (a site ID with `/`, `@` or `%` is sent escaped), and `{site}` written inside `url` is no longer replaced (a `url` from the API may not hold `{` or `}`). An idempotency record written before this change holds an unkeyed hash, so a retry of such a router write with the same key gets `409` instead of a replay; records are short-lived.
- The task wakes every 5 seconds to compare a short list, and sweeps when the list changes: no cost worth measuring, and nothing on the DNS path.
- **Follow-up: label left-out overrides on the page.** A left-out entry is told only by the log's warning. When the files moved the router, the Routers page lists the files' router, still labelled "overrides the file"; when they removed it, the entry has no row at all, and the only remedy is `DELETE /api/v1/routers/{name}`, which the warning names. If the files later bring the router back with the same file and address, the old override applies again, with its own settings (interval, offline days).
- **Known limit: a sweep that can't run doesn't name the row that stops it.** A stored router entry that isn't a JSON object (a hand edit of `state.db` can make one; the API stores objects only) stops the removal of key files on that node: each try (at start, after each change of the routers in effect, after each router write) logs "router keys: the sweep of key files no router names didn't run" with "the entries made through the API couldn't be read", without saying which entry. Key files no router names then stay (0600, in the 0700 folder) until the row is fixed or removed; the old temporary files still go. Naming the row is a follow-up.
- **Follow-up: test nodes left running after a CI timeout.** The tests that start a node as a subprocess (`tests/startup_signals.rs`, like the other subprocess tests) stop it when the test ends, a failed one included, but a test process killed outright (a CI job's timeout) can't, and its node keeps running on its loopback ports (one started to join, which isn't ephemeral, gives up after two minutes and runs standalone). On the self-hosted build VM it stays until it is stopped by hand. Stopping leftover test nodes before a run is a follow-up.
