**Verification:** `api_002_only_an_admin_writes_a_router` (operator, viewer, an operator's agent token with every write area, an admin's agent token holding the scope an earlier build gave routers; PUT, DELETE, dry runs; an admin's session and token may) and `api_002_a_forwarded_router_write_needs_an_admin` (`forward::apply`, with and without `by_admin`), `api_002_agents_never_write_routers`; `api_002_router_cannot_name_another_routers_key` and `api_002_router_credential_paths_are_refused` (`..`, absolute, relative, a link inside `secrets/`, a file put in `secrets/`, another router's file, for each key field; the router's own file at a new address), `api_002_a_pasted_key_goes_only_over_https`, with the unit tests `api_002_pasted_router_secrets_become_new_files` and `api_002_router_bodies_name_no_key_file`; `api_002_a_stored_router_naming_another_routers_key_is_not_polled` (the router task against a loopback console: another router's key with another site, a file outside `secrets/`; the files' router, a stored override keeping its file and endpoint, and a pasted key are polled), `api_002_a_key_file_is_used_only_for_the_router_it_was_saved_for`, `api_002_a_router_key_is_in_the_data_directorys_secrets_folder`, `api_002_a_held_router_is_logged_once_per_reason`; `api_002_a_router_whose_key_file_is_gone_says_key_missing`; `api_002_router_key_kept_when_saved_but_not_applied` and `api_002_router_key_kept_when_the_client_goes_away`; `api_002_router_key_files_are_private_recorded_and_undone`; `api_002_orphaned_router_key_files_removed` and `api_002_a_key_its_own_stored_entry_names_is_kept`; `api_002_router_definitions_redacted_for_viewers` (by kind and not, an MCP `resources/read`); `api_002_keep_in_git_names_a_secret_mount_for_a_router_key`; `api_007_a_backup_never_holds_router_keys`; `deploy/routers-ui-e2e.sh` and `deploy/routers-e2e.sh` (CI); the Playwright test `api_002 routers are added and changed in the UI`. Second round: `api_002_a_shared_router_with_its_key_in_a_secrets_folder_is_polled_on_any_node` (a replica polls with the key under the mount folder and holds it with the hint elsewhere; a promoted primary the same), `api_002_a_pasted_key_replaces_a_file_the_planned_router_lacks`, `api_002_the_start_sweep_waits_for_the_state_database`, `api_002_an_unchecked_certificate_needs_an_address_for_a_pasted_key`, `api_002_a_pin_diff_names_no_router_key_file` (`telltale-api`), `api_002_router_key_kept_when_the_client_goes_away` (now with the audit entry and the replay), `api_002_a_pasted_key_is_kept_for_a_retry_only_as_a_keyed_hash`, `api_002_the_request_key_is_private_and_kept_across_restarts`, `api_002_the_request_key_is_made_whole_or_not_at_all`, `api_002_a_router_write_replays_for_its_idempotency_key`, `api_002_a_routers_site_stays_in_the_path`, `api_002_a_key_is_pasted_only_into_a_normal_data_directory`, `api_002_a_pasted_value_never_shows_in_debug_output`, `api_002_keep_in_git_uses_a_safe_folder_name`, `api_002_the_sweep_never_follows_a_link_out`, `api_002_the_use_rule_without_files_and_with_a_git_commit`, and the Playwright test `api_002 the plain-HTTP notice is for a page reached over the network`. Third round: `api_002_a_pin_command_diff_names_no_router_key_file_below_admin`, `api_002_configuration_changes_running_at_once_are_bounded` and `api_002_a_change_whose_task_fails_answers_a_fixed_500` (`telltale-api`); `api_002_a_retry_while_the_first_change_runs_is_not_made_twice`, `api_002_a_backend_write_whose_caller_goes_away_runs_to_its_end`, `api_002_the_router_task_sweeps_after_the_routers_change`, `api_002_a_router_without_its_key_asks_for_the_key_to_be_pasted`, `api_002_a_missing_mounted_key_says_put_the_file_there`, `api_002_the_sweep_removes_old_temporary_record_files`, `obs_010_cloud_identity_tokens_are_no_credential_files`, `obs_010_an_alert_destination_names_no_router_key`; the replica in `api_002_a_shared_router_with_its_key_in_a_secrets_folder_is_polled_on_any_node` now logs a warning. Fourth round: `api_002_a_stale_override_with_a_mounted_key_is_left_out_of_the_shared_configuration` (matching kept and published; moved: the files' router in effect and shared, a replica built from it has only that, one warning; removed: neither), `api_002_an_override_of_a_removed_files_router_is_held` (read while it matches, never asked once the files remove the router), `api_002_a_replica_with_a_stale_own_copy_still_polls_a_folder_key`, `api_002_a_backend_write_whose_caller_goes_away_runs_to_its_end` (now waiting on the configuration lock), `api_002_the_sweep_removes_old_temporary_request_key_files`, `obs_010_feature_files_are_refused_in_any_case`, `agt_007_a_busy_apply_leaves_the_plan_to_apply_again` (`telltale-api`), and, for the start-up signals, `ops_007_a_stop_during_start_up_drains_and_exits_cleanly` and `ops_009_a_reload_during_start_up_reloads` (`tests/startup_signals.rs`). Fifth round: `api_002_a_stale_override_is_left_out_when_the_primary_cannot_resolve_the_key` (a mounted key that is a link out of the folder, and a key outside every folder), `api_002_the_sweep_removes_only_names_it_makes` (forged records kept; an unreadable stored row stops the sweep), `api_002_the_sweep_knows_every_name_it_makes`, `api_002_a_held_router_of_this_nodes_files_is_told_about_the_folder`, and in `tests/startup_signals.rs` `ops_007_a_stop_during_a_cluster_join_ends_the_node`, `ops_007_ctrl_c_during_a_cluster_join_ends_the_node` and `ops_009_a_reload_during_a_cluster_join_keeps_the_node` (a join to a loopback port where nothing listens holds start-up open).

## ADR-182 — Upgrades driven from the cluster: the node asks, a root update service installs, `ctl upgrade` orders the nodes, and the chart's hook runs it first (Proposed)
**Context:** a community request and pull request (PR #72, Greg Pakes, 2026-11-19), changed on top before it was merged. A cluster with a controller in Kubernetes and native nodes outside it (a Proxmox container, a Pi) is upgraded by hand in three steps: `self-update` on each native node, replicas first, then the chart bump. GitOps automates the last step and nothing does the first two, so a merged Renovate pull request upgrades the controller **before** its replicas, against the order `spec/12` §9 and ADR-059 ask for (`spec/12` says "Helm upgrades handle this via hooks", and the chart has none). Three things stood in the way. ADR-046 says the UI never updates a node, because replacing the binary needs root and the native unit runs as `telltale` under `ProtectSystem=strict` (ADR-038). `self-update` installs the newest build of a channel and can't be pinned, so it can't match a chart's version. And edge binaries are replaced on every build, so only the newest one can be downloaded. This is the project's first component that runs as root, and the first way for the cluster to reach one, so the boundary is stated exactly.
**Decision:**
- **The boundary.** The node asks; root verifies the signed index, binds the version to the checksum, installs only a build newer than the installed one and on the node's channel, rolls back a build that doesn't come up healthy, and records the result. Nothing else comes from the request: it holds an id, a version, a display name and a time, and no URL, mirror, key, channel, argument or environment. What root needs beyond that (the node's channel, where the health check asks) is in its own root-owned unit, written by `install.sh`. ADR-046's reason stands: the node's own service still never replaces its binary.
- **The channel is a setting, not the version string.** `install.sh` writes `Environment=TELLTALE_UPDATE_CHANNEL=edge|stable` into the node's unit and the update service's: `edge` with `--edge`, else the channel of the version it installed (it says which). A node on the stable channel takes only releases; a node on the edge channel takes edge builds and releases newer than its version, and **stays on edge** after a release (the next edge build is still taken). The node (the API and the RPC), `ctl`'s pre-flight and the update service all apply the same rule (`update_api::channel_takes`); without the variable (an install from before it) the version's channel is used, as before. A request never moves a stable node to edge builds.
- **The request.** Asked for a version, a native node writes `<data_dir>/update/request.json` (in a folder of its own it makes, 0750) and nothing else, and answers with the request as it wrote it (its id is what `ctl` follows; never read back from the file, which the update service may have taken already). One request at a time per node: while one waits for the update service, or the last run says `installing` and is under 16 minutes old (the unit's limit), another gets `update_in_progress` (409) and the first is never overwritten (the file is created, never renamed over). A dry run makes the same checks and writes and removes nothing. A version whose new build was **installed** on the node and didn't come up (`rolled_back`, or `failed` after the swap: the previous binary couldn't be put back) is refused there for 24 hours from that run, as the node recorded it (`update_failed_recently`, 409, at the node and again by the update service, which keeps that run through later ones; the node's state shows it as `held`), so a hook that retries doesn't install and restart the node over and over. A run that failed before anything was installed (a network error, the downloads' deadline, a signature or checksum, an edge build moved on, a refused request) holds nothing back: one passing GitHub or DNS outage must not block a version, and every sync of the hook, for a day. The refusal names the run, when it ended, what failed, and the two ways out: `self-update --to` by hand on the node, or wait until the time it gives.
- **The root units.** `install.sh` installs `telltale-update.path` (`PathExists=` on that file) and `telltale-update.service` (oneshot) with the node's data directory written in (from the config's effective `[node] data_dir`; one that isn't a plain absolute path gets no units), the channel, and `Environment=TELLTALE_UPDATE_HEALTH_URL=` (below), and leaves them **off**: `install.sh --cluster-updates` or `systemctl enable --now telltale-update.path` turns them on. `--cluster-updates` is refused, before anything changes on an existing install, when the binary's folder or any folder above it isn't root's alone (owned by root, not writable by group or others), when the data directory is under `/home` or `/root`, or when the data directory's parent isn't root's alone (an older Debian or Raspberry Pi OS image whose `/usr/local` is `root:staff 2775` is refused until `chmod g-w /usr/local /usr/local/bin`, and the message says so), when the API is off (`[api] enabled = false`: nothing could prove a new build came up), and on a node that runs an edge build unless `--edge` (stay on edge) or `--stable` (move to releases) is given (a re-run without either would install the newest release, an older version); and it turns the path unit on only once the node answers 200 at the health check's address (it prints the address; with `--no-start` it turns it on with a warning to check it). These are environment lines, not command-line flags: `install.sh` fetches the units from `main` when it doesn't run from a checkout, and an older binary would refuse an unknown flag. The service has one capability (`CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE`, to read and remove the request in the node user's folder), `NoNewPrivileges`, and the node unit's sandbox (`ProtectSystem=strict`, `SystemCallFilter=@system-service`, `ProtectProc=invisible`, `MemoryDenyWriteExecute`, `PrivateTmp`, `PrivateDevices`, `RestrictAddressFamilies=AF_INET AF_INET6 AF_UNIX`, and the rest), and `ReadWritePaths=` for the binary's directory, `<data_dir>/update/` and its own `StateDirectory=telltale-update`. `systemd-analyze security` rates it 2.0 (the node's own unit 1.6; the PR's first version 5.2). Its lines go to its journal. **The sandbox is defence in depth, not the proof that root writes only `update/`:** that folder sits in a folder the node's user owns, which systemd resolves when it builds the unit's namespace, so a node that swaps it for a link could widen what the unit may write. What keeps root's writes to fixed paths is the code (it writes only the binary's directory, its own state, and removes the request through the folder it opened without following a link). A root-owned request spool outside the data directory is the proper fix and a follow-up.
- **What the update service does, in order** (`telltale self-update --request`, the installed binary run as root):
  1. Opens the request's folder (`O_DIRECTORY|O_NOFOLLOW`: a real directory, not world-writable, owned by the data directory's owner), then the request in it (`O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK|O_NOCTTY`), and checks the opened file: a regular file, one link, at most 4 KiB, owned by the folder's owner. It reads it and removes whatever is at the name (a link, a FIFO, an empty directory; a directory with something in it is left: root never reaches into the node's files). One generic error goes to the world-readable result for anything that isn't a request.
  2. Refuses (recorded, exit 0): JSON of another shape or with an unknown field, a malformed id or `by`, a request more than an hour old or from the future, a version not written exactly as a release or edge build (padding, a newline or a leading zero included), a local build installed, an older version, an edge build on a node set to the stable channel, and a version whose build was installed there and didn't come up in the last 24 hours (by the node's record of that run).
  3. Refuses (recorded) when the host isn't set up for it: the installed binary, its folder or any folder above it isn't root's alone (`install location is not root-owned`), or `TELLTALE_UPDATE_HEALTH_URL` isn't a plain `http://` URL of `/readyz` at an IP address and port of this host (loopback, or an address a socket can be bound to; no name, no user, no other path). Checked again before the previous binary is put back.
  4. Records `installing`; fetches the release's `SHA256SUMS` and its signature and verifies them with the release key built into the binary (no runtime override), then the signed `releases.json`: it must be of the version's channel, name exactly that version, and list the same SHA-256 for this binary's architecture. An edge build is fetched only while it is the newest on the edge channel; otherwise the run is `unavailable`. **One deadline, 480 s, for all the downloads:** past it the run is `failed` and nothing is installed (the old binary stays).
  5. Fetches the binary (at most 128 MiB, its SHA-256 checked), test-runs `--version` (killed after 10 s), keeps the installed one as `telltale.old`, and renames the new one in.
  6. Restarts the node (`systemctl restart telltale`, by its absolute path, waited for 60 s: twice the node unit's `TimeoutStopSec=30`, so the old process is gone by then). The command's own outcome is no verdict on the build: if it outlasts the wait (a long drain, a stop systemd ends with SIGKILL; the restart goes on in systemd) or exits non-zero, the run goes on to the health check, which decides, and `health` notes it. Then it waits up to **120 s** for the node's readiness for a start (`/readyz?startup=1`: maintenance not counted) to pass twice in a row, 2 s apart, at `TELLTALE_UPDATE_HEALTH_URL`: the node's `[api] listen` address and port as `install.sh` found them (a wildcard bind asked at loopback; a unit without the variable asks `http://127.0.0.1:8053`). Then `updated`, with `health: "checked at <url>"`. With the API off (`[api] enabled = false`) there is nothing to check, so the feature isn't available: `install.sh --cluster-updates` refuses, and a unit whose variable is empty makes the update service refuse the run before anything is fetched (`refused: no health check configured`). A node that needs longer than 120 s to come up (very large lists on an SD card) is rolled back.
  7. If it isn't healthy, puts `telltale.old` back (renamed back, after the location check again), clears the node unit's start limit (`systemctl reset-failed`, 10 s), restarts once more and checks again: `rolled_back`, with the reason. If the previous build's own check fails too, the run is still `rolled_back` (the previous build is back in place), with a note that the check may be what's wrong (the address, or `[access] allowed_networks` leaving this host out). Only when the previous binary can't be put back: `failed`. One attempt, no loop.
  The whole run takes at most 884 s (the downloads, the test run, two restarts and health windows, the reset, and the pauses of the tries a program that is "text file busy" is given), under the unit's `TimeoutStartSec=960` with a minute to spare, and `ctl upgrade --timeout` (15 minutes by default) is at least that (`node_update::run_budget_secs`, checked by a test), so systemd never kills a run between the install and its check. The result, `/var/lib/telltale-update/result.json` (written by temporary file and rename in root's own directory), is what the node reads (`GET …/update`): `by` in it is the node's word for who asked (`byVerified: false`), and the journal says so ("as written by the node (unverified)").
- **`rolled_back` is an incident signal, not proof the build was bad.** The node's own process answers `/readyz`, and any local user can bind the loopback port first, so the unprivileged side can make a healthy build look unhealthy (it can't make root install anything else: the rollback only puts back the binary root set aside). `ctl` stops at `rolled_back`; look at the node before asking again (it refuses the same version for a day anyway). A compromised node can do this again each day to keep a fixed version off: a `rolled_back` of a security release is a sign of compromise, to be resolved on the node by hand (`self-update --to`).
- **Who can ask.** An admin (`POST /api/v1/nodes/{id}/update {"to"}`); an agent API token with `cluster:upgrade` (admin level, in no default set, made by an admin; like every agent token it must say why with `X-Telltale-Reason`, which `ctl upgrade` sends); a cluster member the registry lists that isn't ephemeral, over the RPC `update` (as for maintenance: the entry node sends the call to the node it names). An ephemeral member (a resolver pod), a peer the registry doesn't list, and an OAuth access token for MCP can't: the RPC gives an ephemeral or unlisted peer nothing, not even a read of the update state (which names who asked and the errors), and the scope is neither advertised in `scopes_supported` nor taken from an identity provider's token. The node checks again: its install type and update service (`update_unsupported`, `update_not_enabled`), the version (`update_invalid`, `update_downgrade`), the channel (`update_channel`), a version whose build was installed there and didn't come up in the last day (`update_failed_recently`), and the lock (`update_in_progress`). `?dryRun=true` makes every check and writes nothing (AGT-002). Audited on the entry node (`node.update.request`, and `node.update.refused` for a refused admin or agent attempt) and on the target node, which names the sending node and keeps the user it states as unverified (a peer's name is cut to 200 characters before " via <node> (unverified)" is added, so the marker always shows); a request refused at the RPC gate (an ephemeral or unlisted peer, a stale call) is audited there too. It changes no configuration, so a GitOps-managed cluster takes it too.
- **An exception to "an agent token never counts as an administrator" (ADR-179, ADR-180, ADR-181).** `cluster:upgrade` is an agent scope of admin level so that the chart's hook can hold a narrow token instead of an admin's API token in a Kubernetes Secret, which could do anything an admin can. The scope reaches this one route and nothing else; the hook's token also needs `analytics:read` (to read the cluster), which reads every analytics route.
- **Pinned installs.** `self-update --to <version>` installs that version only: a release from `releases/download/v<version>`, an edge build from the edge release while its signed index names it; in both cases the index must be of the version's channel, name the version and list the same SHA-256 as `SHA256SUMS`. By hand, `--to` may go back; a request never does.
- **The order: `telltale ctl upgrade`.** `--to <version>` or `--to-self`. From `GET /cluster`: pods and ephemeral members are the chart's, witnesses only vote and are upgraded by hand, nodes on the version, newer, or on a local build are left alone, and a member never heard from is down. Then a pre-flight: `GET …/update` for every planned node before any is asked; a container is left alone; a node that is up but can't take the request (its update service off, a request waiting or an update running, on the stable channel and asked for an edge build, or a version it refuses for a day) stops the run with nothing asked, the error naming each and why; a channel mismatch says what the node follows, what was asked, and how to move it. `--skip-down` leaves out only nodes that are down (a node set up wrong is not down). Then **replicas first (by site, then ID), the primary last**, one at a time, each done only when its update service has recorded `updated` for **this** request (after the health gate) **and** the cluster view shows it on the version, up, ready and with no configuration lag (`--timeout` per node, 15 minutes by default: at least the update service's longest run); a rollback inside the gate stops the run before the next node is asked. The first failure (`failed`, `refused`, `rolled_back`) stops it. A node that reports the edge build `unavailable` stops it too, unless `--skip-unavailable`: then that node is left on its version with a loud line, and the run goes on (the order is lost for that node in that run, and the line says so). Every request carries `X-Telltale-Reason` (`--reason`, default `telltale ctl upgrade --to <version>`). `--dry-run` prints the order and what would stop it; `--node` limits it; `--skip-if-unreachable` succeeds, with a loud line, only for a first install: the API's name doesn't resolve or nothing listens at its address. A timeout, a TLS failure, packets a policy drops, or an HTTP error (401, 403) fail the run.
- **The chart's hook.** `upgradeHook.enabled` (off by default) renders a Job with `helm.sh/hook: pre-upgrade` (Argo CD runs it as PreSync, on every sync) from the chart's own image, running `telltale ctl upgrade --to-self --skip-if-unreachable` against the release's API Service with an agent token from `upgradeHook.tokenSecret` (`optional: true`: a missing Secret fails the run at once with "set TELLTALE_TOKEN" instead of a pod waiting for it until `activeDeadlineSeconds`); `upgradeHook.whenUnavailable: fail|skip` (default `fail`) passes `--skip-unavailable` for `skip`; `upgradeHook.caSecret` (`name`, `key`) mounts the certificate an `https://` URL with a self-signed certificate is checked against (`--ca-file`). The Job is deleted only before the next one (`before-hook-creation`), so a run's log, a skip's warning included, can be read until the next sync. With `--to-self`, pin `image.tag` to a build (`sha-…` or a version): the chart's default tag `edge` with `IfNotPresent` can run an older cached `:edge` image. A NetworkPolicy with `apiFrom` gets a rule for the hook's pod only while the hook is on. Helm waits for a hook only `--timeout` (5 minutes by default): use `--timeout 45m` (the Job's `activeDeadlineSeconds` is 2700: two native nodes at 15 minutes each, the pre-flight and the pod's start; raise both for more nodes).
**GitOps and Argo CD, case by case** (what the code does):
- Hook off: nothing changes; the chart's pods roll as before, and native nodes are upgraded by hand.
- Hook on, pinned to a release: the first sync after the bump asks each native node behind it for that release, replicas first, then the pods roll. Later syncs find every node on it (or newer) and ask nothing: no request, no download. An edge node (set with `install.sh --edge`) stays on edge and takes the next edge pin.
- Pinned to an edge build that is the newest: the same, for that build. A node on the stable channel stops the run at the pre-flight, said in full.
- Pinned to an edge build main has since passed, with a native node behind it: that node's update service records `unavailable` (only the newest edge build can be fetched). With `whenUnavailable: fail` the hook fails and the sync stays blocked until the pin moves to the newest edge build or a release; with `skip` the node is left on its version, loudly, and the pods roll. Nodes already on the pin or newer are never asked, so a stale pin blocks only while a node is behind it.
- A native node down: the run stops before anything is asked (the pods keep their version) unless `skipDown`.
- The controller up but not ready: on some CNIs (Cilium) a Service with no ready endpoints drops the connection instead of refusing it, so the hook fails rather than skips, and the sync waits until the controller answers (intended). With the hook on and the controller dead, turn the hook off in the chart to roll a fix. For the first 90 s after the node that answers starts, a native node that hasn't connected since isn't listed at all (the view lists the registry's missing members only after giving them that long to reconnect), so a hook run then can pass without it.
- A native node whose new build was rolled back (or couldn't be rolled back): every later sync stops at it for 24 hours from that run (`update_failed_recently`, the node's `held`, at the pre-flight) unless the pin moves on. A run that failed in the downloads holds nothing: the next sync tries again.
- The token: it expires (`expires_in_days`), is revoked, or agents are turned off (`[agents] enabled = false`): **every** sync fails from then on (the hook's first read is refused), a values-only `helm upgrade` too. Make it without an expiry for this narrow scope, or rotate it with a reminder.
- The first install: Helm doesn't run `pre-upgrade` on `helm install`; Argo CD maps it to PreSync, which runs before anything of the app exists, so nothing answers (the Service's name doesn't resolve, or nothing listens) and the hook succeeds with a loud line. No cluster exists yet to make the token in: keep the hook off until the token exists, or create the Secret with a placeholder (a missing Secret fails the hook at once); a PreSync hook runs before the app's own resources, so the real Secret is made outside the app (or as an ExternalSecret or SealedSecret ordered before the hook).
- A native primary whose replicas are the chart's pods: the hook upgrades the native nodes first, so it upgrades that primary before its pod replicas; run `ctl upgrade` by hand in that shape.
**Alternatives rejected:**
- **SSH from a hook into each node:** the chart would hold keys to other machines and run a script only one site has.
- **Each node following the primary's version by itself** (a timer that reads it): no SSH or token, but the primary goes first, against `spec/12` §9, and nothing stops at a failure.
- **The node's service replacing its own binary** (write access to `/usr/local/bin`, or a capability): it undoes ADR-038's sandbox for every install, used or not.
- **A polkit rule letting `telltale` start a root unit:** more moving parts than a path unit, and the same trust in the end.
- **Per-commit edge binaries:** a release-workflow change (assets kept per build, an index per commit). Until then an edge build is installable only while it is the newest; `whenUnavailable` says what a GitOps sync does about it.
- **The channel from the version string** (the first version of this ADR): an edge node that took a release then refused every edge build, and the documented first steps moved an edge Pi to stable. **The health address from the request, or root reading the node's config:** the request carries no URL, and the config is the node's to change; `install.sh` writes the address into the root-owned unit instead.
**What each party can make root do (and not):**
- **A compromised `telltale` process** (the node's user): write the request itself (no API, no audit), and so make root restart the node and install a signed build newer than the installed one on the node's channel (on a stable node any newer release; on an edge node the newest edge build, CI signs one on every push to main, or any newer release, after which it stays on edge), at a time it chooses; make a healthy build look unhealthy (it answers `/readyz`), so root puts the previous one back (`rolled_back`, an incident signal); switch the feature off (requests that trip systemd's start limit, or a directory with something in it at the request's name: the path unit fails until `systemctl reset-failed`); make root read a small file it wrote and remove names in `update/`; and possibly widen what the unit's sandbox lets root write (above; the code writes nothing more). It can't get an unsigned, modified or older binary run, a stable node onto edge builds, a URL, key, argument, environment or health address, or a root write the code doesn't make.
- **An admin:** the same through the API, for any native node, and for several at once if it chooses (one at a time per node is enforced; `ctl upgrade`'s order across nodes is the tool's).
- **An admin's agent token with `cluster:upgrade`:** the same, and nothing else an admin can do.
- **A cluster member the registry lists** (a replica or a primary, compromised or not): the same over the RPC, naming any user (recorded as unverified). **An ephemeral member or an unlisted peer:** nothing, not even a read of a node's update state.
- **The hook's token** (`cluster:upgrade` and `analytics:read`): as an agent token, plus the analytics reads. Whoever controls the chart's values or image controls the controller anyway; through the hook they can move native nodes only to signed builds newer than theirs, on their channel (an edge node may be given a release).
**Not covered:** a root user of the host; a `systemctl` that fails without restarting anything (a missing binary, a refusal on the system bus): the old process still answers the health check, so the run is recorded `updated` while the old build runs, and the next request for that version answers `up_to_date` (the installed binary is the new one); comparing the node's `MainPID` before and after the restart is the planned follow-up; the signing pipeline (edge builds are signed in CI on every push to main, and that step runs repository code with the key in its environment); the units `install.sh` fetches from `main` when it doesn't run from a checkout (as it does the node's own unit); a root-owned request spool outside the data directory (the follow-up that makes the sandbox a proof, and lets the data directory be anywhere; until then `--cluster-updates` refuses one under `/home` or `/root` or in a folder that isn't root's); maintenance around the restart (a native node restarts without a maintenance window, so `node_down` alerts can fire: a follow-up, which would also need `ops:maintenance` for the hook); per-commit edge assets; an MCP tool; a button on the Cluster page; the `spec/08` §4 cluster minimum-version check before an install; Idempotency-Key on the request (a follow-up; the lock already makes a retry answer `update_in_progress`); nodes with the API off (refused).
**Consequences:**
- ADR-046's "the UI never self-updates a node" now reads "never replaces the binary itself": the node asks a root service that the operator turned on (amendment pointer in ADR-046). The Settings panel's steps are unchanged.
- **The first upgrade after this lands is by hand** on each native node, on its own channel: a node without the RPC answers "unknown call" (reported as `node_unreachable` with "upgrade it by hand this once"), and an older entry node answers a bare 404 (`ctl` says so). So a Pi on edge builds needs `sudo telltale self-update --channel edge --restart` (a node on releases: `sudo telltale self-update --restart`; or `--to`) to a build with this feature, then `sudo sh install.sh --edge --cluster-updates` (without `--edge` on a node on releases) for the units: without `--edge`, `install.sh` would install the newest release and set the stable channel, so `--cluster-updates` over an edge build refuses until `--edge` or `--stable` is given.
- Mixed versions: nodes with this feature and without it can be in one cluster; `ctl upgrade` stops at one without it (pre-flight), with `--skip-down` too.
- A transient download failure is a plain `failed` with the old build untouched: it holds nothing back, and the next request (the next sync) tries again.
**Verification:** `ops_014_versions_are_checked_and_ordered`, `ops_014_a_node_keeps_the_channel_set_for_it`, `ops_014_update_routes_need_their_scope`, `ops_014_update_requests_over_http`, `ops_014_a_dry_run_reaches_the_node_as_one`, `ops_014_a_refused_update_request_is_audited`, `ops_014_an_oauth_token_never_carries_cluster_upgrade`, `ops_014_a_call_about_a_nodes_own_state_finds_its_node`; `ops_014_a_node_takes_only_newer_versions_it_can_install`, `ops_014_a_request_is_written_for_the_update_service_and_shown_as_pending`, `ops_014_the_answer_names_the_request_written_and_marks_a_peers_by`, `ops_014_a_second_request_waits_for_the_first`, `ops_014_a_dry_run_writes_nothing`, `ops_014_a_dry_run_leaves_a_dead_request_in_place`, `ops_014_an_ephemeral_member_cannot_ask_for_an_update`; `ops_014_the_update_service_installs_a_newer_version_once_and_records_it`, `ops_014_a_failed_install_is_recorded_and_does_not_restart`, `ops_014_the_update_service_trusts_nothing_in_the_request`, `ops_014_the_update_service_reads_no_symlink_or_large_file`, `ops_014_a_link_to_a_fifo_is_refused_without_blocking`, `ops_014_a_fifo_or_directory_at_the_request_is_refused_and_removed`, `ops_014_a_padded_or_unparseable_version_is_refused`, `ops_014_a_request_never_moves_a_stable_node_to_edge`, `ops_014_an_edge_node_that_took_a_release_stays_on_edge`, `ops_014_a_request_folder_that_isnt_the_nodes_is_left_alone`, `ops_014_the_units_watch_and_write_only_the_request_folder`, `ops_014_a_host_not_set_up_for_it_refuses_before_fetching`, `ops_014_the_health_gate_takes_only_a_plain_address_of_this_host`, `ops_014_the_install_location_must_be_roots_alone`, `ops_014_the_run_fits_in_the_units_time_limit`, `ops_014_a_build_that_does_not_come_up_is_rolled_back`, `ops_014_a_slow_or_failed_restart_command_is_left_to_the_health_check`, `ops_014_a_rollback_whose_check_fails_too_is_recorded_as_rolled_back`, `ops_014_a_failed_rollback_is_recorded_and_stops`, `ops_014_a_version_that_failed_is_not_asked_for_again_for_a_day`, `ops_014_a_failure_before_the_install_holds_nothing_back`, `ops_014_an_edge_build_no_longer_served_is_unavailable`; `ops_014_a_pinned_release_is_downloaded_from_its_own_page`, `ops_014_a_pinned_install_takes_only_the_version_its_signed_index_names`, `ops_014_an_edge_build_installs_only_while_it_is_the_newest`, `ops_014_a_pinned_install_takes_only_an_index_of_the_versions_channel`, `ops_014_downloads_that_take_too_long_install_nothing`; `ops_014_upgrade_order`, `ops_014_the_preflight_reads_each_node`, `ops_014_a_node_that_cant_take_the_upgrade_stops_the_run_before_any_is_asked`, `ops_014_ctl_upgrade_goes_one_node_at_a_time_and_stops_on_failure`, `ops_014_ctl_waits_for_updated_and_stops_at_a_late_rollback`, `ops_014_ctl_upgrade_works_with_an_agent_token`, `ops_014_only_a_first_install_is_skipped`, `ops_014_ctl_upgrade_and_an_edge_build_no_longer_served`, `ops_014_nodes_on_the_version_are_not_asked`; `deploy/helm/upgrade-hook-test.sh`, `deploy/systemd/update-units-test.sh`, and the `deploy/systemd/e2e.sh` step "the update service picks a request up" (the units' channel and health address, the sandbox, a refusal, a FIFO, `install.sh --cluster-updates`).

## ADR-183 — Named devices follow their MAC address: the routers' address-to-MAC lists join the neighbor table for identification, under rules that keep one device's rules off another; host names never identify (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #73 (a user's report, 2026-11-20). A quick rule made from the query log was pre-filled with the name the router reported for a TV ("LGwebOSTV"), and a rule can't target that name. The user gave the address instead, and asked for rules that survive address changes: "a hostname or even better a MAC address".

Phase 0 (no ADR) fixed the form and the refusal. What remains is that a device is recognized by MAC only from the kernel's neighbor table (ADR-021). That table sees a device only with host networking on the same layer-2 network: not in a Docker bridge, a Kubernetes pod or another VLAN.

TelltaleDNS serves no DHCP (ADR-091), but the router integrations (ADR-080, ADR-166, ADR-181) already read:
- UniFi's connected clients;
- OPNsense's DHCP lease rows.

Each gives an IPv4 address and a MAC, and every node shares what its routers report (ADR-125). Until now these were for display only.

ADR-021 deferred host-name matching (FLT-006), and it stays deferred: this ADR doesn't build it, and FLT-006's "hostname" item is not delivered by this change (ADR-021 remains the reference; `spec/01` is not edited). What it delivers is identification by MAC from the routers' lists. ADR-067 says a quick rule's devices may be given "by name, IP, or MAC, as `[[client]]` keys"; the code takes a device name, an IP or a CIDR. API-010 says naming creates a client object with "its IP, and the MAC when known": the dialog now offers the MAC and can store it **without** the IP, which is where this ADR records the difference from API-010's sentence (an IP key would name whoever holds the address next).

A review of the first design (2026-11-20) found four ways a router binding could put one device's rules on another, which are closed below:
1. a lower source filling an address a higher one distrusts;
2. day-long trust windows on the wall clock;
3. older members' unchecked lists;
4. one MAC at many addresses.

**Decision (proposed):**

**Host names never identify a device.** A DHCP host name, a UniFi alias, an mDNS name and a reverse-DNS name name a device for display only. Any device can announce any name, many devices of one model announce the same one, and a UniFi alias arrives in the same field as the DHCP name. This does not build the host-name matching ADR-021 deferred, and doesn't close that deferral: the routers' lists contribute **MAC addresses**, not names, to identification.

**One table, three sources, in this order.**
1. The kernel's neighbor table: what this host saw on its own link.
2. The bindings this node's routers report.
3. Those other full cluster members' routers report.

The chain is otherwise unchanged: client ID → EDNS MAC from a trusted forwarder → **this table** → Kubernetes workload → exact IP → most specific CIDR → network group / `default`.
- A binding identifies only a `[[client]]` that lists that MAC in `match`; it never creates a device.
- Where the table maps an address to a named device's MAC, that device wins over a `[[client]]` keyed by the exact IP, as a kernel entry already does. Where the binding is withheld (below), the exact-IP device wins again.

**A router binding is withheld** when any of these holds:
- **Offline:** the client isn't connected (ADR-166). It gives no binding and withholds nothing.
- **A MAC that can't identify:** a connected client whose MAC is empty, doesn't parse, is all zero or is multicast withholds its address, as an `unsure` one does (a tombstone, below), on this node and, for a member's lease, on every node: otherwise a member's router could fill the address with a named device's MAC (two sites reusing a range). The node says once per router, in the log, that its rows carry such MACs and that the fallback (the device is recognized by its address or network) is by design.
- **`unsure`:** this node marks a binding `unsure` when:
  - its fresh routers, or two rows of one router, give one address different MACs (compared as parsed bytes);
  - they place one MAC at two or more addresses (a range extender or bridge that shares its MAC);
  - UniFi gave a connected client's address from `last_ip` or `fixed_ip` rather than `ip`;
  - the router hasn't answered for **15 minutes**.
- **Tombstone:** an address this node withholds (`unsure`, or a MAC that can't identify) can't be filled by any lower source. The kernel still wins. A member's tombstone doesn't withhold an address this node binds itself (own routers come first); `tombstones` holds only addresses with no binding of this node's, and the count of withheld addresses (`telltale_router_macs_unsure`) is those plus the shared ones, each once.

Silence is counted on a monotonic clock from the node's start (never the wall clock, which can step), with saturating arithmetic. Locally administered ("private") MACs count, and the UI says they may change. Display names keep their rules (within one router the last row still names an address; between routers the order is now fixed).

**Across the cluster.** A node takes other members' bindings from their `devices.names` answers (ADR-125) only when all of these hold:
- the answer is from a **full member**: a node the member registry lists and that is not ephemeral (ADR-143; a resolver pod holds no router keys, ADR-120). Nothing a peer says about itself counts: its Hello's `primary` flag is a claim any node with a valid certificate can make, and the rest of the cluster code refuses it too (a fix round, 2026-11-23, took out a first version of this ADR's exception for "the primary"). A replica's first round can come before its copy of the registry is written; the answer is then not used and the quick retry (below) goes on until the registry lists the answerer, a few seconds;
- the answer carries **`vetted`** (a new field, written by this version: an answer from an older member, whose lists lack these rules, gives no bindings);
- the member answered within the last **15 minutes**.

An answer from a member that is not `vetted` (a member rolled back to an earlier build) also removes that member's bindings kept so far, at once.

Any member's `unsure` lease for an address removes that address. So does a disagreement between two members, and so does one MAC at two addresses across this node's and the members' bindings. The MAC is counted over every binding and every withheld lease, in this node's view and in the members': an address a node withholds, and an address two members disagree about, still count the MAC they gave there, so a MAC-sharing extender can't hide behind an address one node is unsure of. The one exception, and the choice (fix round 3, decision D5): the rows of a router this node has marked stale (silent for 15 minutes) never veto fresh data. They are marked `unsure` as they are, and also `stale`: on the node that reads that router their addresses stay withheld (tombstones), but their MACs are not counted at those addresses, so a device that moved while such a router stayed configured is recognized at its new address, here and on the other nodes. A member's answer carries the mark (`stale`, written only when true, so the wire stays additive: an older node reads the row as `unsure`, as before, and never identifies from it); another node of this version drops a member's stale rows altogether, so they withhold nothing there, neither their address nor their MAC. (Sending a stale router's rows as `offline` on the wire would have the same effect on identification, since a member's offline rows are skipped, but it would also change what the other nodes' display names say about the address (an offline row names only an address no connected row has), and an older node would then take the stale rows for ordinary offline clients; the explicit mark keeps both as they are.)

A node never passes on what it learned from others, so a stale binding lives at most one router-silence window plus one member-silence window: about 30 minutes, plus one exchange. That bound is on the *age of an answer*, not only on silence: a router read once an hour is never silent, yet what it said can be an hour old. So while a device is named by its MAC and `router_macs` is on, a node reads each of its routers at least every 10 minutes (600 s) whatever the router's `interval_secs` says (also when such a device appears during a longer wait): a read that fails is asked again every 15 seconds, well inside the 15 minutes after which an answer is stale. With no device named by its MAC the pace is `interval_secs`. Two nodes that read one router (the high-availability setup) poll out of phase, so a device that has just moved can be withheld on both until both have polled: one MAC then sits at two addresses in their combined view.

**Who hears whom.** A node asks only the members it has a direct stream to, and a member's answer carries only what its own routers said. Replicas dial the primary; the voters link to each other only under automatic failover (`failover = auto` with three or more voters: the default is `manual`, and then a replica has a stream to the primary alone); a resolver pod connects to the primary alone. So a router read by a replica reaches the primary, and, under automatic failover, the other voters, not the resolver pods: a pod gets the primary's own router view. A replica that refuses a configuration (invalid files, a newer schema) never writes the member list, so it keeps no member's bindings: that replica is degraded anyway. The router's key therefore belongs on the primary, and, for a failover, on the node that would be promoted. Relaying members' bindings to pods would lengthen the age bound (ephemeral members' own answers are never used), and is left for later.

The quick retry: a node asks every 5 seconds for at most 2 minutes, then once a minute as before, until an answer is *kept* (from a full member, `vetted`) and carries router leases: the empty answer of a full replica without the router's key, the primary's own answer before its first read of its router, and an answer that is not used (a registry that has not arrived, an ephemeral or unlisted node) don't end it. A cluster with no router anywhere pays about 24 small calls in those two minutes.

`Lease.unsure` and `Names.vetted` are written only when true: a node on an earlier build ignores both and reads the same form. ADR-125's display names are unchanged.

**Where it runs.** One task on every node, off the query path.
- It reads the kernel table every `neighbor_refresh_secs` while `[clients] neighbor_table` is on.
- It adds router and member bindings only while some `[[client]]` matches by MAC.
- It rebuilds when an input changes (at once when the first MAC-keyed device appears), in a blocking task.
- The table is swapped atomically, and the previous one is freed on a background thread after 2 seconds (ADR-023's `retire`).

On the query path: the same one atomic load and one hash lookup as before, and only while a device matches by MAC; a 7-byte entry instead of 6, and one branch on its source. No allocation, lock or I/O, and `QuickRules::decide` is untouched.

**The setting.** `[clients] router_macs`: default `true`; with `false`, only the kernel table and EDNS MACs.
- It is shared configuration, because identity must be the same on every node.
- It **is not written while it is the default**, so a node on an earlier build (the section refuses unknown fields) still reads the shared configuration.
- Set to `false`, it makes an older replica refuse each new version with `shared configuration: unknown field …` (it keeps serving its last one), and an older build won't start with it in its own file. So the opt-out can be used only once every node runs this version.
- It applies on reload, without a restart.

**Rules and devices.** No new scope kind, and no change to what a rule targets: a quick rule's `devices` takes a `[[client]]` name (any case), an IP or a CIDR, as before. A rule that should follow a device names a device recognized by MAC (`[[client]] match = ["aa:bb:cc:dd:ee:ff"]`). Validation reads the configuration alone.

**Naming in the UI and for agents.** `GET /api/v1/clients/{address}/identity` (MCP `identify_device`) gains `recognition`:
- `device`: the configured device the address is recognized as now;
- `identifiedBy`: how;
- `mac`, `macFrom` (`neighbor`, `router` or `peer`), `macShared`: the MAC a device there could be named by;
- `macRandomized`: the MAC is locally administered. It is kept for every caller who may read identities, with `device` and `identifiedBy`, including one who may not read MACs: one bit (the address has a made-up MAC), which names no device and no MAC.

How it is built:
- It is the answering node's own view (set after the federated merge).
- It is computed in the request from the table and the same withholding rules, with no kernel read in the request.
- It is left out at query-log privacy level 2 and above.
- `mac`, `macFrom` and `macShared` are given only to a caller who may read `/api/v1/clients` (which already shows MAC keys): signed-in users and API tokens of any role, and agent tokens with `config:read` not restricted to a group. Others get `recognition` without them. `config:read` is a default agent scope, so a default agent token reads the MAC of any address through `identify_device` and explain, while `/api/v1/dhcp/leases` stays closed to agents: kept on purpose, since the naming flow needs the MAC, and said in `spec/13` and the agent docs.
- Explain follows the same rule for `client.mac` (unless the caller gave it) and its new `macFrom`, and, like `recognition`, shows neither at query-log privacy level 2 and above (a MAC the caller gave is theirs and comes back). It reports `identifiedBy: router_mac`.
- A MAC the kernel's neighbor table holds at two or more addresses (a MAC-translating extender) is `macShared` too, counted in the request over this node's table, off the query path: the kernel table has no shared-MAC rule for identification, and the dialog must not offer that MAC as one device's.

"Name this device…":
- It edits the device the address is recognized as (or the one that already lists the MAC) instead of making a second one.
- It offers **its MAC address**, as the default only when the MAC comes from a router (every node can use it) and isn't shared; otherwise it offers the MAC and says why.
- With the MAC, it stores the MAC without the address: an address key would name whoever gets that address next.

**Observability.**
- `telltale_neighbors` counts the kernel's entries.
- `telltale_router_macs{source="router"|"peer"}` counts the bindings in use.
- `telltale_router_macs_unsure` counts the ones withheld.
- The log says when router bindings start and stop being used, and, once per spell and after a minute, when a device is named by its MAC and `router_macs` is on but no router or member binding reaches this node (whatever the kernel's table holds); on a resolver pod it says that only the primary's own router view reaches it, so the key belongs on the primary. It warns when a router with `tls_insecure_skip_verify` starts feeding identification.

REQ: FLT-006, FLT-005, API-010, CLU-002, CLU-010, OBS-005, AGT-004. Implemented in:
- `telltale_policy::clients` (`MacTable`, `MacFrom`, `IdSource::RouterMac`);
- `devices.rs` (`eligible`, `router_bindings`, the members' bindings, `vetted`);
- `routers.rs` (`rebuild`, `unsure`);
- `server.rs` (`spawn_mac_table`);
- `explain.rs`, `api_backend::recognize`, `federated::identity`, the identity and explain handlers (`telltale-api`);
- `ClientChip.svelte`.

**Alternatives rejected:**
- **A host-name scope or host-name matching:** names are client-chosen and often shared, and node-local names would make validation or matching differ between nodes.
- **A MAC in a rule's `devices`:** it would need the MAC in `decide()` and a schema key an earlier node refuses.
- **Router bindings as a second identification step:** a second lookup per query.
- **Storing the address with the MAC when naming:** the next holder of the address is named and filtered as the device.
- **A node-local switch:** a replica could identify devices differently from the primary.
- **A day-long trust window:** during a controller outage, a revoked API key or a failover, a device that took a departed device's address would inherit its rules for most of a day, up to two days through a member. **One hour** is the alternative if router firmware updates should keep MAC-only devices recognized: still well under typical lease times.
- **Bindings from older members:** their lists have none of the withholding rules.
- **One MAC at several addresses kept:** a MAC-sharing extender would give one device's rules to every device behind it.

**Not covered:**
- MAC spoofing and forged DHCP client addresses: a device that copies a named device's MAC is that device, as with the kernel table.
- A compromised or misconfigured router or full member, which can bind any address to any MAC.
- IPv6 bindings from routers: a MAC-only device's IPv6 queries are recognized only through the kernel's NDP table.
- OPNsense lease states: rows are taken as the API returns them, and conflicts are caught by `unsure`.
- A per-router or per-site choice of which routers identify. In a cluster spanning sites that reuse one private range, a member at another site can bind a local address, and the protection also ends when a site's router goes silent: its stale rows are dropped on the other nodes, so another site's fresh binding for the same address fills it there (the node that reads the silent router still withholds those addresses).
- A MAC that the kernel's table holds at several IPv6 addresses (and one IPv4 address) is one device, not a shared MAC: the dialog's "shared" counts IPv4 addresses only (decision D6), as the routers' bindings are IPv4 only.
- A replica that refuses a configuration (invalid files, a newer schema) never writes the member list, so it keeps no member's bindings: it is degraded anyway, and its log says it.
- An address reused within one router interval (300 s by default) before the next poll: the new holder gets the old holder's rules until the router's next answer (and a minute more on members).
- **Resolver pods and a replica's router.** A node asks only the members it has a direct stream to, and a member's answer carries only what its own routers said, so a router read by a replica reaches the primary and the voters but not the resolver pods, which connect to the primary alone: a pod recognizes a MAC-only device only through the primary's own router view. The router's key belongs on the primary (and on the node a failover would promote). Relaying members' vetted bindings to pods, with their age, is a follow-up: it would lengthen the age bound to about 45 minutes, so it needs an age stamp and an owner decision.
- Recognition by MAC in change simulations and rule-impact estimates (they identify by address, ADR-115), in anomaly mutes (a device needs an address to be muted, ADR-161), on the Clients page's device guess and in identity or rhythm reads by name, and in `telltale explain` on the command line (no routers there).
- Sites beyond about 7,000 connected clients, where the `devices.names` answer outgrows the cluster channel's 1 MiB frame.
- Source NAT in front of a resolver pod, rootless Docker, Podman and Docker Desktop: the address seen isn't the device's.
- The kernel's neighbor entries in the STALE state (the host last saw the device a while ago) still beat a fresh, sure router binding for the address, and are not part of the shared-MAC count: kernel behaviour is unchanged, as the kernel's view always won.

**Consequences:**
- **What users get.** A device named by its MAC keeps its name, groups and device rules when its address changes, within one router interval plus up to a minute on the other nodes. This works with host networking, rootful Docker, Kubernetes with preserved sources (the chart's `externalTrafficPolicy: Local`), and across VLANs.
- **Behaviour change at the default** (release note). Where a `[[client]]` matches by MAC (by hand, or from the Pi-hole importer's MAC clients) and a router reports that MAC at an address the kernel doesn't hold, the device now gets that `[[client]]`'s identity instead of IP, CIDR, network group or `default`. It arrives with the first upgraded node that reads a router. `[clients] router_macs = false` keeps the old behaviour, but only once every node is upgraded.
- **When a binding is missing or withheld:** at start-up, more than 15 minutes into a router or member silence, on an older member's answer, or when the binding is shared or `unsure`. A block rule for the device then fails open and an allow rule fails closed, and the device takes its network's or the default group. DNS is answered throughout (CLU-004).
  - A router firmware update longer than 15 minutes moves MAC-only devices to that fallback until the router answers.
  - A device that moved while its old OPNsense lease is still listed isn't recognized by MAC until the old lease goes.
- **HA.** Router-based identity survives a failover only if the router's key is on two full members, the primary and the node that would be promoted (resolver pods hear only the primary): config-file routers with the key in `/etc/telltale-secrets/` or `/run/secrets/` on both. While two nodes read one router, a device that has just moved can be withheld on both until both have polled (they poll out of phase); if one of them loses the router, its old rows stop counting after 15 minutes of silence and withhold nothing on the others. A key pasted in the UI stays on the node that saved it (ADR-181), so after a failover MAC-only devices fall back 15 minutes later, until the key is entered on the new primary.
- **Mixed versions (CLU-010).** A node on an earlier build accepts a MAC-only `[[client]]` (MAC keys were always valid) but recognizes it only through its kernel table. Upgraded nodes take no bindings from an older member. So in a rolling upgrade (replicas first), MAC-only devices are recognized by router data only once the node that reads the router (usually the primary) runs this version. Nothing in the shared configuration changes for older nodes while `router_macs` is at its default.
- **Cost.**
  - Memory: about 50 KB per 1,000 table entries (twice that during a swap).
  - About 11 bytes per binding per full member that reads a router (kept whether or not a device matches by MAC).
  - A neighbor-table read every `neighbor_refresh_secs` on every node with `neighbor_table` on: new for nodes without a MAC-keyed device.
- **Display.** Past queries are named by the device that holds the address now (names resolve when read, API-010).
- **A member that restarts** answers `vetted` with an empty router view until its first poll, which replaces its cached bindings at once: a pod can fall back for about a minute per primary restart. This is the conservative direction (a block rule fails open and an allow rule closed for that long) and is accepted.
- **Amended ADRs.** ADR-067's wording is amended, ADR-021's host-name deferral stays open (pointer there), and ADR-125's exchange feeds identification (pointers in those ADRs).
- **Requirements.** `spec/01` is not edited (decided by the owner): FLT-006's "hostname" item is not delivered by this change; identification by MAC from the routers' lists is delivered by this ADR instead (`spec/10` T27.1 names it). API-010's "IP, and the MAC when known" describes the dialog's old behaviour; the dialog now offers the MAC and can store it without the IP, for the reason above.
- **Not built:** host-name matching (DHCP host names, UniFi aliases, mDNS names, reverse DNS), a MAC in a rule's `devices`, IPv6 or OPNsense-state parsing, a per-router or per-site trust switch, MAC mutes, "seen at" addresses for a device named only by its MAC.

**Verification (planned):**
- **`telltale-policy`:** `flt_006_router_mac_identifies_named_device`, `flt_006_kernel_entry_wins_over_router_and_peer`, `flt_006_router_macs_counted_by_source`, `flt_006_no_mac_clients_never_read_the_table`, `flt_006_router_macs_off_ignores_router_entries`.
- **`telltale-config`:** `flt_006_router_macs_default_on_and_not_written`, `clu_010_router_macs_left_out_of_the_shared_part_at_its_default`.
- **Routers and members:**
  - `flt_006_routers_disagreeing_on_an_address_mark_it_unsure`
  - `flt_006_unifi_client_without_a_current_address_is_unsure`
  - `flt_006_a_router_silent_for_15_minutes_is_unsure`
  - `flt_006_silence_survives_a_clock_step`
  - `flt_006_rebuild_is_deterministic`
  - `flt_006_one_mac_at_two_addresses_does_not_identify`
  - `flt_006_unsure_mark_is_compatible_on_the_wire`
  - `flt_006_offline_and_unsure_leases_never_identify`
  - `clu_002_peer_router_macs_come_only_from_full_members`
  - `clu_002_a_peers_unsure_lease_counts_as_a_disagreement`
  - `clu_002_peers_disagreeing_on_an_address_drop_it`
  - `clu_002_a_silent_member_is_dropped_after_15_minutes`
  - `clu_010_bindings_from_an_older_member_are_not_used`
  - `clu_002_names_are_asked_again_soon_until_a_full_member_answers`
  - fix round 2: `clu_002_a_member_is_full_only_if_the_registry_lists_it_and_it_is_not_ephemeral`, `clu_002_a_peer_claiming_to_be_primary_gets_no_bindings_when_the_registry_lists_it_ephemeral`, `clu_002_a_peer_claiming_to_be_primary_gets_no_bindings_when_the_registry_does_not_list_it`, `clu_002_the_quick_retry_waits_for_an_answer_that_carries_routers`, `clu_002_the_quick_retry_goes_on_until_the_registry_lists_the_answerer`, `clu_002_the_quick_retry_does_not_end_on_the_primarys_empty_answer`, `clu_002_an_unvetted_answer_drops_the_members_bindings`, `flt_006_a_mac_withheld_by_one_member_still_counts_toward_sharing`, `flt_006_a_mac_at_a_disagreed_address_still_counts_toward_sharing`, `flt_006_a_router_read_less_often_than_15_minutes_does_not_identify` (the 600 s cap), `flt_006_no_router_macs_are_said_once_after_a_minute`, `flt_006_the_no_router_macs_notice_says_what_to_do`

- **The table:**

## ADR-184 — The proof of an unsigned delegation is scoped to its parent zone (Proposed)
**Context:** ADR-098's unsigned-zone proof (T10.9) trusts a validated answer to the DS question at a zone apex. rough-ton's pull request #74 showed that under an NSEC3 Opt-Out parent the validation library returns that DS denial as an ordinary secure response, and added the RFC 5155 §8.6 rule (the closest encloser and an Opt-Out span). Review scoped every part of the proof to the delegation's parent zone: only the parent zone's signed records say which of its names are delegations, so the records of another zone, or a delegation's own record taken as a closest encloser (RFC 6840 §4.1), say nothing about this one.

**Decision:** what a DS answer proves is one pure function of it (`ds_denial_verdict`): signed (a secure DS), an unsigned delegation, or not proven. An unsigned delegation at a name is proven only by:
- **The parent's NSEC owned by the name**, in a secure NODATA, listing NS and neither SOA nor DS.
- **NSEC3 records of the parent zone only**, in a secure NODATA: every NSEC3 in the authority section is owned under one zone, a proper ancestor of the name; all validated; one hash algorithm, salt and iteration count, at most 100 iterations (RFC 9276 §3.2); no NSEC beside them; and an SOA in the section, if any, is that zone's. Hashes are compared as bytes (owner labels decoded from base32hex), names as names; each name is hashed once per proof. Then:
  - if an NSEC3 of that zone matches the name's hash, it alone decides: NS, and neither SOA nor DS (ADR-098's cut check);
  - otherwise (RFC 5155 §8.6): the NSEC3 matching the name's parent (the closest encloser) is not a delegation's (neither NS without SOA nor DNAME), and an NSEC3 with the Opt-Out bit covers the name's hash (the next closer name). A closest encloser further up, through an Opt-Out empty non-terminal, isn't accepted: not proven.
- **An answer the library calls insecure**, only through an insecure record owned by an unsigned top-level domain: a one-label name, the name's own top-level domain (or the name itself when it has one label). Every zone with two or more labels above the name is a candidate of the walk, decided by the rules above.
- **A negative answer the library returns as an error with its proof** proves nothing.
- **Proven zones are remembered per upstream group**, for the smallest TTL of the records that proved them (the library lowers a validated record's TTL to what its signature allows), at most ADR-098's 15 minutes.

**What it proves, and what it doesn't:**
- It proves that the parent zone signed a denial at the name: a cut without a DS, or an Opt-Out span below a name of the parent zone that isn't itself a cut. So the name, if it exists, can only be a delegation of the parent zone without a DS. Or it shows that the name lies below an unsigned top-level domain.
- It doesn't prove that the delegation exists: an Opt-Out span doesn't say which delegations it covers (RFC 5155 §6), so a name inside a real span can be presented as an unsigned delegation. That is inherent to Opt-Out.
- It can't tell a record from before a change in the zone (a span from before a delegation was signed) from a current one while that record's signature is valid: inherent to DNSSEC.

**Consequences:**
- `validate` proves unsigned zones under `.com`, `.net`, `.org` and other NSEC3 Opt-Out parents (ADR-098's 2026-11-21 correction).
- A parent zone whose NSEC3 records use more than 100 iterations can't prove its unsigned child zones: in `validate` mode their names are answered SERVFAIL (in `permissive` mode they are served and counted as bogus).
- The proof makes at most three NSEC3 hashes per DS answer (the name for the cut check; the name and its parent for the Opt-Out rule), each of at most 101 SHA-1 rounds, and only after a bogus verdict, an error or a timeout: off the DNS hot path.
- A name queried while the resolver hasn't cached its parent zone's keys yet can fail for up to a minute, and works again after that minute (issue #77's follow-ups).
- Tested offline only: answers built by hand for each condition, and a signed test top-level domain with NSEC3 Opt-Out (no salt, no extra iterations) and signed and unsigned delegations below it, served by a fake upstream.
- Follow-up, not part of this decision: RFC 5155 §9.2 (a DS answer proven only by an Opt-Out span shouldn't get AD; today it does).
- Note (2026-11-27, hickory-net 0.26.4, pull request #106): the validation library now accepts the Opt-Out proof for the next closer name (RFC 5155 §8.6) itself, and judges the answers of names below parked domains (whose servers answer an SOA for every name) insecure on its own. This decision's proof is unchanged and is reached less often; it still decides the answers the library judges bogus. The offline proof tests use `old.u.t.`, an unsigned zone's address with a signature left over and no key, which the library still judges bogus.

**Verification:** `dns_011_opt_out_accepts_parent_apex_and_span`, `dns_011_opt_out_rejects_delegation_nsec3_as_encloser`, `dns_011_opt_out_requires_one_parent_zone`, `dns_011_opt_out_rejected_when_apex_has_own_nsec3`, `dns_011_opt_out_closest_encloser_above_parent_is_not_proof`, `dns_011_cut_nsec3_must_be_in_parent_zone`, `dns_011_insecure_denial_needs_unsigned_tld`, `dns_011_nsec3_iteration_cap_is_not_proof`, `dns_011_ds_denial_returned_as_error_is_not_proof`, `dns_011_proof_hashes_each_name_once`, `dns_011_proven_zone_is_remembered_for_its_proof_ttl`, `dns_011_hash_range_covers`, `dns_011_nsec3_hash_rfc5155_vectors`, `dns_011_ds_denial_by_nsec3_opt_out`; offline: `dns_011_offline_opt_out_unsigned_delegation_is_insecure`, `dns_011_offline_cname_chain_across_opt_out_unsigned_zones_is_insecure`, `dns_011_offline_signed_delegation_stays_validated`, `dns_011_offline_hickory_opt_out_ds_denial_is_secure`, `dns_011_offline_insecure_zone_proof_is_per_group_and_ttl_bounded`.

## ADR-186 — A sweep of private reverse names is its own anomaly kind, `reverse_sweep`, muted on its own; the NXDOMAIN storm is judged on the other names (Proposed)

**Context:**
- **The report.** In GitHub issue #90, from Michael Roughton, an Unraid host and an LG TV reverse-resolve every address of their private /24 about once an hour. That is 254 lookups of `x.20.0.10.in-addr.arpa` in a minute, 247 NXDOMAIN, 94 % of the device's queries, so each sweep raises an `nxdomain_storm` (ADR-073: at least 30 NXDOMAINs that are at least 50 % of the minute's queries).
- **The workaround falls short.** Issue #38's answer, a per-device mute (ADR-161), works, but it also hides a real storm from that device. `ignore_domains` can't reach the storm count or name a reverse zone.
- **What the reporter suggested.** Leave private reverse names out of the count. ADR-161 already rejected that for this pattern, together with a higher threshold and `[exclusions]`: "a device sweeping the same network would go unseen, which is the case the storm check exists for".
- **The owner's decision (2026-11-23, said on the issue):** split the signal. A sweep of private reverse names becomes its own finding kind, judged and muted on its own, and the storm is judged on the other names.
- **Two facts shape the design:**
  - The telemetry crate already has an allocation-free check for RFC 6303 private reverse names (`internal::is_private_reverse`, the list ADR-152 and `[special] private_ptr_nxdomain` use).
  - The mute table's kind mask is a `u8` that seven kinds already use.

**Decision (proposed):**
- **What is a private reverse name.** It is what `telltale_telemetry::internal::is_private_reverse` says, made `pub(crate)`. Its ranges are unchanged, and it now matches the zone by labels, as the resolver's copy does (a label that only ends in the bytes of `in-addr` or `ip6` isn't under the zone):
  - IPv4: `10/8`, `172.16/12`, `192.168/16`, `100.64/10`, `169.254/16`, `127/8`, `0/8`.
  - IPv6: `fc00::/7`, `fe80::/10`, `::1`.
  - It decides by name, whatever answered it, as for ADR-152.
  - The resolver's copy in `telltale-policy/src/special.rs` stays where it is (the telemetry crate doesn't depend on it). A test in `crates/telltale` keeps the two copies' answers equal over the range edges, upper case, and labels that only end like the zone.
  - Reverse names of public ranges, IPv6 global addresses included, stay in the storm.
- **Counting.** Beside the storm's per-device minute counters, the engine counts:
  - the minute's private reverse lookups (`rev_total`),
  - those answered NXDOMAIN (`rev_nx`),
  - a 256-bit map of the names answered NXDOMAIN (FNV-1a, a bit per name, popcount as a lower bound on distinct names; names that resolve set no bit),
  - up to 5 sample names.
  - Other NXDOMAIN names keep their own samples.
  - The work runs on the aggregator thread, at most once per event. There is no allocation beyond the existing 5 samples per minute, and the query path is untouched.
- **The rules, at the minute's close:**
  - **The minute is a sweep** when `rev_nx ≥ nxdomain_per_minute`, `rev_nx ≥ nxdomain_percent` % of `rev_total`, and the map has at least 16 bits set (`SWEEP_MIN_NAMES`): at least 16 different names answered NXDOMAIN.
  - **A sweep minute's private reverse lookups leave both the storm's count and its total**, and the storm is judged on the rest. In any other minute everything counts toward the storm, as before. So each lookup is judged by one detector, and padding a storm with a sweep can't dilute it.
  - **The carve-out depends only on the minute being a sweep.** It doesn't depend on whether the sweep is reported this hour, or on mutes. A second sweep in the hour, or a muted one, never becomes a storm.
  - **Each kind is reported at most once per device per hour**, on separate clocks.
  - **The finding:**
    - `kind = reverse_sweep`, `domain` absent, window 60 s.
    - `observed` = NXDOMAINs, `baseline` = private reverse lookups, `spread` 0, `threshold` = `nxdomain_per_minute`.
    - The detail gives the numbers, "at least N different names", and samples.
- **No new setting.** The sweep reuses `nxdomain_per_minute` (30) and `nxdomain_percent` (50).
  - Both reported sweeps (#90: 247 of 254; #38: about 225) clear them by 7-8×.
  - Every minute that raised a storm before raises a sweep, a storm, or both. The exception is a repeat sweep in an hour that already reported one, which raises nothing (the second rule above).
  - The 16-name floor sits below what a real sweep at the 30-answer bar has, and above a DNS-SD browse (4 or 5 names) or a loop on a few names. Such a loop stays a storm, as before, also beside private names that resolve (they set no bit).
- **Counted, not informational.** `when = "anomaly"`, the badge, `telltale_anomalies_total{kind}` and the chart's `TelltaleDNSDeviceAnomaly` take it as they take storms. There is no new alert kind.
- **Mutes.**
  - `reverse_sweep` is a mute kind like the others, per device and per group. `AnomalyKind` and `Kind` get it as their last variant, so existing ones keep their order.
  - The mask becomes `u16`, with a compile-time check of at most 16 kinds.
  - **An existing mute that lists `nxdomain_storm` keeps meaning exactly that** (ADR-161: "an explicit list keeps a new kind visible"), so it no longer covers the sweep.
    - Instead, a counted sweep that such a mute covers for `nxdomain_storm` records that mute's name (`stormMute`). The lookup is made once, when the finding is created.
    - The Anomalies page tells an operator what changed, offers **Mute for this device…** with the sweep ticked, and offers **Remove** for the old mute while it still lists only the storm.
    - The release notes say the same.
- **Surfaces.**
  - **API:** `GET /api/v1/analytics/anomalies` gets the kind and an optional `stormMute` (additive). The mute `PUT` accepts the kind.
  - **MCP:** `find_anomalies` describes sweeps, and `plan_set_anomaly_mute` lists the kind.
  - **Metrics:** gain the label. The support package shows only counts by kind.
  - **UI:** the title is "Sweep of local addresses", and the card says "This device looked up the names of many addresses on your network. Network scanners do this, and so do some TVs and NAS boxes, on their own, often once an hour."
  - No new route, scope, listener, file or network call.
- **Privacy:** unchanged. The engine is off at query-log privacy level 1 and above. Findings are readable where storms are, `analytics:read` for agents and not by display sessions. The sample names are the ones a storm already shows.

**Alternatives rejected:**
- **Leaving private reverse names out of the storm** (the reporter's suggestion), `nxdomain_ignore_suffixes`, `ignore_domains` applied to the storm, or `[exclusions]`. Each hides local reconnaissance from every device.
- **A higher threshold.** It weakens the check for every device and is per node.
- **Always taking private reverse names out of the storm.** A loop on one private name would then raise nothing.
- **Classifying by how the name was answered** (`Special::PrivatePtr`). That would tie detection to `[special]` and routes.
- **An informational kind** like `chatty_loop`. It would drop sweeps from every user's alerts by default.
- **Letting an `nxdomain_storm` mute also cover `reverse_sweep`.**
  - The entry would say one thing and do another, and an agent reading it couldn't tell.
  - "Storms only" couldn't be said.
  - The reporter's problem would stay unchanged, with nothing to prompt the switch.
- **Rewriting mutes on upgrade.**
  - Files and Git can't be rewritten.
  - Writing the new kind into the shared configuration while older nodes run would make them refuse it.
  - It would be a silent change.
- **A separate threshold key now.** No reported case needs it.
- **Down-converting for older replicas, or a version gate on the write.** ADR-059 defers per-field gating. The documented upgrade order already makes an older primary refuse the kind.

**Consequences:**
- **For users who muted `nxdomain_storm` to quiet a sweep (#38, #90):** after the upgrade their sweeps count again, and the mute covers only real storms. They need to switch the mute's kind. The page and the notes say how.
- **Mixed versions:**
  - An older node lists a newer node's finding under the raw kind (the API carries kinds as strings), and an older primary alerts on it.
  - During a rollout one sweep can show twice, as `nxdomain_storm` from an older node and `reverse_sweep` from a newer one (the kind is part of the finding ID).
  - **A shared mute naming `reverse_sweep`:**
    - It makes an older replica refuse each new version (CLU-010). The replica keeps serving the last version it took, so DNS is unaffected (CLU-004). Until it is upgraded it logs `cluster configuration not applied (will retry)` with `unknown variant reverse_sweep` every 2 s, and its `sync_errors` check and `telltale_cluster_sync_error` show the same. It doesn't say "upgrade this node": that message is only for a newer `SCHEMA` or an unknown section, and this is a new value inside a known one.
    - An older build won't start with it in its own file.
    - So name it only once every node runs this version (as with `[clients] router_macs`, ADR-183).
    - Nothing writes the value unless a mute names it. `SCHEMA` and `PROTOCOL` are unchanged.
- **Rollback:**
  - `anomaly.json` gains optional fields only. An older version reads it until a `reverse_sweep` finding is recorded; after that it can't parse the state, and devices learn again (ADR-043's rule for unreadable state).
  - A mute naming the kind made through the API is skipped by an older version with a warning. One in a file must be removed first.
- **Memory:** about 64 bytes more per device, inside the 4 KiB budget, and about 64 KiB at 1,024 devices.
- **Detail:** ADR-073's storm rule now reads "of the device's queries other than a sweep's". The golden fixture is unchanged (it has no NXDOMAINs).
- **Not covered:**
  - sweeps slower than 30 NXDOMAINs a minute;
  - sweeps of dense networks, where most addresses have PTR records and so get few NXDOMAINs;
  - sweeps of public ranges, IPv6 global addresses included;
  - a sweep cut evenly across a minute boundary.
  - These go unseen as before. A distinct-names-per-hour rule or a user-listed range is a follow-up if asked.

**Verification:**
- **Engine** (`telltale-telemetry`): `obs_009_a_sweep_of_254_private_reverse_names_is_a_reverse_sweep_not_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_storm_of_other_names_is_still_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_storm_padded_with_a_private_reverse_sweep_is_still_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_loop_on_one_private_reverse_name_stays_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_loop_on_one_private_name_beside_resolved_neighbours_stays_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_sweep_of_a_public_range_stays_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_partial_sweep_is_a_sweep_only_over_the_bar`, `obs_009_private_ipv6_reverse_names_make_a_sweep`, `obs_009_a_second_sweep_in_the_hour_is_neither_reported_nor_a_storm`, `obs_009_a_sweep_and_a_storm_in_one_hour_are_each_reported`, `obs_009_sweep_replay_is_deterministic`, `obs_013_state_saved_before_the_split_still_loads`, `obs_013_a_sweep_of_a_whole_16_keeps_the_state_bounded`, `obs_038_muting_reverse_sweep_keeps_nxdomain_storm_live`, `obs_038_an_nxdomain_storm_mute_leaves_a_sweep_counted_and_names_itself_on_it`, `obs_038_every_kind_is_muted_by_itself_alone`; `obs_009_counting_a_sweep_allocates_nothing_per_query` and `obs_009_a_repeated_sample_name_allocates_nothing` (`tests/alloc.rs`), with `obs_009_a_kept_sample_is_recognised_for_any_name`; the golden file is unchanged.
- **Config and cluster:** `obs_038_a_mute_may_name_reverse_sweep`, `clu_010_the_shared_part_names_reverse_sweep_only_when_a_mute_does`.
