- **Cross-crate:** `obs_009_the_sweep_and_the_resolver_agree_on_private_reverse_names` (the two private-reverse checks agree on every range edge), `obs_038_config_and_engine_kinds_agree`, `agt_005_the_mute_tool_offers_every_finding_kind`, `obs_014_merged_findings_keep_the_storm_mute`, `obs_013_the_anomaly_rule_alerts_on_a_reverse_sweep`.
- **Playwright:** `obs_038 a sweep is muted on its own and a storm-only mute is pointed out`.
- **Unchanged:** the hot-path allocation tests, and `make bench-smoke` against a same-binary control.
- **Sample names:** `obs_009_sweep_sample_names_cut_at_char_boundary` (the sweep's and the storm's samples are cut at a character boundary), and `obs_009_observe_query_takes_any_wire_bytes`, which covers both kinds of samples.

## ADR-187 — `resolver.arpa` is answered here: NOERROR with no records, never forwarded unless a route for it, or a name below it, covers it (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #77 (airart320): with forwarders, `_dns.resolver.arpa` (the name a client asks to discover an encrypted resolver, RFC 9462) was forwarded, and the upstream answered it from its own data (two unsigned SVCB records for its own endpoints, in the reporter's capture). The signed `arpa.` zone denies `resolver.arpa`, so that answer can never validate: in `validate` mode it was SERVFAIL, in `permissive` mode it was served and counted as bogus. The unsigned-zone proof (ADR-098, ADR-184) rightly doesn't rescue it: the forwarder's own `resolver.arpa` data is not a secure denial by the parent. RFC 9462 §6.4 says a resolver MUST treat `resolver.arpa` as a locally served zone (RFC 6303); §4 says a resolver with no designated resolvers SHOULD answer NODATA; §6.1 says a forwarder SHOULD NOT forward it, and MAY when its operator expects that. `spec/03` §3 step 4 plans DDR answers "when configured" (P1, deferred in `spec/10`); what to answer when it isn't configured is a gap, recorded here. The behaviour has been a known issue in every release's notes since v0.2.0.

**Decision:**
- `resolver.arpa` and every name below it (`_dns.resolver.arpa`, and the apex itself) are a locally served zone, a special name beside the private reverse names (ADR-014): NOERROR with no records, no AD, never cached, validated or forwarded. It is decided where the private reverse names are, at the end of the pipeline's own steps: local records and authoritative zones answer first; quick rules, lists, schedules, the group's rewrites and safe search still apply. A rewrite whose target is in it (a group's `rewrite`, a list's `$dnsrewrite` CNAME) is answered as its target is: here unless a route for `resolver.arpa` covers it (the CNAME and no records; HTTPS and SVCB queries get no data, as for any rewrite).
- For a name in it asked directly, the explanation and the change simulation make the pipeline's decision: answered here, or forwarded by a route for it. A rewrite into it they report as a rewrite, as they do for any rewrite target and for private reverse names. Explain says the target "is resolved like any name" (outcome `resolved`), and the simulation counts the query as a rewrite (answered).
- It is forwarded only by a `[[route]]` whose `match_suffix` is `resolver.arpa` or a name below it and covers the name asked (RFC 9462 §6.1: a resolver the owner chose for this zone): the suffix that chose the route must have at least `resolver.arpa`'s two labels (compared in lower case, with or without the final dot). Every other route leaves the name answered here while it sends its devices' other names to its upstream group:
  - a route for `arpa` (every reverse lookup, usually to the LAN's own resolver). It covers `resolver.arpa` only from above, and forwarding the name with the reverse lookups would relay that resolver's or its upstream's own discovery record to the devices again: issue #77;
  - a route for `.`;
  - a route without a suffix, which matches every name: a `[[group]] upstreams` (ADR-096), a `match_group` or a `match_qtype` route.
- A route for `resolver.arpa` with `match_group` forwards it for that group's devices only. Being the longer match, it wins over the group's own upstreams and over a route for `arpa`. The router reports the label count of the suffix that chose the route (`Selection::suffix_labels`, 0 without one). The pipeline, the explanation and the simulation all decide with one function (`pipeline::resolver_arpa_answered_here`).
- The private reverse names keep their own rule (any matching route forwards them): changing it changes answers for existing configurations, so it is left for its own decision.
- `[special] resolver_arpa` (default `true`) turns it off, as every special-name rule can be (ADR-014). It is shared by the cluster and, like `[clients] router_macs` (ADR-183), not written while it is on: a node one version behind refuses a `[special]` key it doesn't know, so at the default the primary sends exactly the keys such a node knows. It should be set to `false` only once every node runs a version that knows it (0.9.0 or later); an older build also refuses it in its own file.
- The classification is one more suffix comparison in `telltale_policy::classify`, which runs on every query: no allocation, lock or I/O. The suffix's label count is the one the router already computes to pick the longest match: one more `usize` in the selection.
- No new requirement ID: DNS-019 already covers the special names in the code and its tests.

**Not done:**
- Our own SVCB answer (the DDR feature, P1). It needs its own design: which listeners to advertise, certificates whose names cover the address the client used (RFC 9462 §4.2), each cluster node advertising itself, and what to answer without encrypted listeners.
- Built-in negative trust anchors for `resolver.arpa` (or any zone): one switches validation off for a subtree, and for this one would relay the upstream's discovery record. `[dnssec] negative_trust_anchors` remains for those who want one.
- Serving an answer the unsigned-zone proof can't finish as insecure.

**Consequences:**
- A client asking `_dns.resolver.arpa` learns that this resolver designates none and goes on asking it as before. In `validate` mode it gets NOERROR instead of SERVFAIL; in `permissive` mode the name is no longer counted as bogus. The upstream's own discovery record (its encrypted endpoints) is no longer passed to clients, and the query no longer leaves the network. This holds for devices in a group with its own upstreams too.
- An owner who routes `arpa` (every reverse lookup to the LAN's resolver, say) keeps `_dns.resolver.arpa` answered here. To forward it as well, they add a route for `resolver.arpa`; `docs/running.md` says so.
- The answer carries no SOA, like the other built-in NOERROR answers, so a client doesn't cache it as a negative answer; each ask is answered here again, with no upstream query.
- Mixed versions: a node on an earlier version keeps forwarding the name until it is upgraded; a replica sees the shared switch only when it is set to `false`.

**Verification:** `dns_019_resolver_arpa_is_locally_served` (the apex, `_dns`, a deeper name, upper case on the wire, look-alike names, the switch off); `dns_019_resolver_arpa_answered_locally_in_pipeline` (SVCB and A for `_dns.resolver.arpa` and A for the apex from a validating client: NOERROR, no records, no AD, the upstream not asked; a local record answers first; a rewrite into it; forwarded with a `[[route]]`; forwarded with the switch off); `dns_019_resolver_arpa_forwarded_only_by_a_route_for_it_or_below` (a group's own upstreams, `match_group`, `match_qtype`, `.` and `arpa` routes leave it answered here while they move the device's other names; routes for `Resolver.ARPA.` and `_dns.resolver.arpa` forward it; a route for `resolver.arpa` with a group forwards it for that group only; a route for `resolver.arpa` wins over the group's own upstreams and over a route for `arpa`); `dns_019_selection_counts_the_labels_of_the_matched_suffix` (the router); `dns_019_explain_says_resolver_arpa_is_answered_here`, `dns_019_explain_forwards_resolver_arpa_only_by_a_route_for_it_or_below` and `flt_013_explain_agrees_with_the_pipeline` over the same kinds of route (explain); `dns_019_turning_resolver_arpa_off_is_a_changed_answer` and `dns_019_simulation_forwards_resolver_arpa_only_by_a_route_for_it_or_below` (the simulation); `dns_019_resolver_arpa_default_on_and_not_written` and `clu_010_resolver_arpa_left_out_of_the_shared_part_at_its_default` (the switch and the shared part); `make bench-smoke` against the base.

## ADR-188 — Denials behind a CNAME are validated link by link, and a wildcard answer in a zone signed with NSEC is served only with the RFC 4035 §5.3.4 proof (Proposed)
**Context:** rough-ton's issue #98 and pull request #99. The validation library (hickory-net 0.26.3) proves an answer's NSEC or NSEC3 denial for the name that was asked, but after a CNAME the denial is about the chain's last name (RFC 4035 §3.1.3, §5.4), so a signed CNAME followed by a signed NODATA or NXDOMAIN is refused as bogus (`www.paypal.com AAAA`, `www.icann.org TXT`). In a zone signed with NSEC the library also asks for an NSEC covering the wildcard itself, which can't exist when that wildcard gave the answer, so a wildcard's answer one label below the wildcard is refused too (`nasa-gov.go-vip.net A`, and `www.nasa.gov A` through it). And a signed CNAME into an unsigned zone whose answers the library misjudges (`c.pki.goog A`) stays bogus as a whole, though ADR-098 and ADR-184 can prove the target's zone unsigned. The pull request added the two rules below. Review kept them and added what happens when a link gets no answer, a limit of their own, two restrictions, tests and this record.

**Decision:** two rules, on cache misses, in `validate` and `permissive` mode alike (the validator doesn't know the mode). Every wildcard answer in a zone signed with NSEC is checked against RFC 4035 §5.3.4 before it is served: the wildcard rule decides its NSEC verdict, and its signatures are checked by the library as for any answer. Link-by-link validation applies to an answer the library refused or returned with a bogus verdict, one the wildcard rule marked bogus included.
- **Link by link.** It runs for a question of class IN whose type follows a CNAME (any type but ANY, CNAME, DS, NSEC and RRSIG; a CNAME's owner has an NSEC and signatures of its own, RFC 4035 §2.3, §2.5), when the answer has a CNAME owned by the question name and either the library refused its denial (`DnsError::Nsec`) and ADR-098's proof didn't show the question's zone unsigned, or the answer has a bogus verdict (the library's, or the wildcard rule's) that ADR-098's proof didn't correct. Each link is then asked again as a question of its own through the validating handle: the CNAME at the name (the target is taken from a CNAME owned by that name), then the question at the target; a target whose answer starts with a CNAME, or whose denial the library refuses behind one, is the next link, and that answer is not used. Each answer is validated by the library for its own name, so a link is never worth more than the same name asked directly.
  - A link is secure or insecure as the library says, or insecure when the library calls it bogus and ADR-184's proof shows every owner in that link's answer, and the link's name, in a zone proven unsigned. Anything else (bogus, indeterminate, an error, a CNAME owned by another name, a loop, more than 8 links) and the answer stays bogus. The verdict is the weakest link's; AD only when it is secure.
  - The answer served is made of those answers only: each link's CNAME with its signatures; of the last answer, only the records owned by the name it answers, with their signatures; its authority section and the links' NSEC and NSEC3 records. Nothing of the refused answer is used. It is cached under the question like any validated answer, and never learned as an RFC 8198 range (it has an answer section).
- **Wildcard answers in zones signed with NSEC.** Every positive answer made from a wildcard (a secure signature covering fewer labels than its owner has) is checked by this rule, over the records the library validated (a refusal of its NSEC proof, `DnsError::Nsec`, carries them too). The rule is left out only for zones signed with NSEC3: when, for every zone that signed such a signature, the authority section has a verified NSEC3 record of that zone, owned directly below it; the rest is left to the library (RFC 5155 §8.8). The answer meets the rule when: the response code is NOERROR and the name isn't a wildcard; the answer section is the question's record set at the question name and its signatures, and nothing else, the records verified and at least one signature verified (a signature that isn't verified, such as a second key's or algorithm's during a rollover, is left out, RFC 6840 §5.4); every verified signature has the same label count, fewer than the name has, and the same signer, a zone the name is in with no more labels than that count; the authority section has no NSEC3; and it has a secure NSEC, with a secure signature by that zone, that covers the next closer name (the name cut to one label more than the count): the next closer name lies after the NSEC's owner and before its next name (or after the zone's last owner), its next name isn't below the next closer name (an empty non-terminal), and the owner isn't a delegation (NS without SOA) or a DNAME above it (RFC 6840 §4.1). An answer that the library didn't refuse, to a question for another type than CNAME, that starts with a CNAME at the question name made from a wildcard, also meets the rule when no record of another name in it is made from a wildcard and the CNAME's record set at the name meets those conditions as for a question for CNAME; the rest of the chain are records of names that exist, each validated by its own signature, and the chain ends in a record of the question's type. An answer that doesn't meet the rule has its records marked bogus and is handled as any bogus answer: ADR-098's proof (its lookups and a proof slot) may still show its zone unsigned; it is validated link by link when it starts with a CNAME owned by the question name, each link then checked by this rule for its own question; otherwise it is SERVFAIL with EDE 6 in `validate`. The check itself reads only the records already in the answer: no lookup.
- **Limits** (NFR-004): at most 8 links; the validation's deadline (ADR-098: two query budgets for the lookup and every proof); at most 4 link-by-link validations at once, on slots of their own, apart from ADR-098's 8 proof slots (the proof a link needs takes one of those); a question whose links didn't hold, or couldn't be checked in time, isn't validated link by link again for a minute; one that ended only because no slot was free is tried again with the next question.
- **No answer in time.** A link's question that times out, or that the upstream fails on, proves nothing: the answer stays bogus (SERVFAIL with EDE 6 in `validate`, never a stale answer; served in `permissive`). It is not an upstream failure.

**What it doesn't change:** cache hits (validation runs on misses only); the library's signature checks; the library's verdict on every other answer (one not made from a wildcard in a zone signed with NSEC, or one in a zone signed with NSEC3, left to the library as above), unless it is refused or bogus and link-by-link validation applies; ADR-184's proof and ADR-098's rules, used unchanged for each link; the minute after a cold start in which a name can fail (ADR-098, ADR-184): a question whose links failed is tried again after a minute, like a name whose proof failed; clients that set CD.

**Consequences:**
- These answers validate: with AD where every link is secure, without it where one is in a zone proven unsigned; in `permissive` mode they're no longer logged as bogus. A wildcard's answer in a zone signed with NSEC whose NSEC doesn't show that the next closer name is absent is bogus (SERVFAIL with EDE 6 in `validate`).
- A record set with a second signature that isn't verified (an algorithm or key rollover, several signers) is indeterminate (served without AD), as any answer with such a signature is: the wildcard rule leaves that signature out, so a wildcard answer with one and with its proof is indeterminate, not bogus; link by link, a link with one is indeterminate, so the answer stays bogus. Answers that stay bogus: a chain through a DNAME (only CNAMEs are followed); a link that reaches the library's depth limit, which ends bogus here and not indeterminate as it does for the question itself.
- A wildcard CNAME at the question name is checked as its own record set when the library didn't refuse the answer and the CNAME is the answer's only record made from a wildcard: secure in one lookup (not for a NODATA behind it). Otherwise (after a refusal, with another record made from a wildcard in the chain, or when the chain doesn't end in a record of the question's type) it is validated link by link, each link for its own question: that costs the walk's lookups, ends bogus while its 4 slots are busy, and isn't tried again for a minute after a link failed. An answer to a question for ANY or RRSIG made from a wildcard in a zone signed with NSEC doesn't meet the rule (it isn't the question's one record set) and is bogus.
- Cost: nothing on cache hits; the wildcard check reads only the answer it checks, and an answer it marks bogus costs what any bogus answer does (ADR-098's proof with its lookups and a proof slot; link by link, up to two validating lookups per link, 16 at most, and the unsigned-zone proof for a link that needs one), all within the deadline.
- If the library proves the denial for the chain's last name, link-by-link validation applies less often (it runs only after a refusal or a bogus verdict); the wildcard rule keeps checking every wildcard answer in a zone signed with NSEC.
- Tested offline only: answers built by hand for each condition of the wildcard rule and of when it applies, and the signed test root's `s.t.` (signed with NSEC: CNAME chains, wildcards one and two labels up, a wildcard CNAME to another wildcard, a name below an existing one, an empty non-terminal, a second signature by another key), `z3.s.t.` (below it, signed with NSEC3, with a wildcard), `t.` (NSEC3) and the unsigned `u.`, `u.t.` and `v.t.`, served by a fake upstream.

**Verification:** `dns_011_wildcard_answer_needs_its_proof`, `dns_011_nsec_covers_the_next_closer_name`, `dns_011_wildcard_rule_is_left_to_nsec3_only_beside_the_signers_verified_nsec3`, `dns_011_wildcard_cname_is_checked_as_its_own_record_set`, `dns_011_proofs_are_limited_and_failures_remembered`; offline: `dns_011_offline_denials_behind_a_cname_are_validated`, `dns_011_offline_links_that_do_not_hold_stay_bogus`, `dns_011_offline_signed_cname_into_misjudged_unsigned_zone_is_insecure`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_replayed_at_an_empty_non_terminal_stays_bogus`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_nodata_is_validated`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_answers_with_their_proof_are_secure`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_answers_with_their_proof_need_no_link_by_link_validation`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_answer_needs_its_next_closer_name_to_be_absent`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_rule_stays_on_beside_an_unverified_or_other_zone_nsec3`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_answers_in_an_nsec3_zone_are_secure`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_answer_with_a_second_signature_is_indeterminate`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_cname_to_another_wildcard_record_needs_each_proof`, `dns_011_offline_wildcard_cname_chain_that_stops_short_is_validated_link_by_link`, `dns_011_offline_link_lookup_timeout_stays_bogus`, `dns_011_link_validation_leaves_slots_for_its_proofs`, `dns_011_offline_composed_answer_keeps_only_the_chain`, `dns_011_offline_nsec_question_does_not_follow_a_cname`.

## ADR-189 — Languages in the web UI: English in the bundle, every other language a data file fetched on demand, checked by structure and owned by a named person (Proposed)
**Context:** snnh's pull request #101 added Simplified Chinese to every page of the web UI with a small translation layer of its own and no new dependency, and showed that the UI can be translated without a library. The project wants languages and regions as a pattern it adopts once and hands to contributors. The constraints are the owner's priorities (performance, then footprint), the 15 MiB image (NFR-001), the strict content security policy (ADR-030), the 400 KiB budget of `spec/07` §3, English text and formats that must not change for today's readers, and maintainers who can review a translation's structure but not its content. Two ways of writing a message were measured on the contributor's tree before any code was written (a key per message, or the English text as the key); with the English text as the key the core grew by 14.9 KiB gzipped and mount time and memory stayed within 10% of main, and keys did not fit the size gate, so this decision takes English as the key.

**Decision:**
- **A translation layer of our own, no dependency.** `t()` for a message, `tn()` for a count, and a `<T>` component for a sentence with markup inside it. Placeholders are `{name}`, substituted in one pass from the parameters' own properties; a missing value renders as empty. `<T>` splits a message on named tags and renders each piece as text or through a snippet the caller supplies: translated text never becomes markup, and `{@html}` stays forbidden. Its message is written as an expression (`msg={'Muted by <b>{who}</b>'}`), because Svelte substitutes `{…}` inside a quoted attribute itself.
- **English is the key.** `const { t, tn, m } = tr('cache')` binds a file's context; `t('Holds')` keeps the English in the source, and there is no English catalog. With English active `t()` returns its literal after substitution, so English output equals the source by construction. A translation is found through a hash (FNV-1a, 32 bits) of the context, the normalized English and an optional note. A translation file holds `[english, translation]` pairs; a generation step that runs before the type check, the tests, the dev server and the build ships only the translations whose English is still in the source (compared as text, never by hash), without the English, and writes the table of their addresses; neither is committed. A translation of older English no longer matches and is never shown.
- **Catalogs are data.** One pair file per locale in `ui/src/locales/`, in one canonical form (sorted by code unit). Every catalog other than English is a hashed JSON file under `/assets/locales/`, fetched when that language is chosen, from a table of literal addresses made at build time. It is never run as script, and the content security policy is unchanged. A catalog that is empty or cannot be read, or a load that fails, leaves the page in English. A pair file's sections are context names (or the left-over section): anything else fails, and the check prints what a file holds only as data (quoted, with line breaks and other controls escaped).
- **Locales are BCP 47 tags** of the form language, optional script, optional region. A language written in more than one script names the script (`zh-Hans`, `zh-Hant`). A manifest lists each locale's owners, direction and whether it is offered. A request (the `lang` parameter, the stored choice, then the browser's languages in order) is matched to a catalog of the same language and script; a bare language in the browser's list takes the script of a more specific entry before it, and a catalog whose tag names no script is taken only for a request of its likely script (a `yue` catalog is not taken for `yue-CN`). A reader of Traditional Chinese is not given Simplified text. There is no catalog of English: every `en-*` tag is refused, since an English request is answered in English and `en-XA` is computed from it.
- **Regional catalogs** follow one model: a catalog falls back to its parent and then to English, and holds only what differs. The manifest reserves the fields; the merge is built when a second regional catalog exists, and does not change the catalog format. Until then a regional language is a full catalog.
- **The catalog is loaded before the page is drawn,** and changing the language is a deliberate step that reloads the page. `t()` is a plain function with no reactive state.
- **Text belongs to one source file.** The context of a message names the file that shows it; a file uses only its own and a small `common` set. Translated text is display text only: it is never an identifier, a stored value or part of a request.
- **English output does not change.** With English, dates, times and numbers keep the browser's own format, as before; the compact forms the UI writes itself (`5m ago`, `1.5 KiB`) become messages whose English equals today's output. The plural form of a message is chosen by the language of the message, never by the browser's language. With another catalog, formatting follows the browser's variant of that language, through `Intl`.
- **Plural categories for the check come from a committed table** (`ui/src/locales/plurals.json`, written once from CLDR by a maintainer command and reviewed), never from the JavaScript engine, whose CLDR data moves with Node and differs between browsers. The browser picks a form with `Intl.PluralRules` and falls back to the catalog's own `other`.
- **English never waits for a translation.** `npm run check:i18n` fails only on what the author of the changed file controls. Missing, outdated and left-over translations are reported, never failed. A change to English alone cannot fail another language. Literal text, an `Intl` call or a physical CSS property in a source file fails whether or not the ratchet baseline lists the file (part A's last commit made it so for a file the baseline does not list): a new file holds none of the three, and a moved file that still holds some keeps its counts only when its baseline entries are renamed to the new path by hand: the baseline command lowers counts and lists a new file only at zero, and never raises a count.
- **A translation must agree with its English.** It has the same placeholders, tags and web addresses (in any case; a `scheme://` counts in any width, so a fullwidth one is not hidden from the rule). It keeps every structural token of the English (anything with `://`, a path, `[x]`, `=`, `_`, `$`, `|`, a host and port or an IP address; glossary terms such as `NXDOMAIN`), every number, and every word the English entry lists as to be kept verbatim (literal values a reader types, such as `email` or `ntfy`; the list is kept beside the English, so nothing changes on the page). It adds no address, host and port, path or setting (`://`, `/x/`, `=`, `$`, `|`) that the English does not have, also when written with fullwidth or ideographic punctuation (a second reading with the punctuation folded to ASCII, where only a dotted host and port counts and the English's own addresses are left out where each stands whole, never its words). A bare `https://` followed by a letter of another script is an address in that script. A plural form other than `other` may leave out placeholders (a dual, or a "one" that names no number); both English forms of a plural name the same placeholders. It contains no direction overrides, format characters, default-ignorable code points (invisible fillers, every variation selector), tag characters, surrogates that are not half of a pair (added with ADR-190) or words that mix scripts. Hyphenated words, short acronyms, the other tokens a translation adds and a `.` between two letters of another script are reported, not failed. A maintainer may exempt a message in `notes.json` from the digit rule (`exemptDigits`, with a reason), or allow it named tokens under the added-token rule (`exemptAdded`, a list such as `["24:00"]`, for a time, ratio, date pattern or price); the allowed tokens are then reported, and any other added token still fails. The check fails a translation that does not agree, and the browser never shows one: English is shown instead. The browser re-checks every rule that needs no data file; for a catalog the build shipped it leaves out the digit and added-token rules, which the check already applied with the maintainers' exemptions, and it applies them to anything else (a catalog given by another source, never counted as built) as that source's code brings them, showing none of such a catalog without them (amended with ADR-190, so that an English reader's first load does not carry those two rules). What the rule cannot read (an address in the language's own script without a scheme, digits of another script, an address with spaces) is for the language's owner, who reads the text.
- **Context names are unique.** Two source files never share one context; where two files have the same name, a committed map gives the library file a prefixed name.
- **`format.ts` keeps its exports.** The formatters are built by a factory for one locale and installed before the page is drawn; the functions the UI already imports delegate to it.
- **A language is offered by a flag in the manifest,** set by a maintainer at release time. A language under 80% of the text that existed at the previous release, at two releases in a row, is no longer offered until it catches up; it stays reachable with `?lang=`. Each language has named owners in the manifest, who answer for its content; maintainers review structure. Strings marked sensitive (destructive confirmations, security warnings) need a back-translation in the pull request that changes them.
- **The choice is kept per browser** (`telltale.locale`), like the theme, the detail level and the domain icons (ADR-167): no server state, no API, nothing in the cluster. `?lang=` applies to one page load and is not stored. The setting is under Settings, Account, and on the sign-in page, and is shown only when another language is offered.
- **Budgets.** The 400 KiB budget of `spec/07` §3 becomes the budget of the UI with English. Each further language may take 80 KiB gzipped, and all of them together 400 KiB. `npm run build` prints and enforces the three figures and fails if text of another language is found outside `/assets/locales/`.
- **What is not translated:** API problem messages, alert text, the CLI, logs, the audit log, the OpenAPI document and MCP tool descriptions, and sentences the server composes. For an error with a stable `code` the UI may show a translated title, and always shows the server's English `detail` under it. The in-app help and the documentation stay English for now and are marked as English text on a translated page.
- **A pseudo-locale** (`en-XA`, longer accented text) is computed from English in the browser, reachable only with `?lang=en-XA`, and used by the tests to find text that bypassed the layer and translated text that reached a request or storage.

**Not done (considered):**
- A key per message with an English catalog: measured at about 39 KiB over main, about 24 KiB more than English as the key, over the size gate; it would give strings stable names.
- A translation library (a store-based runtime with ICU MessageFormat; a compiler that turns messages into functions): a dependency or a build plugin for what a few hundred lines do here.
- One UI build per language: several copies of the UI in one image, or a language fixed at install time.
- A language setting stored with the account: a state-store change, replication and a new API operation for a display preference. It can be added later behind the one function that reads the preference.
- Right-to-left layout (logical CSS properties, mirrored icons, isolated names and addresses, a right-to-left pseudo-locale): its own task, required before a right-to-left language is offered. Until then a check refuses new physical CSS properties.
- Translated in-app help: a second on-demand file per language with its own budget, as a later task (it needs the image build to copy the translated topics).
- The merge of regional catalogs (above).

**Consequences:**
- The layer itself (runtime, matching, the browser's agreement check, the pseudo-locale, the format factory) adds at most 4 KiB gzipped to the core; moving the English text behind `t()` adds about 15 KiB more (14.9 KiB measured on the contributor's tree). At the end of part A, with every page on the layer, the core measures 317.0 KiB gzipped (324,567 B), against 292.9 KiB (299,915 B) for the same tree before the layer: 24.1 KiB more in all, under the 320 KiB gate. A reader of English downloads no other language.
- Each offered language adds about its gzipped size to the image (the binary embeds the compressed file, ADR-168): about 41 KiB for the first one, at most 0.39 MiB for all languages under the envelope.
- A developer adds text by writing `t()` in the file that shows it: one file changes. Text typed straight into the markup, or into a text prop of a component (a label, a title, an empty state, a noun, a summary, a hint, a placeholder), fails the check in a file the ratchet baseline holds at zero and in a file it does not list (a new one).
- A translation pull request touches only that language's file and, for a new language, its manifest entry (the check fails either one alone). A reviewer who cannot read it checks that, the green check, the report's sensitive and swap lines and the language owner's approval.
- An English edit that changes the text in anything but its spacing and the form of its quotes, dashes and ellipsis (a full stop added or a capital letter changed counts) drops that string's translations until they are confirmed again (`carry` keeps them when the meaning is unchanged); English is shown meanwhile.
- Strings have no stable names: tests and docs refer to the text.
- `spec/07` §3 states the three budgets; `ui/README.md` has the contributor guide; `docs/running.md` describes the setting once a language is offered.

**Verification:** `api_012 interpolation is single pass and own-property only`, `api_012 a nullish parameter renders as empty`, `api_012 message markup becomes text and named snippets only`, `api_012 locale matching follows the request order and scripts`, `api_012 english plurals come from the catalog language`, `api_012 english plurals ignore the browser language` (A1), `api_012 a missing plural category falls back within the catalog`, `api_012 plural forms other than other may leave out placeholders`, `api_012 a plural form other than other may hold fewer placeholders`, `api_012 the runtime and the script hash alike`, `api_012 english formats match the frozen functions`, `api_012 catalogs are canonical`, `api_012 a translation keeps the placeholders tags urls and tokens of english`, `api_012 a translation adds no address port path or setting`, `api_012 a maintainer may exempt an entry from the added-token rule`, `api_012 an added-token exemption in notes.json reports what it lets through`, `api_012 the agreement rule takes linear time on any text`, `api_012 url and token extraction`, `api_012 forbidden code points`, `api_012 findings name the rule the token and its place`, `api_012 catalog entries named like object members are data`, `api_012 an english-only change never fails a locale`, `api_012 missing and outdated translations are reported not failed`, `api_012 sensitive changes and swaps are reported against the previous version`, `api_012 the check sees each mistake it is for`, `api_012 the tk() lint reads a name where it is declared`, `api_012 the shipping step reports structured findings`, `api_012 the job summary prints no catalog value outside a fence`, `api_012 the console report prints no line a file controls`, `api_012 prebuild writes the shipped catalogs and the url table`, `api_012 the pair-file parser refuses what json would lose`, `api_012 check and sync take plural categories from the committed table`, `api_012 the languages tool says which node it needs`, `api_012 npm runs the generation step inside its scripts`, `api_012 the runtime drops a translation that differs from current english`, `api_012 the browser re-checks digits and added tokens only where the build did not ship the catalog`, `api_012 the catalog step falls back to english`, `api_012 a file uses only its own context`, `api_012 migrated files hold no literal text`, `api_012 a new removed or renamed file leaves the ratchets green`, `api_012 the baseline command lowers counts and never raises one`, `api_012 the attributes a reader sees or hears are text`, `api_012 the codemod moves text and attributes behind t()`, `api_012 the codemod lists what it cannot move safely`, `api_012 the codemod output renders the same english`, `api_012 the codemod output passes the languages check`, `api_012 only format.ts formats`, `api_012 no new physical css properties`, `api_012 an english session requests only core assets`, `api_012 lang parameter applies to one page load and stores nothing`, `api_012 pseudo locale leaves no literal text: <page>` (A1), `api_012 pseudo text never reaches a request or storage` (A1), `api_012 a chosen language loads its catalog and sets lang` (B), `api_012 auto follows the browser list in order` (B), `api_012 a language is hidden by its manifest flag` (B), `api_012 a failed catalog request falls back to english` (B); the size lines of `npm run build`. A test marked (A1) or (B) is written by that part.

## ADR-190 — Testing a language pack in the browser: a local file checked by the build's own rules, under an English banner above every page, removed at the next load after any upgrade, after 7 days, or on request; the template comes from the build (Proposed)
**Context:** ADR-189 makes English the only language in the bundle, written in the source, and every other language a file of `[english, translation]` pairs per context. A translation is checked by structure, in the build and in the browser, and owned by a named person. A translator then needs to see their file on a running TelltaleDNS before opening a pull request, without a development checkout or Node tooling: on every page, at real widths, with the rules the build will apply. English exists only in the source, so the browser alone can't list the texts to translate.

The constraints are those of ADR-189: performance, then footprint; the 15 MiB image (NFR-001); the strict content security policy (ADR-030); an English reader who downloads nothing new. Add the deployments TelltaleDNS runs on: replicated and stateless pods, read-only root file systems, and clusters with one management plane, where nothing outside DNS may affect answering (CLU-004).

A label can be made to lie without breaking any structural rule. So whoever loads a file must see at all times that the labels come from it, and the next person at the same browser must not meet those labels at sign-in. Plural categories, as JavaScript engines report them, differ between engines and CLDR versions, so a file built from them in one browser can fail the repository's check in another.

**Decision:**
- **The pack lives only in the browser.**
  - It is a file the user picks or drops under Settings, Account. The card appears in the Advanced view, and in the Simple view too while a pack or a notice is active (ADR-036).
  - It is read with the browser's file API and kept, with its record, in one site-storage key for that address.
  - Nothing about it is uploaded, stored on a node, replicated, backed up, logged or audited. No API operation or MCP tool is added: like the theme, the domain icon style (ADR-167) and the language choice (ADR-189), it is a display matter in one browser.
  - It can't be loaded from an address, a link parameter or a message from another page.
- **Plural categories come from a committed table.**
  - `ui/src/locales/plurals.json` maps each canonical language to its categories, in the order zero, one, two, few, many, other. It is generated once from CLDR and reviewed, and changed only by deliberate commits.
  - The repository's check, `sync` and the browser read it. The engine's plural rules are used only at runtime, to pick a category, with the fallback to `other`.
  - A language tag whose language isn't in the table is refused, and so is a tag the engine rewrites to another language. So are English, the pseudo-locales, `qaa`–`qtz`, `mis`, `mul`, `zxx`, `und` and private-use tags.
  - Display names from the browser name the language and decide nothing. The direction follows the script.
- **The template comes from the build.**
  - The generation step that writes the shipped catalogs (ADR-189) also writes one skeleton, `assets/locales/template-<hash>.json`, from the same extraction `sync` uses. For every current English entry it holds the disambiguation note, the translator note, the sensitive tag and the verbatim words; it also holds the glossary and a copy of the plural table.
  - For the tag the translator types, the browser turns it into `<tag>.json`, using one module shared with `sync` and ordering strings by code unit. The bytes are what `sync` writes for a new language.
  - A companion `<tag>-notes.txt` carries the header (tag, name, direction, the web UI build it was made from), plain instructions, the glossary, the notes, the sensitive entries and the verbatim words. A header inside the pair file would make it differ from what `sync` writes.
- **The check is the build's own.**
  - The browser runs the generation step's shipping function, from a module the check script shares: a pair is current when its context, normalized English and note equal an extracted entry as text, never by hash. It also runs the whole agreement rule, a module of its own apart from the runtime's first-use check, so a page's first load never carries it.
  - Only the file's current pairs are held to the rule there: a pair whose identity is not in the template is left over without it, so every English the rule reads in the browser is the build's own. In the browser, where every English the rule reads is the build's own, it reads a translation in linear time: none of its patterns backtracks over an unbounded run, its loops read through sets, and each of the English's own patterns is built once. Four times the input costs under six times as much on shapes made against it, and a whole file of 1 MiB of such pairs is checked in about a second or two under Node (0.28 to 1.57 s on files of up to 400 distinct structural English entries shaped against it; about a minute before the rule was made linear). The check script, which holds each pair to its own English half, grows with the product of the two token counts on input shaped against it (below).
  - A file is refused whole, and nothing is stored, when it is not UTF-8 JSON of the pair format within the limits (1 MiB, 10,000 pairs, 600 contexts, strings of 4,000 code units, context names), or when it repeats a key. The shared parser refuses duplicate keys for `sync` too.
  - An entry that breaks the agreement rule, or has other plural categories than its language, is dropped (English is shown) and listed with the rule in plain words. The card counts the texts that break a rule, each pair once, and lists every finding. Hyphenated words, short acronyms and added tokens are listed only.
  - Left-over pairs say why: a changed note, English that isn't in this build, or a pair set aside under `~leftover`. Coverage per context and any difference from the canonical bytes are shown.
  - Text from the file is shown as plain text, isolated for direction, with forbidden code points as visible markers. It is never treated as message markup.
  - **Download for a pull request** writes what `sync <tag>` writes for that file at this build: canonical bytes, missing entries added empty, left-over pairs under `~leftover`, translations unchanged.
  - The screen names what only the repository's check can judge: the manifest entry and owner, back-translations of sensitive entries, English changed since this build, and the bytes as committed.
- **Applying it.**
  - After a confirmation that says what a pack can do, the page reloads with the pack as its language. The pack's table goes through the same installation as a fetched catalog, before the page is drawn, and the runtime checks each translation against its English the first time it shows it.
  - The record keeps the check's table, stamped with the web UI build and the file's fingerprint it was made for. A page load with that build and that file installs the kept table without the template or a new check. Any other load (no kept table, another stamp, or a kept table that is not well formed) checks the file again and keeps the new table in the same one write. When the record with its table does not fit the browser's storage, the record alone is kept, and each load checks the file.
  - `?lang=` applies to one page load and pauses the pack; the banner says so. Otherwise the pack comes before the stored choice and Auto.
  - `<html lang>` and `dir` are the pack's, and the format locale follows ADR-189's rule.
  - A language the build offers under the same tag is replaced, never merged. It works when no language is offered.
- **Paused where someone else may be next, and for anyone but the user who loaded it.**
  - On the sign-in page (its two-factor and recovery steps included), the setup page, the screen shown when the server can't be reached, a display link and a display session, the pack is not applied. Whether a user is signed in, and who, is read from the public sign-in status before the page is drawn.
  - A pack belongs to the user who loaded it: Use records who is signed in, and a page load pauses the pack for anyone else, with a banner line that says another user of this browser loaded it (no name is shown).
  - The banner there offers **Show the test pack on this page**, which applies it for the next page load only.
  - The app tells the pack's code who is signed in whenever that changes. Someone going away from a page that shows the pack reloads it: Sign out, a request that finds the session gone, or the display page reached by a change of the hash. That load is paused whatever the status says, so a status that disagrees with the app's own requests (two nodes behind one address) never makes a page reload again and again.
  - A sign-out that goes on to the provider's (an OIDC session, API-004) is not reloaded. The app's sign-out sets `devpack.leaving` before it asks the server: while the answer is awaited, a request that finds the session gone and the report of nobody it brings make the pack's code hold its reload back. With a provider's address in the answer the mark stays, the page leaves for the provider, and the page the provider sends back to is paused by the same flag; a reload there would cancel the provider's sign-out. Without one (a local sign-out), or when the request fails, the sign-out clears the mark in its `finally` and calls `devpack.settled`, which reloads once if a reload was held back; otherwise the report of nobody reloads as for any sign-out. One sign-out runs at a time: a call while one is under way (a second click before the answer) gets that call's promise and does nothing else, so it sends no second request and never clears the mark of a page on its way to the provider. Back to the page that left, which the browser may show again as it was from its back-forward cache, reloads it into a paused load.
  - A sign-in on a page paused for nobody or for another user reloads it, into the pack for the user who loaded it, with a flag of its own. A load that follows either reload, for a sign-in or for someone going away, does not reload for the first user the app reports. The app reports on every change of page, and only a change of who is signed in counts. So reads that disagree, in one direction or in turn from load to load, reload a page twice at most in a row, and a later sign-in on that load still reloads (after a sign-out the app's first report is nobody). "Show the test pack on this page" keeps its one load as it is.
- **The banner.**
  - Every page shows an English banner first in the document, the sign-in, setup, wall and display pages included. It is set left to right, with a stacking order above every overlay of the UI (drawers, menus, popovers, the wall). Its height is passed through the CSSOM to the app's fixed and sticky layers (the header, the side menu and its scrim, the drawers, the wall), which move down by it, so nothing covers it. Two small layers are placed from their own anchors and not moved: a tip's bubble, which opens upward only when there is no room below its label, and the health panel, which opens upward from the bottom of the side menu. In a window too short to open them toward the page, they can reach under the banner, which stays on top.
  - It names the language, the file name (forbidden code points removed, at most 48 characters, isolated for direction) and a 12-hex SHA-256 fingerprint of the file, and has a button that removes the pack and reloads.
  - The banner, the confirmation, the notices, the line naming the pack in use and the card's Use, Download for a pull request and Remove buttons hold no message of the layer: their English is written as it is, under `translate="no"` and `lang="en"`, so neither a pack nor a shipped catalog changes them. The text lint and the pseudo-locale rule already hold text under `translate="no"` as kept as written; no exemption is added.
- **Lifetime.**
  - The record holds two stamps: the web UI's build identity, the content-hashed file name of the entry module (which changes with the English by construction, and which the build checks holds the template's address), read from the page's own entry module script, from this address, whatever other module scripts the page holds; and the server's build commit, read when the pack is loaded.
  - The first stamp is compared before every page is drawn. The second is compared with the build the signed-in UI already polls.
  - Any difference removes the pack, with a notice: an upgrade, a rollback or a downgrade, a server-only release included. Another web UI build removes it at the next page load, signed out too. Another server build removes it at the first page after sign-in: that page draws with the pack until the build poll's first answer, which comes as the page mounts, then reloads.
  - It is also removed 7 days after loading (loading the file again restarts the 7 days), and when its stored copy can't be read. The clock is read both ways: a record loaded after now, or meant to last longer than 7 days, is expired, and a notice dated after now goes like one 7 days old.
  - A check that throws twice in a row on the same file retires the pack. A template or checking code that can't be fetched leaves the pack for the next load.
  - A tab writes over or removes only what it read, while the key still holds the same record (fingerprint and load time), the same notice or the same damaged value: a tab left open never replaces or deletes a pack another tab loaded since. A notice takes a pack's place only after the key is emptied, and a page reloads for it only when the key no longer holds the pack, so storage that takes no write never makes a page reload into the same pack.
  - A page is never blank because of a pack. Any error or timeout gives the normal language. The pack's code, its two requests and the checking code share the catalog's limit of 4 s from the start of the language step; an import that stalls is given up then, and a pack decided after that is not applied (its banner says it could not be checked). A failure while drawing removes the pack, with a notice that says a page could not be drawn with it, and reloads once. A pack lookup never throws. A tab opened before an upgrade says the page must be reloaded.
- **Who.** Any signed-in role can load a pack: it changes nothing on the server. Once loaded it applies in that browser for that address, to the user who loaded it.
- **Cost.**
  - A reader who never uses it makes no extra request and loads no pack code: two storage reads in the load step, the card's heading and Open in Settings (Advanced view), one hook in the existing build poll, one call when the signed-in user changes, the sign-out's mark that it is under way (set before it asks the server, kept or cleared by the answer) with its call to the pack's code when the page does not leave for the provider, one sign-out at a time, and the stacking offsets.
  - The pack's code is lazy: the boot (pages with a pack in use), the card, and the checking code they share, which a page load with a kept table does not fetch.
  - The template is fetched only to download or check. `npm run build` gives it a line and a cap of its own, 64 KiB gzipped, outside the budget of the UI with English and outside the total for all languages. It fails if the template is not the one file it allows under `assets/locales/`, or if the entry module does not hold the template's address.
  - The gates, measured on the build machine against the language layer's base: an English reader downloads at most 1.0 KiB gzipped more for this task's own change (measured 958 B with the language-pack code of fe50d5a3: the card's sentence is in its chunk, the digit and added-token rules are in the pack's code, which gives them to the runtime, and the pack's code reads the web UI's build from the page); the core line, lazy code included, grows by at most 24 KiB (measured 22,955 B, the same way; the design's first figure was 16 KiB, revised because the pack's code is lazy, loaded only by someone who opens the card or has a pack in use, and the core stays far under the spec's 400 KiB); a pack user's boot takes at most 300 ms median at a 4x slower CPU on 2,600 pairs (measured at 1fa9c893: 47.3 ms with the kept table, which the design's own fallback for this ceiling introduced, against 48.3 ms for the same build's control; 484.1 ms when the file is checked again). The 1.0 KiB gate is this task's own: the bundle that also carries the keyed-lists fix adds more to an English first load, 1,319 B, and grows the core line by 23,501 B (measured at fe50d5a3, which is 42970ebe with the one-at-a-time sign-out).

**Not done (considered):**
- A file on the server in a temporary folder: a file on one replica is not on the others, and read-only root file systems leave no temporary place but the persistent data directory. It needs an API operation with the agent rules (AGT-001 to AGT-005), audit and backup exclusion. Whatever one user stores would change the labels other users see.
- Keeping the pack in the cluster's state: replication and a state-store change for one person's test.
- Removing the pack only when the web UI changes: a server-only release would keep it, contrary to "removed on every upgrade". It saves about 100 bytes.
- A new API field carrying the server's version to signed-out pages: loading needs a session, and the signed-in UI already polls the build.
- Plural categories from the engine: engines and CLDR versions disagree, so the template, `sync` and the check could disagree.
- Two storage keys: two tabs could pair one file's record with another's text.
- A header section inside the template: it would differ from what `sync` writes, and every committed catalog would carry bytes that change with each build.
- The template as a script chunk, computed from the bundle (which holds no contexts or notes), or published only with releases (offline networks and development builds would have none).
- Merging a pack over a shipped catalog: it would hide what the pack itself holds.
- Showing English beside sensitive strings while testing: it changes the layout the pack is meant to test.
- A server switch to forbid packs, or a role limit: the pack changes nothing on the server, and the browser's user can already run any script in their own page.
- A context of messages a pack may not translate (an earlier draft): a shipped catalog could still change them, and English written as it is under `translate="no"` needs no rule of its own.
- Checking the file again at every page load: about half a second at a 4x slower CPU for 2,600 pairs, where the kept table takes about 50 ms.
- Events between tabs, starting from a built language's translations, and choosing the direction by hand: not needed for the loop of template, check, use and pull request.
- Session storage for the pack, a key per user (the record names its user), and an editor in the browser.
- Checking a file in chunks between which the page answers: the check is one synchronous pass of the shipping function the check script shares, and its worst case is now about a second or two for 1 MiB under Node; a resumable form of it is more than that needs. A worker is not used.
- Moving the tip's bubble and the health panel by the banner's height: inline-style code in two eager components (about 60 B gzipped), for windows too short to open them toward the page.
- Calling the pack's user hook from the app's per-user effect, to save its own: that effect would then run on every change of page, and reread the view mode and the icon choice (with storage blocked, it would reset a mode chosen in memory).
- Clearing the signed-in user only after the page has left for the provider's sign-out: it changes the sign-out of every user, not only with a pack, and the app would keep showing a signed-in page while it waits for a provider that may not answer.
- A time limit on the sign-out's hold: the app's requests set none, so while an answer to Sign out never comes the mark stays, and a session that ends meanwhile draws the sign-in page with the pack's text under its banner until an answer comes or someone signs in there (Remove and signing in still work). With one sign-out at a time, a further click on Sign out, with a pack or without, waits for that same answer. A timer would add eager code for a server that takes the request and never answers it.
- A mark of three values (waiting, leaving for the provider, neither) in place of one sign-out at a time: a second click would still send a second request, and a second answer that came first would still reload the page before the first answer comes.
- A maintainer's exemption from the digit rule for the check's limits, in place of plural messages: the runtime's first-use check of a pack has no notes, so it would show English for a translation the card's check passed.
- Holding `{max}` in every plural form of the check's limits: ADR-189's rule lets a form other than `other` leave a placeholder out, and an exception per message would need notes the runtime's first-use check does not have. Languages such as Russian and Polish show their `many` form for these limits, so a number written there in place of `{max}` is held to no rule. The notes for translators say to keep `{max}` in every form of a limit.

**Consequences:**
- A translator without tooling can produce, check and see a language, and send the downloaded file as a pull request unchanged. Developers with a checkout keep `sync`, a manifest entry with `offered: false`, and `?lang=<tag>`.
- For this task's own change, an English reader downloads 958 B more and makes no new request (1,319 B with the keyed-lists fix of the same bundle). Its core line grows by 22,955 B, nearly all of it the pack's lazy code (the card about 10 KiB, the checking code about 5 KiB, the boot and its banner about 4 KiB, the record's code with the digit and added-token rules about 2 KiB). The template takes 29.4 KiB on its own line; in the image both are about their gzipped size (ADR-168).
- A pack user's page load does one more public status request; with the kept table it fetches nothing else for the pack. Use asks for the server's build and for the public status (who is signed in). The record with its table is about 1.5 times the file's text (a 2,600-pair German pack: 383,588 characters, under 0.8 MB as the browser stores it).
- With a pack in use, a sign-out that goes on to the provider draws the sign-in page with the pack's text until the browser leaves for the provider (T29.3's AC (4) says so), and so does a session that ends while a sign-out waits for the server's answer, until that answer comes. Back to that page, shown again from the browser's back-forward cache, reloads it into a paused load; Playwright's headless shell never shows a page again from that cache, so the two Back tests launch a full Chromium where one is installed (CI's two browser jobs install it), first check that the browser they got shows a page of the app again from that cache, and skip, saying why, where it does not. A sign-in on that page, if it never left, also takes the Back listener away. If the provider's address never replaces the page, that page stays so until the next page load or sign-in: a sign-in by another user reloads it, paused, and after a sign-in by the same user a later sign-out or session end takes the pack away as any does.
- The check's three limits (contexts, pairs, characters in a text) are plural messages whose count is the limit, with the number formatted for the language: a translator writes the noun in the form that number takes and copies no digit.
- A pack is removed by every upgrade, including each development build of an edge install, and on each switch between builds when nodes with different builds answer behind one address.
- The browser's check can differ from the repository's when main's English or rules have moved on since the build. The repository's check decides, and English-only changes still never fail a language (ADR-189).
- A pack someone else wrote can show misleading labels to the person who loads it. The confirmation, the banner above everything, the pause where someone else may be next and for any other user, `?lang=en`, the 7 days and the removal on any upgrade are the safeguards. The documentation advises loading other people's packs on a test installation.
- A kept table is trusted a little more than a file: a page load installs it without the file's check, and the runtime's first-use check has no verbatim words or glossary, so a table planted in the key could drop a term the file's check would keep; planting it needs script on this origin, which could change the page anyway.
- `sync` (ADR-189) keeps the first pair of an identity when a file has two, as the build ships it, and sets the later one aside under `~leftover`; it kept the last and dropped the first. So the card's "only the first is used", the page, its Download for a pull request and `sync` agree, plurals included: the browser's check lists a later pair of a plural's identity as there twice and never shows it, whatever the first pair's categories.
- The agreement rule also forbids a surrogate that is not half of a pair, which draws as U+FFFD (ADR-189's list, amended here; no catalog, fixture or template message held one).
- The check script still places each finding by a search from the start of the translation, which grows faster than the text on shapes made against the rule (a few milliseconds for a pair of 4,000 units). The worst shape left is an English of thousands of structural tokens against a translation of tokens joined by a fullwidth equals sign: about 20 ms a pair there (0.4 s before). It never reaches the browser, whose English is the build's own.
- Nothing about a pack appears in a backup, a support package, the audit log or any other browser.
- The modules the check script shares with the browser import each other by file name (`./safe.ts`), as Node's own type stripping needs; `ui/tsconfig.json` allows such imports (`allowImportingTsExtensions`, with `noEmit`). It is a compile-time setting only: the bundle does not change.
- `ui/README.md` (Languages), `docs/running.md` (Web UI), `spec/07` §3 and the site's budget page describe it; the site's page for translators is its own task (T29.4). `.gitattributes` keeps `ui/src/locales/*.json` as LF text.

## ADR-196 — The validation library's cache is bounded: 8,192 record-set proofs per upstream group (Proposed)
**Context:**
- hickory-net's validating handle keeps one cache of record-set proofs (an LRU) and sizes it at 1,048,576 entries unless told otherwise (`DEFAULT_VALIDATION_CACHE_SIZE`; `validation_cache_size` sets another size, in 0.26.3 and 0.26.4 alike). TelltaleDNS never sized it, and builds one handle per upstream group.
- An expired entry is skipped on lookup but not removed, so entries leave only when the cache is full: memory grows with every distinct record set validated, up to that capacity.
- hickory-net 0.26.4 (pull request #106) also keys each entry with a copy of its record set and signatures (the fix for GHSA-qgw4-m8xx-p695).
- Measured on 2026-11-27, an entry costs 1.6-1.7 KiB of RSS on 0.26.4, against 0.22-0.44 KiB on 0.26.3. Unbounded, that is about 1.7 GiB per upstream group at capacity (0.2-0.45 GiB on 0.26.3), far outside `spec/00` §5 (64 MiB loaded, a 128 MiB pod limit, a Pi Zero 2 W).

**Decision:**
- Each group's handle is built with `validation_cache_size(8_192)` (`VALIDATION_CACHE_ENTRIES` in `telltale-upstream`'s `dnssec.rs`). The least recently used entries go first.
- It is a constant, not a configuration key: the bound is about memory per group, which the measurements below fix, and a key would need writing only when it differs from its default, plus an upgrade-order note. A key can follow if live numbers show a need.
- A test-only builder sets other sizes for the offline tests.

**Measurements** (the offline harness on the build machine, debug build; RSS growth over the warmed validator, each case in its own process; filled to 1.25 times its size, the RSS stops growing once the cache is full):

| entries | hickory 0.26.4 | hickory 0.26.3 |
|---|---|---|
| 4,096 | 6.4-6.8 MiB | 0.9-1.7 MiB |
| 8,192 | 12.7-13.5 MiB | 1.8-3.5 MiB |
| 16,384 | 25.4-27.1 MiB | 3.4-7.0 MiB |
| 65,536 | 100.6-108.1 MiB | 13.8-28.0 MiB |

Each range spans three kinds: signed NXDOMAIN (NSEC), signed wildcard answers, unsigned NODATA. A signed answer takes about two entries (its record set and its proof), an unsigned denial one.

**What a bound costs** (0.26.4; signed names asked twice in turn, upstream questions per name in the second round):
- A record set validated again while its proof is kept costs only its own question.
- Once more distinct record sets are validated in turn than the cache holds, each re-validation costs what the first validation did: its zone's key lookups again (3 questions instead of 1 for a signed wildcard answer, the name and two DNSKEY lookups).
- With 8,192 entries, 2,048 names cost 1.00 each and 16,384 names 3.00. With 16,384 entries, 4,096 names cost 1.00 and 8,192 names already 3.00. The library's own size gives 1.00 for 32,768 names.
- Re-validation while a proof is still kept happens mainly on prefetch of popular names and after the answer cache evicts an entry; the answer cache serves repeats meanwhile.

**Consequences:**
- Worst case per upstream group: about 13 MiB on 0.26.4, against about 1.7 GiB unbounded.
- Below roughly 30,000-60,000 distinct record sets validated, a node uses up to about 10 MiB more than 0.26.3 unbounded did; past that it uses less.
- Validating handles are kept for up to 65 groups (the map is cleared beyond 64), so the total worst case scales with the groups that validate.
- A node whose re-validated working set exceeds about 4,000 signed names pays the first-validation cost (two DNSKEY lookups each) for those re-validations.
- No configuration or wire change, no upgrade order.
- Revisit if the library frees expired entries or shrinks its keys, or if a live trial shows the bound costs lookups.

**Verification:** `dns_011_offline_validation_cache_is_bounded`.

## ADR-195 — An answer is secure only when it reaches its question: the asked type at the name or at the end of its CNAME chain, or that name's own proof that it has none (Accepted)
**Context:** DNS-011. What answers a question is the record set of the asked type at the question name or, after CNAMEs (RFC 1034 §3.6.2), at the chain's last name; or the denial of it there, proven by that name's own records (RFC 4035 §5.4, RFC 5155 §8, RFC 6604). A verdict made only from the proofs of the records an answer holds would also be secure for a signed answer that holds neither, such as a CNAME without its target. One such answer is ordinary: a signed CNAME into an unsigned zone whose target has no record of the type (a content network's name without AAAA, say), whose authority section holds at most that zone's unsigned SOA.

**Decision:** on cache misses, in `validate` and `permissive` mode alike (the validator doesn't know the mode, ADR-188), for a validated answer with a secure verdict to a question of class IN whose answer section isn't empty:
- An answer with an empty answer section is left as it is (a denial, judged by its own proof). A question that doesn't follow a CNAME needs its answer at the question name: for CNAME, a CNAME there; for DS, a DS or a CNAME there; for ANY, a record there other than a signature; for NSEC or RRSIG, a record of that type there.
- For any other type, the CNAME chain is followed through the answer section from the question name (the name itself when it has no CNAME), one step per answer record at most; a loop ends it without reaching the question. A CNAME made from a DNAME is followed like any other (such an answer is bogus before this rule is reached: that CNAME has no signature of its own, ADR-188).
- The answer reaches the question when the chain's last name has a record of the asked type in the answer section; or, in a NOERROR answer, when the authority section has that name's own proof that it has none: a verified NSEC owned by it, or a verified NSEC3 owned by its hash under a zone the name is in (at most 100 iterations, as in ADR-184), whose types include neither the asked type nor CNAME, and that isn't a delegation's or a DNAME's (NS without SOA, or DNAME; RFC 4035 §5.4, RFC 5155 §8.5, RFC 6840 §4.1).
- An answer that doesn't reach the question is **indeterminate**: served as it came (DNSSEC records only for clients that set DO, records in resolution order), without AD, in both modes; counted as indeterminate. No lookup is made for it.
- Such an answer is kept 300 s at most: each record of its answer section with a longer TTL gets 300 s; a shorter TTL, and the other sections, are left as they are. The cache keeps an answer for its shortest TTL over the answer and authority sections, so it is cached, and served, for 300 s at most, and clients see those TTLs. `[cache] min_ttl` above 300 s, or serving stale answers, can keep it longer, as for any answer. Every other answer keeps its TTLs.
- Link-by-link validation (ADR-188) holds its last answer to the same rule, for the name it answers: a last answer with records that doesn't reach the question ends the walk, and the answer stays bogus (SERVFAIL with EDE 6 in `validate`).
- An answer with a record of another name than the question's made from a wildcard (a secure signature covering fewer labels than its owner has) is handled as bogus, and so validated link by link, each name for its own question: in zones signed with NSEC3 as in those signed with NSEC (ADR-188's condition for a wildcard CNAME, applied to every answer).
- In a zone signed with NSEC3, a record set at the question name made from a wildcard needs, beside it, a verified NSEC3 record of its signer's zone whose range covers the next closer name for that record set's own signature (the name cut to one label more than the signature's label count; RFC 5155 §7.2.6, §8.8), whatever else the answer holds. Without it the answer is handled as bogus, as above. An NSEC3 record of that zone with more than 100 iterations leaves the record set as it is; zones signed with NSEC keep ADR-188's rule.
- Why indeterminate. Bogus would answer SERVFAIL in `validate` where such answers are served now, and would hand them to ADR-098's proof and to link-by-link validation (ADR-188), which serves them only while one of its 4 slots is free, doesn't try a question again for a minute after a link failed, and can't succeed when the upstream doesn't answer the target (an authoritative server that answers only for its own zones): the ordinary answer above would then fail under load. Insecure would say that a zone was proven unsigned, which nothing here proves. Indeterminate is the verdict of an answer whose proof couldn't be finished: not secure, never refused (ADR-098).

**What it doesn't change:** cache hits; every verdict but secure, and the TTLs of every answer but those made indeterminate here; empty answer sections; answers that reach their question; answers found bogus before this rule (ADR-098's proof and link-by-link validation, ADR-184, ADR-188, unchanged but for the last-answer rule above); a UDP answer cut at its size, which the upstream layer asks again over TCP before validation sees it.

**Consequences:**
- An answer that doesn't reach its question is served without AD and counted as indeterminate, not secure. Nothing that was served before is refused, except a link-by-link last answer that doesn't reach its question, an answer whose wildcard record set at the name in a zone signed with NSEC3 comes without the cover of its own next closer name, and, while link-by-link validation has no free slot, the wildcard CNAME below.
- The ordinary answer above (a signed CNAME into an unsigned zone whose target lacks the type, or doesn't exist) is indeterminate. Its exact verdict is insecure; proving that takes ADR-098's proof of the target's zone, with lookups this check doesn't make.
- An answer made indeterminate here is asked again upstream once its 300 s are over, where it was kept for its own TTL. A name whose records last an hour is validated again up to 11 more times an hour while it is in use, each time with the lookups its validation needs (1 to 4 upstream questions for the test zones' answers of this kind).
- A wildcard CNAME in a zone signed with NSEC3 whose target has no record of the type stays secure when the answer carries the target's own NSEC3 record. A chain that ends in an NXDOMAIN or a wildcard's NODATA in an answer validated in one lookup is indeterminate: only a denial by the last name's own record is read here.
- A wildcard CNAME in a zone signed with NSEC3 to a name another wildcard serves is validated link by link: secure, at the cost of ADR-098's proof's lookups and the walk's; bogus while its 4 slots are busy, and for a minute after a link failed (as for zones signed with NSEC, ADR-188).
- Cost: nothing on cache hits or with validation off; on a miss, for a secure answer with records, one walk over the answer section, and for one that doesn't reach its question, one pass over the authority section with at most one NSEC3 hash per record; for each wildcard record set at the name in a zone signed with NSEC3, one NSEC3 hash per NSEC3 record of its zone. No lookup.
- Tested offline only: answers built by hand for each condition, and the signed test root's `s.t.` (signed with NSEC: chains of one and two links, a DNAME, wildcard CNAMEs, a CNAME into `z3.s.t.`), `z3.s.t.` (signed with NSEC3: a wildcard address, wildcard CNAMEs below empty non-terminals, a name below one that exists), `t.` (NSEC3) and the unsigned `v.t.`, served by a fake upstream with each answer whole, with parts of it left out, with records in another order, and with the zone's SOA in the answer section.

**Verification:** `dns_011_cname_answer_reaches_the_question`, `dns_011_only_a_secure_verdict_of_a_chain_that_stops_short_changes`; offline: `dns_011_offline_cname_answer_needs_its_chain_to_reach_the_question`, `dns_011_offline_cname_answers_that_reach_the_question_keep_their_verdicts`, `dns_011_offline_answer_needs_the_asked_type_or_its_own_denial`, `dns_011_offline_nsec3_wildcard_cname_to_another_wildcard_record_needs_each_proof`, `dns_011_offline_nsec3_wildcard_record_needs_the_cover_of_its_own_next_closer_name`, `dns_011_offline_answer_that_does_not_reach_its_question_is_kept_300_s_at_most`; unit `dns_011_an_answer_that_does_not_reach_its_question_is_kept_300_s_at_most`; ADR-188's tests, unchanged.

## ADR-191 — A change made through the API is checked against the merge the node applies; on a promoted primary the version it builds on stands for the files (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #113 (gael1980). ADR-040 says "API writes are validated against the full resulting config before they're stored" and that the files win. ADR-051 makes a primary promoted from replica build on the last cluster version it applied (`cluster/applied.json`), which sits in the files' place in the merge (`replication::unpinned`). The write checks merged onto something else: a device change onto the running configuration (and, when a stored row had the name, without the base's device of that name), every other kind onto the node's own files. On a promoted primary, renaming a device that version also has (`Newt`, made in the UI on that node earlier) kept its address on two devices: the check passed, the reload's merge failed, and the node used its base alone, so every stored entry (devices, client IDs, groups, quick rules, upstream overrides) stopped applying on every node while the reload reported success. One node with a `[[client]]` named like a stored device could do the same. The rows stayed, and the API could not remove them (404 for a stored device not in effect). The spec didn't say how the API treats the version a promoted primary builds on, or how a stored entry that isn't in effect is removed.

**Decision:**
- **One merge for the check and the reload.** A write through the API (devices; local names, forwarded domains and quick rules, ADR-042; the kinds of ADR-069; the one-of sections) is checked against the merge a reload applies: every stored entry as the write leaves it, merged (`managed::merge`) onto the base. The base is the config files, or, on a primary promoted from replica that isn't a Git source or under a Git authority, the files with that version's shared sections (`replication::managed_base`, kept in `Sources::managed_base` at start-up and by every reload, beside `file_config`). A write whose merge doesn't validate is refused before anything is stored (422 with the merge's reasons), and the configuration version doesn't move. For a device whose match key a device of the base already holds, the fuller hint names that device and where it is defined; the node that answers gives it only to whoever may read every device (people, and agents that may simulate: AGT-004, as for a quick rule's device hint), and the plain hint names none. A dry run's simulation, for a device change as for every other kind, compares the change with what the stored entries make on that base now (the base alone while they don't apply, which is what is in effect then).
- **The base stands for the files.** Its devices, local names, forwarded domains and quick rules are read-only through the API (409), as the files' are; on a promoted primary the 409 says the entry is in the version the node builds on, and that version's quick rules are listed with `source` `file`, so they aren't offered for editing and the expiry sweep leaves them alone. A stored device of a name the base has isn't in effect (the base's is): it can be deleted, or renamed to another name (the merge then decides), as before, but not changed in place. The kinds of ADR-069 override or hide the base's entry, as they do the files'.
- **The node's own files still decide what they decided.** Which plugin upstreams the files define (ADR-180), where a credential file may be and who may set a list's local file or an alert destination's credential file (ADR-179, ADR-180), and a router's key files (ADR-181) are read from the node's own files and stored entries, as before.
