Only the validation merge, and what the check takes for the configuration in effect, move to the base. So, by decision, the check that refuses a group naming a plugin upstream the merge leaves out (`left_out_guard`) reads the node's own files while the merge reads the base: on a promoted primary it can refuse such a group when only the version the node builds on defines that upstream, and what it lets through is still merged without the plugin.
- **A stored device that isn't in effect can be deleted.** `DELETE /api/v1/clients/{name}` of a stored device the configuration in effect doesn't use (the base has its name, or the stored entries don't apply) removes the row (200). While the stored entries don't apply, deleting a stored device is accepted even when they still don't apply after it, and the answer's warnings say why: it takes away nothing in effect, and two such rows must be removable one at a time. Every other write must leave a merge that applies.
- While the files couldn't be read at start-up (ADR-179), a device change is checked against the running configuration, as before.
- **Amendment (2026-11-28, Proposed): a write reads its base when it is planned.** The base is read as the next reload reads it (`replication::managed_base` over the config files, which reads the node's role and the version it last applied from disk), on the blocking thread that plans the write, not taken from the copy the last reload kept in `Sources::managed_base`: that copy lags a promotion until the reload that follows it, and a write in that moment would be checked on the node's own files. The copy now serves what the API lists (a quick rule's `source`), and a dry run's simulation compares with what the stored entries make on the base its plan read. The cost is a read of the node's cluster files for each write, off the DNS path. The fallback's log line, and a refused device change's fuller hint, name the stored devices whose places (`client[3]`) the reasons give; while the stored entries didn't apply before a device change, or when a delete is refused because something else stored names the device, the plain hint says so; and where another stored device holds a name or match key the change uses, the plain hint says to give the device a name and match keys no other device uses (people see it in the fuller hint too, with that device's name). Deleting a stored device that a device of the base hides answers (and audits) the stored row as `before`. Deleting a stored device that isn't in effect counts no recent queries (nothing in effect is relabelled). A caller that may not read every device (the rule of the fuller hints) is shown such a row by its name and `source` only (the device in effect of that name, looked up before the write, is shown whole), while the audit entry keeps it whole. For a device refusal whose reasons can name devices it can't read, such a caller gets a `detail` that says what is true without them: that the stored entries don't apply now, before the change (an operator sees why when making the change in the UI), with a hint that doesn't point at the reasons; or that the change conflicts with another device, which the answer doesn't name (the hint says what to do; a hint that points at the reasons comes without that pointer). A delete's warning that the stored entries still don't apply comes to it without the reasons, and the delete's impact then doesn't point at the warnings. A `409 cluster_pinned` carries its diff (the shared configuration the pin holds back) only to a caller that may read every device; the detail and the hint's sentences go to every caller. An agent token's Idempotency-Keys are its own.

**Not done (later designs):** rolling back a write whose reload sets the stored entries aside for a reason the check can't see (the files changed on disk since the last reload); marking the entries made through the API in the shared version, so a promoted primary can still change the devices of the version it builds on. **Amended 2026-11-28 (Proposed):** a promotion can also set the stored entries aside without any write, when they don't validate on the version the node is promoted onto. And on a promoted primary the devices, local names, forwarded domains and quick rules of the version it builds on can't be changed: not through the API (409), and not through the node's own files, whose shared sections that version replaces (CLU-006). That change is the one issue #113 asked for, so marking the entries made through the API in the shared version is proposed as the first of these designs.

**Consequences:**
- On a standalone node and on a primary that was never a replica the base is the files, and every write accepted before and applied by its reload gets the same answer (a device change's configuration to simulate lists the devices in the store's order, and keeps a file's device that a stored row of the same name hid). What changes there, only for writes whose reload didn't apply them: a change in place to a stored device that a file's device of the same name hides is refused (409; it used to be stored with no effect), and while the stored entries don't apply, a write that doesn't make them apply is refused instead of being stored to no effect. A refusal for a match key a file's device holds names that device in the fuller hint, for those who may read every device. A dry run's simulation that repairs the stored entries is run instead of answering 500.
- On a promoted primary, a write that names what only the version it builds on has (a device, an upstream, a group) is accepted, and one that names what only the node's own files have, which the reload replaces there (CLU-006), is refused.
- No wire, schema or configuration change, and nothing on the DNS path: the merge runs once more for each write.

**Verification:** `api_010_rename_onto_an_address_the_base_still_holds_is_refused`, `api_010_rename_shadowed_by_a_file_device_is_refused`, `api_002_an_accepted_plan_never_falls_back` (every kind the API writes), `clu_003_promoted_primary_checks_writes_on_its_inherited_base`, `api_010_a_device_write_on_a_node_without_an_inherited_version_is_planned_as_before`, `api_010_delete_removes_a_stored_device_not_in_effect`, `api_010_renaming_a_hidden_stored_device_to_a_free_name_applies_as_before`, `agt_004_a_group_agent_is_not_told_which_device_holds_a_key`, `clu_003_an_inherited_quick_rule_is_not_offered_as_api_and_is_not_swept`, `api_002_a_dry_run_compares_with_what_the_stored_entries_make_now`; for the amendment, `api_010_delete_of_a_hidden_stored_device_reports_the_row`, `clu_003_a_write_right_after_promotion_is_checked_on_the_inherited_base`, `clu_003_the_write_base_is_what_unpinned_merges_onto_for_every_role`, `api_010_a_fallback_names_the_stored_rows_to_delete`, `api_010_a_node_logs_the_stored_devices_its_fallback_sets_aside`, `api_010_a_hidden_row_delete_counts_no_recent_queries`, `agt_004_a_group_agent_is_shown_a_hidden_stored_row_by_its_name_only`, `agt_004_a_caller_that_may_read_every_device_is_shown_the_hidden_row`, `agt_004_a_group_agent_deleting_a_device_in_effect_is_shown_it_whole`, `agt_004_an_agent_without_querylog_read_is_shown_a_hidden_row_by_its_name_only`, `agt_003_an_agent_token_gets_its_own_stored_answer`, `agt_004_a_restricted_caller_is_not_given_the_reasons_of_rows_it_cant_read`, `agt_004_a_restricted_agent_is_told_what_is_true_in_place_of_the_reasons`, `agt_004_a_refusal_about_the_callers_own_input_keeps_its_reasons`, `agt_004_an_agent_without_querylog_read_deleting_a_device_in_effect_is_shown_it_whole`, `agt_003_an_agent_tokens_retry_replays_its_own_answer_only`, `agt_003_an_idempotency_key_is_stored_per_caller`, `agt_004_a_pinned_refusal_carries_its_diff_only_to_whoever_may_read_every_device`, `api_010_a_conflict_with_another_stored_device_says_to_use_free_keys`, `agt_004_a_restricted_caller_is_not_pointed_at_reasons_it_does_not_get`.

REQ: API-002, API-010, CLU-003, CLU-013, AGT-003, AGT-004.

## ADR-192 — The loop-detection option can be left out per upstream: `loop_tag = false`, on by default and not written while on (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #111 (cheebun): every query TelltaleDNS forwards carries its loop-detection EDNS option (code 65429 with the node's random 8-byte tag, `spec/04` §7). Some public resolvers drop any query with an EDNS option they don't know (RFC 6891 §6.1.2 asks them to ignore it), so every query to such an upstream times out and its breaker stays open. `spec/04` §7 tags every outbound query and offers no way out; the only exception for a forwarded query is a self-probe (OBS-020, T11.3), and a `recursive://` upstream asks authoritative servers without it. The tag catches a query that comes back to the node that sent it with its options intact: an upstream that is this node's own listener, or a forwarder that passes EDNS options on to it. It doesn't catch a loop between two TelltaleDNS nodes, since a node never forwards a client's options, so tagging only private addresses or only other TelltaleDNS nodes (the reporter's second suggestion) would keep no detection that matters and lose some.

**Decision:**
- A per-upstream boolean `[[upstream]] loop_tag`, default `true`. With `false`, nothing sent to that upstream carries the option: its queries, its health checks, the draft check (`POST /checks/upstream` builds from the same fields), and the lookups of its host name through its own `bootstrap` servers. The system resolvers from `/etc/resolv.conf` are shared by every hostname upstream without its own `bootstrap`, and keep it. A self-probe never carries it, as before.
- At the default nothing changes: the same bytes leave as before (the option first, then an ECS subnet when one is set).
- The upstream decides once, when it is built: one flag (`loop_tag && !self_probe`), read where the exchange encodes its query. No allocation, lock or I/O per query; the bootstrap is off the query path (its answers are cached for their TTL).
- The key is shared with the rest of `[[upstream]]` and, like `[special] resolver_arpa` (ADR-187), not written while it is on: a node one version behind refuses an `[[upstream]]` key it doesn't know (a shared version that has it is refused, and the node keeps serving the one it has), so a cluster that never sets it shares exactly what such a node reads. A node that can't read a shared version carrying the key refuses it, keeps serving the one it has, and refuses every later version while the key is in it: it stops converging until it is upgraded or the key is removed. So the key should be set only once every node runs a version that knows it (the release that ships this decision); an earlier build also refuses it in its own file. The user docs keep this rule in one place, `docs/running.md` "Setting `loop_tag` in a cluster", which the site and `spec/04` point to.
- Turning the option off doesn't fix a loop, it hides one: a query that comes back is no longer dropped, and goes round. So `false` is refused where the configuration can tell the upstream is this node: when the URL's host, or a `bootstrap` server, is a loopback address (127.0.0.0/8, `::1`, or one mapped into IPv6), an unspecified address, the name `localhost` (or a name under it), or an address a `[[listen]]` entry uses, on any port. Validation reads only the configuration, and doesn't check everything in it. Still accepted: a DNS stamp whose address is this node (only the router decodes stamps), and this node's `[api]`, `[metrics]` and `[cluster]` addresses (only `[[listen]]` is used). Not known there at all: the machine's other addresses, another name for it, and a resolver that forwards back. The draft check (`POST /checks/upstream`) builds without validating, so it can report "Works" for a body that saving refuses. The docs say all of this. Refusing on any port also refuses a forwarder on another port of this host. Each node checks the merged configuration against its own listeners, so the primary accepts an upstream at a replica's listener, and that replica refuses the version as above and stops converging while the upstream stays.
- `false` is also refused on a `recursive://` upstream (stub zones included), where it would do nothing. It stays valid on every kind that sends the query's bytes on: `udp`, `tcp`, `tls`, `https`, `h3`, `quic`, DNS stamps, and plugins (`unix://`, `exec://`).
- No new requirement ID: UPS-009 holds loop detection (`spec/04` §7).

**Not done:**
- A health hint for an upstream that times out on tagged queries but answers untagged ones (the reporter's third suggestion): it needs its own design (when to probe, and what to say).
- Tagging only some upstreams by address or kind: see Context.
- Any change to the cluster's wire protocol.

**Consequences:**
- A resolver that drops unknown options can be used, at the cost of loop detection through that upstream. The docs say that turning the option off hides a loop rather than fixing it, to keep it on for any upstream that can reach this node, and not to turn it off to quiet `forwarding loop detected`.
- The UI's upstream form doesn't offer the key; the configuration files, the API (the body has the same fields as the TOML section) and Git do. A domain forwarded in the UI or the API keeps the option; an `[[upstream]]` with the key and a `[[route]]` forward a domain without it.

**Verification:** `ups_009_loop_tag_false_leaves_the_option_out` (a query and a health check carry no option; with an ECS subnet, only the subnet; an upstream at the default beside it keeps the tag); `ups_009_loop_tag_is_on_by_default` (from the configuration and built in code); `ups_009_default_query_bytes_are_unchanged` (golden bytes from the base, for `encode_query` and for the exchange, also with DO and CD and with an ECS subnet); `ups_009_loop_tag_default_is_not_shared` (the shared part, a replica's merge, and back); `ups_009_bootstrap_follows_the_upstreams_loop_tag` (the router gives the upstream's own bootstrap the switch, the system bootstrap keeps the tag, and what the lookups send); `ups_009_loop_tag_off_is_refused_toward_this_node` (each refused form in the URL and in `bootstrap`; a public address, a LAN address no listener uses, host names with public or system bootstrap servers, and loopback with the option on are accepted); `ups_009_loop_tag_off_is_refused_where_it_does_nothing` (`recursive://` and a stub zone refused; a DNS stamp and both plugin kinds accepted); unchanged and green: `obs_020_self_probes_carry_no_loop_tag`, `loop_tagged_queries_are_dropped`. No benchmark: the exchange reads one stored flag where it negated another.

## ADR-194 — DoH behind a reverse proxy: the client's address only from listed proxies, plain HTTP only on loopback or a Unix socket, and no request answered without a client address (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #91. cheebun runs Caddy in front of TelltaleDNS. Caddy terminates TLS for DoH and has to re-encrypt to a loopback `doh` listener, because a `doh` listener without `tls` is refused (`validate.rs`, `needs_tls`). He would prefer a Unix socket. He showed that Caddy sends a PROXY v2 header over a Unix socket as TCP4 or TCP6 with the client's address, source port 0 and an all-zero destination. gael1980 runs a proxy on other machines of a private network in front of a three-node cluster. Every request appears to come from the connector's address, so access lists, rate limits and device statistics see one client. He asks for trusted `X-Forwarded-For` (or `X-Real-IP`) from listed private-network proxies, and for client IDs in device analytics.

The decisions this builds on:
- ADR-039 reads PROXY v2 before TLS and keeps the socket's address for `LOCAL`, with no source allowlist. The parser also returns no address for AF_UNSPEC and AF_UNIX.
- ADR-039's amendment of 2026-11-27: a header without an address keeps the socket address, and the settings for that address apply.
- ADR-034 reads the API's client from the rightmost `X-Forwarded-For` entry that isn't a listed proxy, and never trusts loopback for that header by itself (amendment of 2026-11-18).
- ADR-099 counts per-address connection limits on the client's address.
- ADR-149 prefers no service to plain HTTP where TLS is expected.

A plain listener behind a proxy would see only loopback, so a request without a client address must never be answered as the socket's peer. The design note is `docs/design/doh-behind-a-proxy.md`; the owner accepted its recommendations on 2026-11-27 (listed peers on other machines, the walk from the right, a forwarded loopback address refused; no `X-Real-IP`, no h2c).

**Decision:**
- **Two problems, decided separately.** (A) A `doh` listener may take the client's address from a proxy the operator lists, over TLS as today. (B) A `doh` listener may serve plain HTTP, only for a TLS-terminating proxy on the same host. Client IDs in device analytics are not part of this decision (an issue of its own).
- **New keys on `[[listen]]`, `proto = "doh"` only, all node-local** (`listen` is never replicated). Each is written only when set (`skip_serializing_if`, the ADR-187 pattern), so the serialized form of every existing listener is unchanged.
  - `trusted_proxies = [networks]`, default none. The only peers this listener accepts: a connection from any other address is closed at accept, before a byte is read. The same networks are the hops skipped in `X-Forwarded-For`. Valid only with `proxy_protocol` or `x_forwarded_for`. Required with `x_forwarded_for` on an address. A network of prefix length 0 is refused. A network wider than one host is a warning ("list only your proxies"), and so is a network a loopback listener can never see.
  - `x_forwarded_for = true`, default false. The client is the rightmost `X-Forwarded-For` entry that isn't inside `trusted_proxies`.
    - All field lines are joined in order, and at most 16 entries are read from the right.
    - The selected entry must be a bare IPv4 or IPv6 literal of at most 45 bytes. If it isn't, the request is refused. The walk never skips it to an entry further left. An IPv4-mapped IPv6 literal is the IPv4 client: only the form of `RequestMeta.peer` changes, since the rate limiter, the access list and client matching already map such addresses.
    - Entries are read as the API's walk reads them (ADR-034, amendment of 2026-11-27): per entry, trimmed of ASCII whitespace; a port, brackets, a zone id, a token, an empty entry or an entry that isn't text is unusable, and bytes left of the entry that decides never matter. The outcome differs by design: the API answers the connection's peer, a `doh` listener refuses; and the DoH walk also refuses only listed proxies, more than 16 entries, and an unspecified or loopback client.
    - Exclusive with `proxy_protocol`. `Forwarded` and `X-Real-IP` are never read, and no other DNS listener reads `X-Forwarded-For`.
    - `config check` warns when `max_connections_per_address` is left at its default.
  - `plain_http = true`, default false. No TLS. Refused together with `tls`.
    - Allowed only on a loopback address (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`) or a Unix socket.
    - Requires `proxy_protocol = true` or `x_forwarded_for = true`.
    - A `doh` listener with neither `tls` nor `plain_http` is still refused with today's message.
  - `addr` also accepts `unix:/absolute/path` (a plain listener only). An `ip:port` keeps its form. `unix_mode` (default `"0660"`) is the socket file's mode, set after bind and before listen. An existing path is replaced only if it is a socket nothing answers on: a symlink, a file or a live socket is a bind error. The file is removed when the listener closes. A world-writable mode is a warning.
- **No fallback to the socket's peer.** On a listener with any of these keys, these never reach the pipeline:
  - a connection whose PROXY header is `LOCAL`, AF_UNSPEC or AF_UNIX, or names an unspecified address (`0.0.0.0`, `::`) or a loopback one (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`, their IPv4-mapped forms), is closed;
  - a request without a usable `X-Forwarded-For` client (missing, unusable at the selected entry, only listed proxies, over 16 entries, an unspecified or loopback address) is answered 403. On HTTP/1.1 its connection is closed (`Connection: close`), so nothing behind it on the stream is read. HTTP/2 frames each request on its own: its connection, which carries the proxy's other clients, stays (the design note's R13 planned a close on both).

  Both are counted by reason (`telltale_doh_forwarded_refused_total{reason="unlisted_peer"|"no_client_address"}`), with at most one log line per listener and reason per minute, never with a header's value; the line names a refused PROXY header's kind (`read_source`). A refused header is counted there only, never in `telltale_proxy_protocol_no_address_total` (ADR-039's amendment of 2026-11-27), which counts the connections that keep the socket's address. These lines and that amendment's no-address lines go through one limiter, each on a minute of its own. TCP4 and TCP6 headers with source port 0 and an all-zero destination are accepted. Without the new keys, ADR-039's rules are unchanged (`LOCAL` keeps the socket's address).
- **The forwarded client is the client** for `[access] allowed_networks`, the rate limit, identification, the query log, analytics and dnstap: the listener sets `RequestMeta.peer` to it (port 0 for an `X-Forwarded-For` client). The per-address connection limit counts the PROXY client after the header (ADR-099). With `x_forwarded_for` it counts the socket's peer, the proxy.
- **Conflicting framing.** An HTTP/1.1 request that states its length with both `Content-Length` and `Transfer-Encoding` is framed by `Transfer-Encoding` and its connection is closed after the answer (RFC 9112 §6.3), so nothing behind it on the connection is read. hyper's HTTP/1 server does this itself (it removes `Content-Length` before the request reaches the listener), so the listener adds no check of its own; a test pins the behaviour. (The design note planned a 400 here; the effect sought, no second request read from such a connection, is the same.)
- **A plain listener speaks HTTP/1.1 only** (no h2c, no upgrade). With PROXY v2 it serves one request per connection, so a pooled connection can't carry another client's request.
- **Plain and `x_forwarded_for` listeners** send no `Alt-Svc`, take client IDs from the path only (the SNI there is the proxy's), and are skipped by the listener probes with a reason that points to `[probe] targets`. The plain listener has its own arm in `bind_stream`: it is HTTP, never the DNS-over-TCP catch-all.
- **Declined:**
  - plain HTTP on any address another machine can reach (LAN, Docker bridge, pod network, wildcard);
  - believing a forwarded address from an unlisted peer;
  - `X-Real-IP` and `Forwarded`.
- **Order:** A first (`trusted_proxies`, then `x_forwarded_for`), then B (loopback and the Unix socket together). No new requirement ID: DNS-003, DNS-020 and DNS-014 cover it.

**Implementation:** part A is built (T31.1, 2026-11-27): `trusted_proxies` and `x_forwarded_for` on TLS `doh` listeners, in `crates/telltale-net/src/forwarded.rs` and `doh.rs`. Part B (`plain_http`, `unix:` addresses, `unix_mode`, one request per connection under PROXY v2) is not built yet; until it is, a `doh` listener always needs `tls`.

**Not done:**
- h2c;
- keep-alive on plain listeners with PROXY v2 (a later ADR, after a measurement);
- `trusted_proxies` on `tcp` and `dot` listeners;
- a per-listener access list (a public DoH endpoint behind a proxy needs `allowed_networks` widened for every listener);
- client IDs from the `Host` header;
- peer-credential checks on the Unix socket;
- detecting a proxy that passes a client's `X-Forwarded-For` through unchanged;
- device analytics keyed by client ID (its own issue);
- a fuzz target for the PROXY header parser (`proxy_header`).

**Consequences:**
- A proxy on other machines gives each client its own address while the hop stays encrypted. A layer-4 balancer can be the only peer a listener accepts. With part B, a proxy on the same host needs no second TLS hop.
- Once a listener sees real clients, `allowed_networks` and the rate limit apply to them: clients outside your allowed networks are refused (EDE 18), where before they shared the proxy's address.
- A client on the proxy's own host is refused through the proxy and uses the local listeners. A proxy health check that sends `LOCAL` or asks the DoH path is refused: a TCP check or the web UI's `/livez` is the documented alternative.
- Everything on `trusted_proxies` is trusted to name any client: it can choose a client's policy, spend its rate budget on every transport, and write its address into the log. On a loopback plain listener that is every process on the host, which a Unix socket with mode `0660` narrows to the proxy's group.
- Listener settings never leave a node, so mixed-version clusters are unaffected: the shared configuration is the same bytes with or without the keys. An older binary refuses its own file if it contains the new keys (at start it doesn't run; on a reload it keeps the running configuration), so they must be removed before a downgrade.
- Per-query cost:
  - existing DoH listeners: one predictable branch at accept and one per request;
  - `x_forwarded_for` listeners: an allocation-free walk of at most 16 short entries per request;
  - plain listeners with PROXY v2: one loopback or Unix connection per query instead of a TLS session.

  UDP, TCP, DoT, DoQ, DoH3 and the pipeline are unchanged.
- Updated: `docs/running.md` ("Behind a reverse proxy", under Encrypted DNS for your devices), `docs/configuration.md`, `site/guide-encrypted.html`, `site/guide-monitoring.html`, `docs/config-schema.json`, `spec/08` (DoH through an ingress), the support package's classification table (`listen[].trusted_proxies[]`; `listen[].unix_mode` with part B). No API operation, MCP tool or UI change.

**Verification (part A):**
- Configuration: `dns_003_x_forwarded_for_needs_trusted_proxies`, `dns_003_proxy_protocol_and_x_forwarded_for_are_exclusive`, `dns_003_trusted_proxies_only_with_a_forwarded_source`, `dns_003_trusted_proxies_refuses_everyone_and_warns_on_networks`, `dns_003_forwarding_keys_keep_the_tls_requirement`, `dns_003_new_listener_keys_not_written_at_default`, `clu_003_listener_keys_never_shared`, `ops_011_support_package_classifies_the_new_listener_fields`.
- The walk: `dns_003_xff_rightmost_untrusted_entry`, `dns_003_xff_unparsable_entry_is_not_skipped`, `dns_003_xff_reads_entries_as_the_api_does` (the API's `api_004_xff_*` cases), `dns_003_xff_walk_is_bounded`, `dns_020_forwarded_unspecified_or_loopback_is_not_a_client`, `dns_003_trusted_networks_match_by_prefix`, `dns_003_refusal_lines_at_most_once_a_minute`, `dns_003_xff_selector_allocates_nothing`; the nightly fuzz target `forwarded_for`.
- Listener: `dns_003_xff_only_from_listed_peers`, `dns_020_proxy_v2_only_from_listed_peers`, `dns_020_local_refused_once_trusted_proxies_is_set`, `dns_020_refused_headers_are_counted_as_refusals_only`, `dns_020_a_refused_header_is_named_by_kind`, `dns_020_each_listener_line_has_its_own_minute`, `dns_020_local_keeps_socket_address_without_new_keys`, `dns_020_forwarded_unspecified_or_loopback_refused`, `dns_020_proxy_v2_port_zero_and_empty_destination_accepted`, `dns_003_xff_missing_or_unusable_refused`, `dns_003_xff_ignored_without_the_key`, `dns_003_forwarded_listener_closes_after_a_refused_request`, `dns_003_xff_listener_closes_after_conflicting_framing`, `dns_003_plain_and_xff_listeners_send_no_alt_svc`, `dns_003_xff_listener_ignores_sni_client_ids`, `dns_003_client_id_from_path_survives_forwarding`, `dns_003_per_address_limit_counts_the_proxy_with_xff`.
- Server and pipeline: `dns_014_rate_limit_counts_the_forwarded_client`, `dns_014_access_list_applies_to_the_forwarded_client`, `obs_001_query_log_records_the_forwarded_client`, `obs_020_probes_skip_listeners_that_need_a_forwarded_address`.
- Kept unchanged: `dns_003_doh_get_post_client_ids_and_errors`, `dns_003_doh_http1_and_proxy_protocol`, `dns_004_doh_advertises_http3`, `dns_020_parses_v4_v6_and_local`, `dns_020_rejects_bad_headers`.
- Part B, when built: `dns_003_plain_doh_needs_opt_in`, `dns_003_plain_doh_loopback_or_unix_only`, `dns_003_plain_doh_requires_a_client_address_source`, `dns_003_plain_doh_not_with_tls`, `dns_003_plain_doh_http1_only`, `dns_020_plain_doh_local_or_unix_family_refused`, `dns_020_plain_doh_proxy_v2_one_request_per_connection`, `dns_003_plain_doh_listener_speaks_http`, `dns_003_unix_socket_mode_set_before_listen`, `dns_003_unix_socket_never_replaces_a_file_or_a_live_socket`, `dns_003_unix_socket_removed_on_close`, `dns_020_proxy_v2_over_unix_socket_tcp4_family_accepted`.
- Benchmarks: `make bench-smoke` and the DoH row of `make bench-transports` against a same-binary control on the benchmark machine (not run for part A; see the design note, §11).

## ADR-198 — An active check is the half-open probe once the backoff has run out (Proposed)
**Context:** GitHub issue #116 and pull request #117 (Greg Pakes): `spec/04` §5 opens an upstream's breaker for 10 s (doubling to 5 minutes) and then lets one probe through. Only a query's attempt order (`Group::attempt_order` → `Health::admit`) moved an open breaker to half-open. The active checks keep querying an idle upstream, and `lib.rs` says this is so that "a recovered or idle upstream's health stays current", but `Health::observe` ignores every outcome while the breaker is open. So an upstream that no query reached stayed benched for good after it had recovered, while every check succeeded. Seen on 0.8.1: a three-node cluster whose gateway rebooted. All three nodes benched their one upstream (NextDNS over DoH, the only member of `default`). The two replicas, which serve the LAN, closed it within two minutes. The controller pod, which forwarded no client queries, stayed open for 22 hours, with about 60 successful checks an hour, and reported SEVERE `upstream_group_down` ("no upstream in group default is answering") the whole time. The first query to reach it closed it at once.
**Decision:** `Upstream::health_check` asks `Health::admit` first. Once the backoff has run out, that moves the breaker to half-open with the check as its probe, so the check's outcome closes the breaker, or opens it again with the backoff doubled, exactly as a query's probe does. While the breaker is closed, `admit` changes nothing. Before the backoff has run out, the check's outcome still counts in the upstream's statistics (its requests and failures, the rolling window and the latency average), as before, but doesn't move the breaker. While a query's probe is out, the check takes no second probe, and its outcome counts as it always has: whichever of the two is recorded first settles the breaker.
**Consequences:** an idle upstream that recovered is back in use within about a minute of its backoff ending (a check's own outcome counts as use, so an idle upstream is checked on every other 30 s tick), not at the next query, which on a node without clients may never come. Health findings and `telltale_upstream_breaker_state` reflect the upstream rather than the node's traffic. A check that fails as the probe doubles the backoff, up to 5 minutes, as a query's failed probe does. The check proves that the upstream answers `. NS`, often from its cache, not that it resolves other names: a router whose own uplink is down can pass it, and the queries that then fail can open it again. An upstream that doesn't answer `. NS` with NOERROR stays benched for longer on a lightly used node, since each of its checks opens the breaker again with the backoff doubled: a server that refuses it, such as an authoritative-only server, belongs in a stub zone (DNS-022: `stub_zone` in the file, `"stub": true` through the API), and a plugin upstream should answer it. Nothing changes on the query path: a check takes one lock more, about once a minute per idle upstream, and sends nothing that wasn't sent before.

**Verification:** `ups_006_active_check_closes_a_recovered_idle_upstream`, `ups_006_the_check_loop_closes_an_idle_benched_upstream` (the check loop by itself, with no query: the case of issue #116), `ups_006_failed_active_check_doubles_the_backoff`, `ups_006_the_check_takes_the_one_probe_and_a_query_is_still_answered` (a query meanwhile is not admitted, and a group of one still answers it); green with and without this decision, as they pin behaviour it keeps: `ups_006_active_check_waits_for_the_backoff`, `ups_006_a_check_settles_the_breaker_while_a_querys_probe_is_out`, `ups_006_a_check_leaves_a_closed_breaker_alone`, `ups_006_failed_probes_double_the_backoff_up_to_five_minutes` (20 s to the 300 s cap, then 10 s again after a success). The tests' helper records each failure with the 400 ms timeout, so a check in them has a 400 ms timeout. No benchmark: the change is off the query path.

REQ: UPS-006.

## ADR-197 — A query waiting on another's upstream request takes its outcome; the request is tried once more for the waiting queries, within their time (Proposed)
**Context:** DNS-006, `spec/03` §4: identical concurrent misses share one upstream request (a singleflight map keyed like the cache); the first query leads it and the others wait on it. When the leader got no answer, each waiting query resolved again on its own, with a new budget. A question an upstream sends back to this node as a query of its own (a forwarder in front of the node that builds its own query, so the option of `spec/04` §7 doesn't come along; or an upstream set to leave the option out, ADR-192) waits on the request that sent it.

**Decision:** a waiting query takes its leader's outcome; the leader's own failed try is tried once more within the time left; a request makes at most two tries.
- A query waiting on a request takes the leader's outcome: its answer, or its failure (a stale answer where one may be served, otherwise SERVFAIL, as the leader's own client gets).
- Each query that joins a request carries its deadline: the query budget plus the 100 ms its client waits beyond it, from when it started waiting. A leader whose own try gets no answer tries once more, within the time left until the latest of its followers' deadlines, when at least 100 ms is left. That second try is still the shared request: a query that joins it meanwhile takes its outcome; if it answers while the leader's own client still waits, that client gets the answer too. There is no third try, and a leader that no query joined doesn't try again.
- A leader that goes away without an outcome (its caller is cancelled, as a DNS64 lookup is when its DoH client disconnects) leaves its followers to resolve on their own, each within the time left until its own deadline, and only with at least 100 ms left.
- A shortened try (the second one, or that of a follower whose leader went away) runs with the configured budget and is cut from outside when its time is up. Nothing shared with other queries learns the shorter time: the DNSSEC validator keeps the budget of a group's first lookup for every later lookup of that group, an attempt's timeout feeds its upstream's latency average and breaker, and a failed unsigned-zone proof is remembered for a minute.
- A failed request leaves the map before its failure is published, so a query that arrives after it leads a request of its own.

**Alternatives considered:**
- *The follower takes the leader's outcome, and nothing is tried again.* One upstream query per miss in the case above, and the smallest change. Rejected: with one upstream, the leader's only try may wait the whole budget (ADR-165); when that one query or its answer is lost, every query that joined it would get SERVFAIL, where each used to ask again and be answered.
- *The follower resolves again on its own, within its own remaining time instead of a new budget.* No bound in the case above: the question that comes back starts as a new client query with a whole budget of its own.
- *Caching resolution failures for a short time* (RFC 9520 §3.2): a possible later step. It changes what new queries get after a failure and needs its own design (a cached failure must not hide a stale answer, DNS-007).

**What it doesn't change:** cache hits; a leader that gets an answer, and the queries waiting on it (they take it, as before); a miss no other query joined (it fails as before, without a second try); no configuration key, no wire or API change.

**Consequences:**
- A request makes at most two upstream tries, however many queries join it. A question an upstream sends back to this node ends with the request it waits on, and every resolution is done by the latest deadline of the queries waiting on it.
- Queries whose leader failed share one second try instead of making one each.
- A query that joins a request during its second try takes that try's outcome, even when its own deadline is later: if that try fails too, it gets SERVFAIL (or a stale answer) then, where it used to make a request of its own.
- A follower that joined within a few milliseconds of its leader has about 100 ms left when the leader's try ends at the budget, so whether the second try is made for it turns on those milliseconds.
- The leader's own client gets the second try's answer when it lands within its wait (it used to get SERVFAIL). When the second try fails too, that client is answered up to 100 ms later than before, at the end of its wait, and only when a follower's time made the leader try again.
- A shortened try that runs out of time emits no upstream event, and its queries still out are dropped rather than left to finish; the leader's first try has already been counted in the upstream's health.
- Cost: on the follower path, one deadline compared under the lock `join` already takes; on the leader's failure path, one more map lookup under that lock and a timer for the second try. Nothing on cache hits or on the leader's path when it gets an answer.

**Verification:** `dns_006_singleflight_coalesces_identical_misses` (a follower takes the answer; a dropped leader leaves it to resolve), `dns_006_a_failed_request_gives_every_waiting_query_its_outcome` (100 followers, one failure; a later query leads its own), `dns_006_a_second_try_runs_within_the_waiting_queries_time` (no follower, no second try; the latest deadline, never a new budget), `dns_006_no_second_try_with_less_than_the_least_time_left`; through the pipeline: `dns_006_an_upstream_that_sends_questions_back_ends_with_the_miss`, `dns_006_many_queries_for_one_name_share_one_request` (20 clients), `dns_006_a_follower_whose_leader_ran_out_of_time_gets_the_second_try`, `dns_006_no_second_try_with_less_than_100_ms_left`, `dns_006_a_follower_takes_its_leaders_answer`, `dns_006_a_follower_of_an_abandoned_leader_resolves_within_its_own_time`, `dns_006_a_second_try_leaves_the_validation_budget_as_configured` and `dns_006_an_abandoned_follower_leaves_the_validation_budget_as_configured` (DNSSEC on: a shortened try as a fresh validator's first lookup, then a name that takes longer than that try had is answered).

REQ: DNS-006, DNS-011.

## ADR-172 — A cluster support package: each node redacts its own part under one key, with labels every node agrees on (Accepted)
**Context:** GitHub issue #55 (the later phase of #50, ADR-156): in a cluster the problem is often on another node, and a package of the node that answered covers only that one. ADR-156 settled the direction (each node redacts before its data leaves it, the asking node's key travels over the mutually authenticated stream) and left three points: how the key reaches the others, what an older peer does, and the size cap across nodes. One more came up in building it: ADR-156 numbers a name by when it is first met (`device-3`), so two nodes would give one device two labels, and the issue asks that a device have one name on every node.
**Decision:**
- `POST /api/v1/system/support-package` takes `cluster: true`; `telltale support-package --cluster`; a checkbox on the System page. It is the node that was asked that puts the package together. Admin only, no agent scope, no MCP tool, like the single-node package.
- That node makes a random 256-bit key for the package and sends it in a new read (`SupportCollect`) over the cluster stream to every reachable member. The key is held in memory for the call and never stored or logged. A member that is ephemeral (a resolver pod) may be asked but may not ask: the key lets its holder undo the address permutation, so only a member the cluster trusts with more than reads may hold one.
- Each node makes its normal package (collectors, redaction, classification, the same limits) under that key, but with **keyed labels**: a name's label is `device-` and eight hex digits of a keyed hash of the name (a few more if two names land on one label), not a number by first encounter. The same name is the same label on every node, in any order, and under another key it is another label. Addresses and MAC addresses were already a keyed permutation. A single-node package keeps its numbers.
- A node sends back its **part**: its files as text, its manifest, and the counts, gzip-compressed JSON. The cluster channel carries about a mebibyte per answer, so the node cuts its largest files until the compressed part fits 1,000,000 bytes (the manifest says what was cut), and the asking node reads it with a limit on the unpacked size.
- The asking node puts the parts side by side (`nodes/<node>/...` with each node's own `MANIFEST.json`) under a summary `MANIFEST.json` (`kind: cluster`) and a README. The size cap is the single-node 20 MiB divided into equal shares, one per reachable member; a part over its share, or a file whose path is not allowed, is left out and said so. A node that doesn't answer (45 s), is older than this feature (it doesn't know the read, so it answers an error), is busy making another package, or has no open stream is listed with a **fixed reason**: nothing a peer says is copied into the package. Real node names appear only at the `none` level; otherwise the asking node names a missing node with the same keyed labels.
- No pseudonym map: the names are replaced on the nodes they belong to, and a map would have to carry them over the network, so `keepMap` with `cluster` is refused (400) and `--keep-map` with `--cluster` is an error.
**Alternatives rejected:** numbering by first encounter and renaming at the asking node (the asking node would hold every node's names, which is what the design avoids); sending each node's data to the asking node to redact (the unredacted data would cross the network); a map the asking node assembles (same); a separate large-transfer channel for parts (the cap and the compression keep a part under a mebibyte, and a node that can't fit is listed, not hidden).
**Consequences:**
- A cluster package is made with one request, and a device reads the same in every node's files. The labels are hashes, so they say less about order than `device-3`; the manifest and README explain them.
- Two different names that hash to one label get different labels on the node that sees both, but another node that sees only one of them may give it the shorter label: a rare ambiguity, harmless to reading.
- An older node is listed as missing until it is upgraded; a rolling upgrade gives a partial package for a while, and says which nodes.
- Each part is redacted the same way a single-node package is (best effort for free text), and the README says so.
**Verification:** pure tests in `telltale-support` (labels the same in any order and under one key, different under another; a part survives the wire and a damaged or oversized one is refused; a part is cut to fit; the package lists present, missing, and over-share nodes; paths that escape a node's folder are refused; two nodes with one name get two folders), the request checks, and the existing single-node package tests unchanged.
## ADR-171 — The query log can be searched for what does not match: a "not" per filter, and a read an older peer doesn't know (Accepted)
**Context:** GitHub issue #58: a user asked for "does not contain" in the query log search. The name filter matches in five ways (contains, exactly, with subdomains, wildcard, regex) and the other filters (client, status, type, response code) are "is one of"; nothing says "everything except", and a regex can't (the engine runs in linear time and has no lookaround). The analysis queries already have `!=` and "not in".
**Decision:**
- Five filters can be turned around: name (each of its five matches), client, status, type, and response code. They are typed booleans (`nameNot`, `clientNot`, `statusNot`, `qtypeNot`, `rcodeNot`) on `GET /api/v1/queries`, the first four on `GET /api/v1/queries/stream`; `--not-name`, `--not-client`, `--not-status`, `--not-qtype` on `telltale qlog search`; the Query log page's Match menu ("does not contain", "is not", "is not, or under", "does not match wildcard", "does not match regex"), a "not" box beside Client, Type, and Rcode, and a "not" chip before the statuses. Group, upstream, and slowness are not turned around. The Live view honours them (not regex, which it never had).
- A flag without its filter is a 400 that names it (as `match` without `name` is), not ignored. The page drops such a flag from an old address instead of sending it. A negated `rcode` keeps the queries that got no response code.
- The store's `Filter` carries a `Not` (five flags). The name and client predicates are resolved against each segment's dictionary into a yes/no per name, so a negated one only flips that set: no extra work per row. What a negation gives up is skipping by bloom filter (a bloom says "maybe present", never "absent from every row"): a negated name or client reads every block the time range and the other filters leave, as "contains", wildcard, and regex already do. A block is skipped for a negated status or response code only when every value in it is excluded (the header masks). Limits, threads, and the idle priority are the existing ones; the docs say to give a time range on a long log.
- In a cluster, peers decode `QueryParams` and `TailParams` leniently, so an older peer would ignore the new flags and answer rows that were not negated into a negated search. A negated search is sent as a new read (`QueriesNegated`, `TailOpenNegated`) that an older peer doesn't know, so it is listed among the nodes not reached (as other newer reads are) and never answers wrongly.
- The address keeps the existing keys and adds the flags (`match=exact&nameNot=true`): an old link keeps meaning what it did, and an older page opening a new link shows the un-negated search.
**Not done:** negating group, upstream, or slowness; combining filters with OR; lookaround in regex.
**Consequences:**
- A negated name search costs about what a "contains" search does; a negated exact name no longer gets the one-block shortcut.
- The OpenAPI document, the UI types, and the MCP tool schema (`search_queries`) gain five optional booleans.
- During a rolling upgrade, a negated search of the whole cluster reaches only the upgraded nodes.

## ADR-170 — Crates off the DNS path are built for size (Accepted)
**Context:** After ADR-168 and ADR-169 the images were 13.39, 12.66, and 13.10 MiB of the 15 MiB gate. The 2026-11-17 review found that building the crates that are not on the DNS path with `opt-level = "s"` instead of 3 makes the binary smaller, and asked for a benchmark first, because performance ranks above footprint. The owner chose to take it if it was safe ("Yes", 2026-11-17) and to measure SQLite's C code at `-Os` separately.
**Measured (the build VM; release profile with fat LTO; binaries built from 938c9a9, stripped and gzipped as a stand-in for the layer):**
- **The off-path list** (26 package specs in the measurement, `rusqlite` among them): 13,116 to 12,746 KiB, -0.36 MiB. Of the 1,737 functions in the DNS-path crates (`telltale-proto`, `-net`, `-cache`, `-filter`, `-policy`, `-telemetry`, `-upstream`), 1,724 are identical in size and 13 differ: startup and configuration code (the preset deserializers, a once-cell initializer, a telemetry drain), none per query. Rotated `bench-smoke` with an identical-binary control, five rounds, cache-hot median: baseline 526k queries/s, control 500k (-5.0%), this list 510k (-3.2%), inside the control's spread. Miss-heavy: flat.
- **The same list plus `telltale-config`:** -1.0 MiB in all, but `ClientTable::identify` (per query) grew from 3,428 to 4,039 bytes, the query handler changed, and cache-hot throughput was 474k (-9.9%, median p50 95 µs against 55). Not taken.
- **SQLite's C code (`libsqlite3-sys`) at `-Os`:** a further -0.31 MiB, but on the rollup store's own tables and queries (`hour_top` and `rollup_minute`, in memory, a copy of the binary as control within 0.5%) the dashboard's week-wide top list was 10.5% slower, minute-blob range reads 11%, hourly point reads 22%, and bulk insert 38%. Not taken.
- **The `rusqlite` wrapper at `"s"`:** top list -0.8%, blobs -0.8%, bulk insert +0.5%, point reads +4.6% (the control's spread was about 4%). The saving is small and it is on that same query path. Not taken.
**Decision:** the 25 package specs in `[profile.release.package.*]` of the root `Cargo.toml` (the HTTP API and its OIDC, OpenAPI, and certificate parts, cluster replication, the git source, the support package, and the config-file parsers) are built with `opt-level = "s"`. `telltale-config`, `rusqlite`, `libsqlite3-sys`, and every crate that runs per query stay at the release profile's 3. `crates/telltale-config/tests/profile.rs` fails if a hot-path crate is added to the list, if an entry is not exactly `opt-level = "s"`, or if an entry no longer matches `Cargo.lock` (a `name@version` entry that a dependency update outdates is only a cargo warning, and the saving would be gone without anyone noticing).
**Consequences:**
- About 0.35 MiB less on every architecture (the size gate in CI prints the real numbers).
- The API, OIDC, and replication code is somewhat slower per call. It runs per request and per replication message, not per DNS query, and the dashboard's own queries run in SQLite, which is unchanged.
- A new crate on the DNS path is not on the list by default; one that is added to the list has to pass the test, which names the crates known to be hot.

## ADR-169 — A compact zoneinfo and SQLite without its unused modules (Accepted)
**Context:** ADR-168 left two low-risk cuts undecided. The owner chose both on 2026-11-17 ("do items 3 and 4 too") after T23.1 took the images to 13.83, 13.08, and 13.54 MiB. The review had measured them on amd64: a slim zoneinfo -0.25 MiB, SQLite without full-text search and the R-tree index -0.20 MiB. Neither is on the DNS path.
**Decision:**
- The image's zoneinfo is built by `zic -b fat` from the package's own `tzdata.zi` (Dockerfile) instead of copied from `/usr/share/zoneinfo`. Debian's package ships the database three times (the main directory, `posix/`, and `right/`, which carries leap seconds); the build writes it once, with each name a hard link to one file, and copies it with `cp -a` so the layer stores each file once. Measured with tar and gzip on the build VM (tzdata 2025b): 347 KB for the distribution's directory, 151 KB built this way. The build fails if a zone name the distribution's directory has is missing from it (the host's own `localtime` and `posixrules` links and the two extra trees aside). jiff is the only reader (schedule time zones, FLT-010). `crates/telltale-config/tests/zoneinfo.rs` builds a directory the same way from the host's `tzdata.zi` and checks that jiff reads every file, that every name is present, and that the offset, abbreviation, and daylight-saving flag agree with the host's directory for every zone (weekly) and for twelve zones with every kind of rule (every six hours), from 2020 to 2075.
- **`zic -b slim` was built, tested, and is not used.** It is 87 KB, 0.06 MiB smaller still, but jiff 0.2.37 refuses one of its 598 files: `America/Ojinaga` from tzdata 2025b ("expected last transition in TZif file to have DST offset matching the offset derived from the POSIX time zone transition rule"), so a schedule in that zone would be rejected as an unknown time zone. The first version of this change was slim; the test found it. The fat files, which are what distributions ship, are all accepted. If a later jiff accepts the slim file, the 0.06 MiB can be taken, and the test is the check.
- SQLite is built without FTS3, FTS5, the R-tree index, and the dbstat table, by `LIBSQLITE3_FLAGS` (`-U` flags, which the bundled SQLite's build script applies after its own `-D` defaults). The flags are in the repository's `.cargo/config.toml`, not in the Dockerfile, so `cargo test` runs against the SQLite the image ships and anyone building from source gets the same one. `crates/telltale-store/tests/sqlite_build.rs` asserts the four modules are gone and that foreign keys on by default, the JSON functions, STAT4, column metadata, and thread safety stay. No code here uses a virtual table, `MATCH`, or dbstat. A real image build confirmed it: none of the four option strings is in the shipped binary, and STAT4, column metadata, and the foreign-key default are.
**Not done:** a slim zoneinfo (above); dropping the other optional parts of the bundled build (load-extension support, soundex, API armor): they cost little and are harder to be sure of.
**Consequences:**
- About 0.4 MiB less on amd64. A real image build with the slim variant printed 13.32 MiB against 13.83; the fat variant adds about 0.06 MiB. The size gate in CI prints the real numbers.
- A zone that only a `posix/` or `right/` path named (nothing here uses one) is no longer in the image. A time zone added to the database later is picked up by rebuilding the image, as before.
- A SQLite file that defines a full-text or R-tree table still opens (SQLite looks a module up when the table is used); using such a table fails with "no such module".
- `spec/08` §2 says the image carries a compact zoneinfo.

## ADR-168 — A leaner image: the OpenAPI document compiled in, a gzip-only embedded UI, no CA bundle (Accepted)
**Context:** The image gate is 15 MiB compressed per architecture (NFR-001). At 13bb27d the images were 14.84, 14.16, and 14.82 MiB (amd64, arm64, armv7), so one more feature of the size of the domain icons (+0.11 MiB) would have failed the gate on amd64 and armv7. A measured review (2026-11-17: the compressed layer sizes the gate sums, three release builds on the build VM, the BuildKit CI uses) found where the bytes are. The binary is about 97% of the image. Of it, utoipa's OpenAPI document builder was about 0.6 MiB, and the 677 KB document was rebuilt at every request. The embedded UI cost about twice its gzip size, because the build kept a raw copy beside each `.gz` and the layer's compression can only shrink the raw one. And a CA bundle (0.12 MiB) that no code reads.
**Decision (the owner chose these, 2026-11-17):**
- `GET /api/v1/openapi.json` serves `docs/api/openapi.json`, compiled in with `include_str!`, not a document built from `ApiDoc` while running. `api_001_committed_openapi_matches_the_code` still builds it from the code (a test-only function) and keeps the file equal to it; `UPDATE_OPENAPI=1` regenerates the file.
- `npm run build` replaces each text file in `ui/dist` with its `.gz` when that is smaller (`ui/scripts/postbuild.mjs`). The server sends the stored file unchanged to a client that accepts gzip, and inflates it for any other (curl without `--compressed`, a probe) with `miniz_oxide`, which is already in the tree. A file with no `.gz` is served as stored, and a damaged `.gz` is refused (500), never served half-read. This amends ADR-030's size-and-caching bullet ("so no compression crate on the server"): the server now has an inflater, and no new crate.
- The image has no CA bundle (`/etc/ssl/certs/ca-certificates.crt`). Every TLS client (upstream DoT and DoH, list fetches, event sinks, SMTP) verifies against the Mozilla roots compiled in with `webpki-roots`; none reads the system store, and `rustls-native-certs` is not in the lockfile. A private CA is set with `tls_ca` as before, which doesn't depend on the bundle's path.
**Not done (reviewed):** zstd layers (about 2.1 MiB smaller, but Docker before 23.0 and containerd before 1.5 can't pull them, and Debian 12's `docker.io` is 20.10); removing `.eh_frame` (the watchdog's stack capture unwinds with it, ADR-163); the linker's identical-code-folding (the cross compiler rejects it); zopfli re-compression of the layer (pipeline and provenance cost); a smaller zoneinfo and SQLite without its unused full-text and R-tree modules (ADR-169); building crates off the DNS path for size (decided after a benchmark).
**Consequences:**
- The three changes took the images from 14.84, 14.16, and 14.82 MiB to 13.83, 13.08, and 13.54 (amd64, arm64, armv7; read from the size gate of the CI image job on 3027e71; measured per item on the build VM for amd64: OpenAPI -0.60, UI -0.29, CA bundle -0.12), and every future UI file costs about half what it did.
- Serving the OpenAPI document is faster: no 677 KB document is built per request. The served document can't differ from the committed one, and the drift test is what keeps the committed one equal to the code.
- A client that doesn't accept gzip costs one inflate of at most about 700 KB per request (rare: browsers always accept gzip).
- `spec/08` §2 no longer lists a CA bundle in the image.

## ADR-167 — Domain icons: a brand tile or a monogram, drawn locally and never fetched (Accepted)
**Context:** A user asked for icons left of domain names, from `https://icons.duckduckgo.com/ip3/<domain>.ico`. The owner declined any dependency on a third-party icon service (2026-11-16). Measured that day: the endpoint is unofficial (no published terms, uptime, or rate limit), serves ICO only, and answers a 404 with a grey placeholder for a subdomain or a name with no site (`api.github.com`, `time.nist.gov`); and every icon request tells the service which names are in the query log, which shows work names, internal names, and trackers. The UI's content security policy allows images only from `'self'` and `data:`, and at privacy level 1 and up names are hashed (ADR-102), so there would be nothing to look up. Fetching from the sites themselves, from the server, would connect a resolver host to names any client chooses, many of them blocked or malicious (an SSRF and egress risk; review 06-02 fixed one of this kind). A lab on a synthetic demo network, in light and dark and at 390 px, showed the design below on the real lists.
**Decision (the owner chose it on that lab, with rounded shapes, 2026-11-16):**
- The UI draws a mark left of a domain name in the dashboard's Top domains and Top blocked lists and in the query log's name column. All of it is drawn in the browser from data already in the UI: no request is made for an icon, the content security policy is unchanged, nothing about queries leaves the page, and the DNS path and the API are untouched.
- A name that belongs to a brand in the map gets the brand's tile: its colour and glyph from Simple Icons (CC0-1.0, pinned at 16.38.0; the marks are their owners' trademarks and only identify the name). The map is curated, from a list in `ui/scripts/brand-icons.mjs`: 107 of the 133 brands listed have a glyph, mapped from 228 domains and hosts (CDN names too: `nflxso.net` is Netflix). The longest matching suffix of a name, on whole labels, wins, so `notnetflix.com` is not Netflix. The script writes the committed data files (`brands.ts`, `map.data.ts`): a build never needs the network or the package.
- Every other name gets a monogram: the first letter of its owner's label, in a tint of a hue taken from a hash of the registrable domain (one lightness and one chroma for every hue, so none outweighs another; both themes). A name of the network itself (`.arpa`, `.lan`, `.local`, `.home`, `.internal`, `.svc`, one label) gets a quiet house. A name the privacy level hid (`h` and 16 hex digits) gets nothing, and must not pass for a name of the network.
- A blocked name wears the same mark with most of its colour drained. A near-black mark flips to a light tile in the dark theme.
- The glyphs are a separate lazy chunk (about 120 KB raw, 51 KiB gzipped), fetched when the first brand tile is needed. Monogram only and Off never fetch it.
- The choice is Settings → Account → Domain icons: a style (Brand and monogram, the default; Monogram only; Off) and a size (Default, 20 px; Small, 16 px). It is kept per user in the browser (`telltale.icons.<user>`), like the detail level: no server state, no API, no configuration, nothing in the cluster.
**Consequences:**
- The open set has dropped several large companies' marks (Microsoft, Amazon and AWS, LinkedIn, Slack, Disney+, Hulu, OpenAI, Xbox, Minecraft, Nintendo, Adobe, the BBC, Bing; 25 of the 133 listed brands), so their names wear a monogram. 20 of the 27 service presets get a brand tile. A name outside the map is a monogram, never a guess.
- The map is data we maintain: a brand is added to the list and the data regenerated. `docs/third-party-notices.md` carries the Simple Icons notice and the trademark disclaimer.
- Cost: about 6 KiB gzipped in the main chunk and the 51 KiB lazy chunk (the UI is 295.6 KiB of its 400 KiB budget); no server work.
- Other pages (Clients, a device, Analyze, Explain, Flow, the Traffic map, the Wall) don't carry the mark yet.

**Amended 2026-11-17 (the owner: "go on adding the bootstrap icons pack as well as long as it doesn't create too much overhead"):** a second pack, for the marks the first one dropped.
- [Bootstrap Icons](https://icons.getbootstrap.com) 1.13.2 (MIT) is the second choice: when Simple Icons has none of a brand's candidate slugs, the generator takes the Bootstrap glyph named with a `bi:` prefix in the list. Eleven of the 25 missing brands have one: Microsoft (also used for Office, Azure, and Edge names, which have no mark of their own), Teams, Windows, Xbox, Bing, Skype, LinkedIn, Amazon, Slack, OpenAI, Nintendo. Disney+, Hulu, Minecraft, Adobe, the BBC, Walmart, and AWS are in neither pack and stay monograms. A glyph with no colour gets a brand colour chosen by hand in the generator (`BOOTSTRAP`), and its path sits on a 16 grid, so the data carries the grid size when it isn't 24 and the component reads it.
- Overhead: none at run time, and the chunk is smaller. The generator now minifies path data with svgo to two decimals (a third of the bytes, under 1% difference at 16 and 20 px), which more than pays for the eleven added glyphs: the glyph data is 86.9 KiB raw and 35.4 KiB gzipped (it was about 118 and 51 for 107 glyphs). svgo, and both packs, are used only when the generator runs (`npm i --no-save`, pinned in the script, checked by it); nothing is added to `package.json`, to the build, or to the image.
- `docs/third-party-notices.md` carries the Bootstrap Icons licence; the developer page `site/dev-icons.html` has the recipe for adding a brand or a pack.

## ADR-166 — UniFi: name offline clients from the console's client history and configured clients (Accepted)
**Context:** T8.2, ADR-080 ("not done: UniFi's offline known clients"). `stat/sta` lists only connected clients, so a device that has since left (a phone off Wi-Fi, a laptop asleep) loses its name: its queries in the log and the top lists show a bare address, though UniFi still has its alias.
**Decision (proposed):**
- After `stat/sta`, each UniFi poll also reads the v2 client history (`/v2/api/site/{site}/clients/history?withinHours=<offline_days × 24>`, Network 7+, the clients the console remembers within the window, with their `last_ip`; it answers to an API key) and the configured clients (`/api/s/{site}/rest/user`: aliases and fixed addresses), with the same API key or login cookie.
- Both are best effort: a console that can't answer either (an older Network application has no v2 API) still gives its connected clients, and the failure is logged at debug only, so the router isn't marked as failing.
- A known client is used only if it has a name (an unnamed one adds nothing a bare address doesn't). Merged by MAC: a connected client keeps its address and takes a known name only when it has none. An offline client gets its last address, else its fixed one (only while `use_fixedip` is on: UniFi keeps `fixed_ip` after a reservation is turned off), and only if no connected client holds it; between offline clients claiming one address, the most recently seen wins.
- **Bounded (added in review; the first version asked for every remembered client, with no limit).** (1) `[[router]] offline_days` (default 30, 0 for connected clients only, at most 365): the window goes to the console as `withinHours`, and every row with a `last_seen` older than it is dropped (a row with no date can't be judged and stays). (2) At most 1,024 offline clients per poll, most recently seen first (`OFFLINE_MAX`): they ride in every node's answer to `devices.names` (about 130 bytes each, against the cluster channel's 1 MiB frame) and sit in memory on every node. (3) An offline client is marked (`Lease.offline`) and is **not "seen"**: it names an address in the query log, but device identification (ADR-117) doesn't count it. Counted there, a remembered device would never age out, and with `identify.max_clients` (1,024) the places taken by devices that aren't there could push out ones that are.
- The default is not written to the replicated configuration and the mark is not written for a connected client, so a node on a build before this one (the `[[router]]` section refuses unknown fields) still reads the shared configuration and the `devices.names` answers. Roll every node before setting `offline_days` in the shared configuration.
**Consequences:**
- Offline devices keep their UniFi names in the UI and the query log. Two more read-only requests per poll (every `interval_secs`, 300 by default), for the last 30 days only: a site with thousands of remembered clients no longer makes an answer that grows with its whole history. This turns on for every existing UniFi integration at the default; `offline_days = 0` restores the old behaviour.
- A stale last address can name a device UniFi doesn't see now (a guest that left, its address since leased to something unnamed TelltaleDNS can't tell apart). Names configured in TelltaleDNS and its own DHCP leases still win.

## ADR-165 — A member with no one to hand over to may use the rest of the budget (Accepted)
**Context:** GitHub issue #35 (0.7.0-edge). `spec/04` §4 caps each attempt at min(`timeout_ms` (400 ms by default), 3 × EWMA + 50 ms, 1 s). A forwarder's EWMA reflects its cached answers: about 6.5 ms for NextDNS over DoH on a home network, so the cap is about 70 ms. A name the forwarder has to resolve itself takes much longer: a carrier's Wi-Fi Calling gateway with TTL 0 took 900–1,200 ms at Cloudflare, Quad9 and NextDNS alike. In a group of one, that single attempt timed out at ~70 ms, `Group::resolve` had nothing left to try, and the client got SERVFAIL ("no upstream answered") after ~100 ms with 1.9 s of the budget unused. On two nodes in a day: 346 SERVFAILs (0.3 % of answers, enough to fire the availability SLO's ticket alert), 735 upstream timeouts, 2 upstream SERVFAILs; every lookup of the Wi-Fi Calling name failed. Larger groups do the same when every member is cut off. `timeout_ms` can't help: it only lowers the cap, and its 400 ms default is below these answers too.
**Decision:**
- The adaptive bound and `timeout_ms` are there to give up on one member and move to the next; hedging (ADR-013) already starts the next member before that. So the attempt on the **last member in the attempt order whose breaker is closed** may use the query's whole budget (`Upstream::timeout_for(true)`), bounded by what's left of it, as recursive upstreams already may (ADR-074). Earlier members keep their timeouts, and hedge delays are unchanged.
- Members whose breaker is open or half-open (last resorts and probes) never get it. An upstream that is really down costs up to the budget per query until three timeouts in a row open its breaker, and then, as a last resort, fails within its `timeout_ms` (400 ms by default).
- No retry of a member that timed out: the one attempt waits longer, so the upstream isn't asked twice and no timeout is recorded against an upstream that was only slow (answered within the budget).
- **Correction (2026-11-21, `parallel`; PR #76, contributed by Michael Roughton):** "earlier members keep their timeouts" assumed every earlier member has a later one to hand over to. `parallel` starts its first `parallel_fanout` members at once, so none of them does. The members sort by EWMA and only the last member with a closed breaker was patient, so the faster racing members were cut off at 3 × EWMA + 50 ms: the fastest one in a group of two, and every racing member when a slower member follows them. With Cloudflare (EWMA about 10 ms over DoH, so about 80 ms) and Google racing, every name Cloudflare had to resolve itself (typically 100–200 ms) was abandoned while Google, the last member, was waited for: about 7 % of Cloudflare's attempts counted as timeouts (none against Google, and none when the same names were sent to Cloudflare directly), its breaker opened now and then, and cold lookups took Google's time instead of the faster answer. So with `parallel`, every member with a closed breaker among the first `parallel_fanout` is patient too; members after them, probes and last resorts are not, and the other strategies are unchanged. Hedge delays are unchanged, but a racing member that is no longer cut off no longer starts the next member early: with three or more members, the first one after the race starts at the last racing member's hedge delay, which can be later than the old cut-off (by up to 3 × the difference between the last and the first racing member's EWMA, less 40 ms). Tested by `ups_005_parallel_racing_members_are_patient`, `ups_006_probe_and_last_resort_never_patient`, `ups_006_one_at_a_time_strategies_patient_only_last_closed` and `ups_005_parallel_members_each_wait_for_a_slow_answer`.
- **Correction (2026-11-23, the end of a query):** a patient attempt's timeout ends at the query's deadline. A query that ended there with only a SERVFAIL or REFUSED in hand, or with nothing, returned and dropped its attempts, which aborted those still running before they recorded an outcome: a dead sole upstream cost the full budget on every query and its breaker never opened (only the active checks could bench it), and the same held for a silent member behind a SERVFAIL and for every member of a `parallel` group in a total outage. Attempts still running when `resolve` returns now finish in the background, as the losers of an answered query already did, and record their outcome. The client's answer is not delayed: the query still returns at its deadline. Each attempt ends at its own timeout, which was set from what was left of the budget when it started, so at most one attempt per member is outstanding for each `resolve` started in about the last 2 s (one client cache miss can start several: DNSSEC validation looks up keys and proofs through the same group); a retry over TCP after a truncated answer (up to `timeout_ms` of its own) and DNSCrypt's rounds can run past that. One consequence: a name the upstream takes longer than the budget to answer (over 2 s) now counts as a timeout against the patient member, where the aborted attempt recorded nothing. Three of those in a row, with no answer from it in between (a NOERROR, NXDOMAIN, SERVFAIL or REFUSED resets the count), bench even a healthy sole upstream for 10 s (doubling, up to 5 minutes, while its probes fail). While benched it is a last resort with its `timeout_ms`, so cold names slower than that (400 ms by default) get SERVFAIL until a probe succeeds. That is bounded by the breaker's backoff, and narrower than 0.7.0, which cut every attempt at min(`timeout_ms`, 3 × EWMA + 50 ms, 1 s), so that names slower than about 70–400 ms counted the same way. Tested by `ups_006_silent_member_behind_a_servfail_is_benched`, `ups_006_dead_sole_upstream_is_benched`, `ups_006_query_ends_at_its_deadline_while_its_attempt_finishes` and `ups_006_burst_of_misses_leaves_no_attempt_behind`.
- **What the `parallel` correction costs:** a racing member that goes silent. Its attempts now run for the budget (2 s) instead of about 3 × EWMA + 50 ms, in the background while the other member answers, so its three timeouts, and its open breaker, come about 2 s after it goes quiet instead of almost at once, and until then every cache miss leaves a 2 s attempt to it running. After that it is a last resort with its `timeout_ms` (400 ms by default; the recorded timeouts lift its EWMA above that): started with the admitted members when fewer than `parallel_fanout` are admitted (as in a group of two), else after them, once the hedge delay has passed or one of them has failed. In a local test at 100 cache misses a second, the breaker opened after 2.0 s instead of 79 ms, with up to 201 attempts to the silent member outstanding instead of 44; clients still got the other member's answers in a few milliseconds. The recorded 2 s timeouts also raise its EWMA, so after it recovers it sorts behind the other member for longer. Clients don't wait while the other member answers NOERROR or NXDOMAIN; if it answers SERVFAIL or REFUSED quickly, that answer is kept as the fallback and the client waits for the silent member: up to the budget (serve-stale still answers at 1.8 s) until it is benched, then up to its `timeout_ms`. In the local test with the other member answering SERVFAIL in 5 ms, queries sent one after another: 2.0 s for each of the first three, then 0.40 s. Queries that start before the first three timeouts land (about 2 s after the member went quiet) each wait the full 2 s. Before, only the first such query got the SERVFAIL after the silent member's short timeout: in a group of two, the timeout recorded then sorted the silent member last, which made it the patient one, so the queries after it waited for it too. This is the trade this ADR made for the last member, now made for each racing one.
**Consequences:** a slow name is answered in the time it takes, up to the budget, for single-upstream setups too. A blackholed sole upstream makes clients wait up to 2 s for SERVFAIL, not 70–400 ms, until three timeouts in a row open its breaker (the first three queries when they come one after another; every query started in the 2 s before those timeouts land waits the full 2 s), and then up to its `timeout_ms` for as long as it doesn't answer, since last resorts and probes are never patient (measured, queries one after another: 2.0 s for each of the first three, then 0.40 s). Serve-stale (1.8 s) is unchanged. The patient attempt is at most the 2 s budget, under the 3 s after which a silent pooled connection is closed (ADR-148). Tested by `ups_006_single_member_waits_for_a_slow_answer` and `ups_006_last_healthy_member_waits_for_a_slow_answer`.

## ADR-164 — Debug files for every build: link once with line tables, publish the split-off symbols beside the stripped binary (Accepted)
**Context:** GitHub issue #7. The release binary is stripped (`strip = true`), so a stack taken from a hung node (a `gdb` or `eu-stack` capture, or the watchdog's dump, ADR-163) is a list of addresses nobody can read. The owner promised the reporter "debug symbols with 0.7.0" (2026-11-14). Two things limit the options: the image must stay under 15 MiB compressed per architecture (NFR-001), and the binaries are built reproducibly with `cargo auditable` (NFR-005), whose dependency data (`.dep-v0`) must survive.
**Decision (proposed):**
- **One link, then a split.** The Dockerfile's build stage links with `strip = none` and `debug = "line-tables-only"` (the profile's environment overrides, in that build only), then uses `llvm-objcopy` from the pinned toolchain's `llvm-tools` component: `telltale.debug` gets the symbol table and the line tables (`--only-keep-debug`); the shipped binary is the same link output with the symbol table and debug sections removed, `.dep-v0` kept, and a `.gnu_debuglink` to the debug file's name and CRC. A second build with symbols would have a different layout, so its addresses wouldn't match the shipped binary; `-C split-debuginfo` keeps no symbol table and isn't supported for fat-LTO static musl builds through zig.
- **No build ID.** The zig linker writes none, so the pairing is the release it came from (same version, same architecture) and the debuglink's CRC, which `gdb` checks.
- **Published as** `telltale-<arch>-linux.debug.zst` (zstd level 10) for the three architectures, in every release and every edge build, under the signed `SHA256SUMS` like the binaries and their SBOMs (`verify.sh` checks that the lines exist). `releases.json` doesn't list them: a node never fetches one, and `self-update` takes only its own binary by name.
- **What it costs, measured on the build VM (2026-11-16, the real Docker build for amd64):**
  - The shipped binary is 34,754,048 bytes against 34,748,816 (5,232 more, 0.015%): `.text` is 4,864 bytes larger and everything after it shifts, because turning debug info on changes the linked layout a little (it also adds a one-byte `.debug_gdb_scripts` reference). It is **not byte-identical code**, so it was benchmarked rather than assumed: `bench-smoke` alternating baseline and split binary, ten runs each: cache-hot throughput median 530,993 against 526,753 qps (ranges 482k to 586k and 449k to 557k), cache-hot p50 45.5 against 33.0 µs, miss-heavy p50 217 against 223 µs. No difference beyond the run-to-run spread.
  - The debug file is 141 MB raw, 25.5 MB at zstd 10 (21.4 MB at 19). Three of them add about 75 MB to each edge publish.
  - The stripped binary no longer carries the `.comment` section (the compiler's identification string).
- **Reproducibility.** The nightly two-clean-builds check still compares the binary byte for byte; it reports as a warning if the two debug files differ.
- **Reading a stack:** `zstd -d telltale-<arch>-linux.debug.zst -o telltale.debug`, then `addr2line -e telltale.debug -f -C -i <address>` (docs/running.md). With line tables, an inlined call shows the line of its own source.
**Alternatives rejected:** shipping the unstripped binary (141 MB more in every image); full debug info (several times larger, and the line tables answer what a stack needs); a debuginfod server (a service to run); symbols only, no line tables (byte-identical code, and function names alone lose where inside a large function the thread waits).
**Consequences:** the Docker build adds a few seconds; the image workflow uploads 75 MB more; the release page gets three more files; a stack from any build can be read after the fact if its release's debug file is kept (edge builds are replaced on every push, so a dump from an edge build is read with that build's file while it is still published).
**Accepted 2026-11-16:** the owner answered "Push and accept" after seeing the numbers above, including that the code is not byte-identical and that the images were already within 0.34 MiB of the 15 MiB gate (this adds about 0.04 MiB).
**Verification (planned):** the A/B above; the split binary keeps `.dep-v0` and gains the debuglink; `addr2line` on the debug file resolves a frame the watchdog's e2e produces (ADR-163's `deploy/watchdog/e2e.sh` on a debug build); the edge image run publishes the three files and the size gate and SBOM checks still pass.

## ADR-163 — A watchdog restarts a node that stops answering and leaves evidence of why: a thread outside the runtime, two checks, a bounded dump (Accepted)
**Context:** GitHub issue #7.
